Atomic decompositions for derived categories of G G -surfaces
Alexey Elagin, Julia Schneider, Evgeny Shinder

Abstract
. We construct canonical semi-orthogonal decompositions for derived categories of smooth projective surfaces. These decompositions are compatible with the operations in the minimal model program, such as blow-ups and conic bundles. Therefore our construction confirms a conjecture of Kontsevich in dimension two. We work in the G G -equivariant setting and over an arbitrary perfect field, and canonical decompositions are consistent with group change and algebraic field extensions. Our method is based on the G G -minimal model program for surfaces and on the Sarkisov link factorisation of birational maps between Mori fibre spaces. We characterise rationality of surfaces, and in certain cases, birationality between surfaces in terms of the pieces of these decompositions, which we call atoms.



1.
Introduction

One of the most important questions
about derived categories of coherent sheaves in algebraic geometry is: given a birational map

\phi\colon X\dashrightarrow Y
between smooth projective varieties, how

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
are related?
This question has been considered in the pioneering work on derived categories by Bondal and Orlov for flips and flops

[
16
, p. 40]
,
[
17
]
. A
conjecture of Kawamata
[
46
]
predicts that if
\phi
is a K-equivalence, then
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
are equivalent. This is known to be the case for
3
-folds by a result of Bridgeland

[
19
]
.

A key observation is that if

f\colon Y\to X
is a
derived contraction
, that is, a morphism with
{\mathrm{R}}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
(e.g. a smooth blow-up), then the derived pullback functor
f^{*}\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)

is fully faithful and
we have a semi-orthogonal decomposition

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)=\langle\mathcal{K},f^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\rangle,\text{ where }\mathcal{K}=\ker(f_{*}):=\{F\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)\colon f_{*}(F)=0\}.

The kernel category
\mathcal{K}
decomposes further in special cases such as smooth blow-ups and projective bundles
[
67
]
, and some Fano fibrations
[
54
]
,
[
57
]
,
[
79
]
. The most prominent case is Orlov’s blow-up formula, see
[
67
]
or Proposition
3.9
: if
{\tilde{X}}
is the blow-up of
X
at a smooth centre
Z
of codimension
r
then

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\tilde{X}})=\langle\underbrace{{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z),\ldots,{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)}_{\text{$(r-1)$ times}},{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\rangle.

Thus derived categories of varieties which are birational, or more generally related by operations of the Minimal Model Program (MMP), are expected to have common pieces in their semi-orthogonal decompositions.
On the other hand, it is known that semi-orthogonal decompositions into minimal pieces are in general not unique, already in dimension two.

Conjecture 1.1

(Kontsevich)
.

There should exist canonical semi-orthogonal decompositions, well-defined up to mutations, which are
compatible with geometric operations.

This conjecture has been made more precise in the Noncommutative Minimal Model Program by
Halpern-Leistner
[
36
]
relating semi-orthogonal decompositions to paths in the stability manifold.

Existence of such canonical decompositions
would imply that the pieces of the decomposition for
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
can be an obstruction to rationality of
X
, because
only pieces coming from varieties of dimension at most
\dim(X)-2
will appear if
X
is rational.
A particular case is the celebrated cubic fourfold conjecture by Kuznetsov
[
56
]
that says that
a smooth complex cubic fourfold
X\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
is rational if and only if the main component
{\mathbf{A}}_{X}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is equivalent to the derived category of a K3 surface.
In the recent work
[
45
]
, Katzarkov, Kontsevich, Pantev, and Yu proved irrationality of a very general complex cubic fourfold
X
using their groundbreaking theory of Hodge atoms which is based on decomposing quantum cohomology into canonical pieces. As one of the steps in the proof, they show that the Hodge atom analog of the component
{\mathbf{A}}_{X}
of a cubic fourfold
X
is indeed not coming from surfaces.

In this paper we construct canonical decompositions for varieties up to dimension
2
, over a perfect field and in the equivariant setting.
Using these decompositions we obtain some new results on rationality and, more generally, birationality of surfaces.

1.1.
Atomic decompositions for
G
-surfaces

We will now formalize what it means for semi-orthogonal decompositions to be compatible with geometric operations.
Motivated by the theory of Hodge atoms
[
45
,
21
]
,
we make the following definition. For a fixed group
G
, let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a category consisting of some class of
G
-varieties (Definition
2.3
) as objects and some class of
G
-equivariant derived contractions as morphisms.

Definition 1.2

(see Definition
4.3
)
.

A
G
-atomic theory for
{\mathcal{V}}

assigns for
all
X\in\mathrm{Obj}({\mathcal{V}})

a mutation-equivalence class of
G
-invariant
semi-orthogonal decompositions, called
atomic decompositions
, with the following property.
For any morphism
f\colon Y\to X
in
{\mathcal{V}}
, there is
an atomic decomposition

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle
and an atomic decomposition for
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
that is obtained from

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)=\langle\ker(f_{*}),f^{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\dots,f^{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle

by possibly splitting
\ker(f_{*})
into several components.

From now on until the end of this subsection, we work over an algebraically closed field
{\Bbbk}
in the equivariant setting: that is, we fix a group
G
and consider
G
-varieties and
G
-equivariant morphisms. In particular, we allow the
geometric case
of a finite group
G
acting
{\Bbbk}
-linearly, and the
arithmetic case
where
G
is a Galois group.

Our first main result is the construction of
a
G
-atomic theory
for the category

G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}

consisting of
G
-varieties of
\dim\leq 2
and rational derived contractions. By a rational derived contraction in
\dim\leq 2
we mean a derived contraction with geometrically rational generic fibre, or equivalently, a composition of
G
-MMP (Minimal Model Program) steps, that is,
G
-blow-ups of surfaces and
G
-Mori fibre space structure maps.

Theorem 1.3

(see Theorem
4.14
)
.

For every group
G
there is an explicit
atomic theory for

G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}
.
Furthermore, the constructed theories are compatible with passing to subgroups
H\subset G
.

By compatibility for a subgroup
H\subset G
we mean that
G
-atomic decompositions can be refined to
H
-atomic decompositions.
Theorem
1.3
proves Conjecture
1.1
in dimension 2.
In the case when
G
is a Galois group
it
strengthens earlier results by Auel–Bernardara
[
1
]
and Bernardara–Durighetto

[
9
]

who introduced the Kuznetsov component for geometrically rational surfaces in the arithmetic case and proved that it is a birational invariant.

Example 1.4
.

In the simplest case
{\Bbbk}=\overline{{\Bbbk}}
and
G=\{e\}
, for rational surfaces our result says the following. There are two types of minimal rational surfaces:
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and Hirzebruch surfaces
\mathbb{F}_{n}
,
n\geq 0
,
n\neq 1
, with atomic decompositions

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbb{P}}^{2})=\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-2)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-1)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\rangle,\quad{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{F}_{n})=\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-s-h)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-s)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\rangle.

Here
h
is the fibre class and
s
is the negative section class. Now if
X
is any rational surface with contractions
X\to X_{1},X\to X_{2}
to minimal rational surfaces then the two exceptional collections on
X
induced from
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
by the blow-up formula both define atomic decompositions, in particular, they are mutation equivalent.

The proof of Theorem
1.3
relies on the Minimal Model Program for
G
-surfaces, using Sarkisov links to check compatibility as in the arithmetic case for components of derived categories in
[
1
]
,
[
9
]

and the Grothendieck ring of varieties in
[
60
]
.
There are several steps in the proof.
The almost trivial step is to construct the (unique) atomic theory for curves.
Then we construct a decomposition of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
for every two-dimensional
G
-Mori fibre space
X\to B
, based on the Karpov–Nogin
3
-block exceptional collections for del Pezzo surfaces
[
43
]
. We check that these decompositions are
compatible with Sarkisov links between Mori fibre spaces
: two atomic decompositions for two Mori fibre spaces forming the sides of a Sarkisov link induce mutation-equivalent decompositions on the roof. This requires case-by-case checking all possible types of links and constitutes the longest part of the proof.

This allows for a unique construction of an atomic theory for
G
-surfaces matching the decompositions for
G
-Mori fibre spaces: for arbitrary
G
-surface
Y
we choose a sequence of blow-ups of
G
-orbits producing
Y
from a Mori fibre space
X/B
or a surface
X
with
K_{X}
nef, and augment atomic decomposition for
X
be adding an atom
\ker f_{i*}
for any blow-up
f_{i}
in the sequence as in the blow-up formula.

One important aspect of our atomic theory is that we do not decompose K-nef surfaces such as Enriques surfaces, i.e. by construction they are assigned a semi-orthogonal decomposition with a single piece. This is motivated by both the MMP, where such surfaces are the end steps, as well as by the quantum cohomology viewpoint, where such surfaces are assigned a single Hodge atom
[
45
]
.

We refer to pieces of atomic semi-orthogonal decompositions
of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
as
G
-
atoms
; these pieces, as triangulated categories with a
G
-action, are independent of the choice of an atomic decomposition because mutations simply permute the pieces of a decomposition.

Of particular importance are
G
-atoms of permutation type
: these are
G
-atoms generated by an orthogonal exceptional collection
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
which is transitively permuted by
G
.

Proposition 1.5

(see Proposition
3.41
)
.

A
G
-atom of permutation type, up to
G
-equivariant equivalence, is determined by a transitive representation
\rho\colon G\to{\mathrm{S}}_{n}
and a cohomology class
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n})

where the
G
-action on
({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n}
is defined by the permutation
\rho
and the action on scalars is fixed.

If
f\colon Y\to X

is a blow up of a
G
-orbit
Z
, then the
G
-atoms of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)

are those of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
together with a new
G
-atom of permutation type corresponding to
Z
with a trivial twisting class
[\alpha]
.
Therefore the set of
G
-atoms of permutation of type with nontrivial twisting class is a
G
-birational invariant of
X
. This birational invariant is reminiscent to the Amitsur invariant
[
59
,
11
]

and gives similar obstructions to
G
-birationality.

Remark 1.6
.

Conjecturally, the atomic decompositions we construct
correspond to Hodge atoms constructed by Katzarkov–Kontsevich–Pantev–Yu
[
45
]
and further used as an obstruction to rationality in
[
21
]
.
We thank Jenya Tevelev for explaining to us the following observation in this direction.
The number of atoms for
G
-minimal del Pezzo surfaces – two atoms for del Pezzo surfaces of degree
4
and below, and three atoms for del Pezzo surfaces of degree
5
and above – matches the number of distinct eigenvalues of the quantum multiplication by the canonical class at the
G
-invariant point
[
5
, §3]
, which by definition corresponds to the Hodge atoms in
[
45
]
.

Remark 1.7
.

The exact connection between the atomic decompositions we construct and the Noncommutative Minimal Model Program by Halpern-Leistner
[
36
]
, further developed in
[
35
]
and
partially realized for surfaces
[
35
,
44
]
deserves a separate investigation. It is quite likely that the two theories coincide.

Our first application of Theorem
1.3

is to the motivic invariant
[
61
,
60
]
of birational maps between surfaces, which was extended to the equivariant case by
[
49
]
.
In terms of atomic decompositions, the invariant of a birational map between
G
-surfaces
X
and
Y
can be understood as the formal difference
between the multisets of equivalence classes of
G
-atoms in
X
and
Y
.
Thus the motivic invariant for equivariant self-maps of a
G
-surface is always trivial, see Theorem
5.6
.
This extends the result for surfaces in the arithmetic case
[
60
]
,
and contrasts with the case of
G
-threefolds where such invariants are in general nonzero
[
61
, §3.3]
,

[
49
, §7]
,
[
62
, Corollary 4.6]

1.2.
Applications in the arithmetic case

We now explain applications of our atomic theory to birational geometry of
G
-surfaces.
We assume that we are in the arithmetic case, that is,
{\Bbbk}
is a perfect field and

G=\Gal(\overline{{\Bbbk}}/{\Bbbk})
acts on the scalar extension of varieties from
{\Bbbk}
to
\overline{{\Bbbk}}
.
Using Galois descent for semi-orthogonal decompositions we deduce from Theorem

1.3
:

Theorem 1.8

(see Theorem
4.15
)
.

For any perfect field
{\Bbbk}

there is an explicit
atomic theory for

\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}
.
Furthermore the constructed theories are compatible with passing to algebraic field extensions
{\Bbbk}\subset{\mathbb{L}}
.

Theorem
4.15
is a more general version, allowing a
{\Bbbk}
-linear action of a group on our varieties,
and the theories will be compatible with taking subgroups just like in Theorem
1.3
.
To prove Theorem we rely on a general results from the theory of descent of semi-orthogonal decompositions
[
29
]
,
[
30
]
,
and in particular
the Galois descent as developed in
[
1
]
,
[
3
]
.
We use a bijection between
G
-invariant thick subcategories in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}})
and thick subcategories in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
which preserves semi-orthogonal
decompositions (Lemma
3.43
, Proposition
3.45
).
This allows to descend
a
G
-atomic theory over
\overline{{\Bbbk}}
to an atomic theory over
{\Bbbk}
.

We aim for a birational classification of geometrically rational surfaces
in terms of their atoms.
Our first result in this direction is:

Proposition 1.9

(see Theorem
5.22
)
.

A surface
X

is rational if and only if
all
atoms of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
are of the form

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L)

for finite field extensions
L/{\Bbbk}
.

Proposition
1.9
refines the main results of
[
1
,
9
]
who proved that rational surfaces have semi-orthogonal decompositions into pieces of the form
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L)
, and constructed a nonvanishing Kuznetsov component for nonrational geometrically rational surfaces.

In our approach the ’only if’ implication of Proposition
1.9
holds by construction of the atomic theory because a blow up of a closed point with residue field
L
adds an atom of the form
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L)
. For the ’if’ implication we have to prove that
X
has a toric model and index
\ind(X)=1
(this uses the formula in Proposition
1.13
below) which then implies that
X
is rational.
In the geometric case the
’only if’
implication still holds, however the ’if’ implication fails, see Example
5.9
. We conjecture that in the geometric case,
atoms of a
G
-surface
control stable linearisability,
see Conjecture
5.10
.

For the next application we need the following definition. We say that
X
is
birationally rich
if it is a geometrically rational surface which is
birational either to a del Pezzo surface of degree at least
5
, or to a conic bundle of degree at least
5
. Informally speaking, these are surfaces with the most number of birational maps, and ’birationally rich’ serves as a moral antonym to ’birationally rigid’, even though the two are not mutually exclusive. Every rational surface is by definition birationally rich, and for algebraically closed fields the two notions coincide.

Proposition 1.10

(Proposition
5.12
)
.

A surface
X
is birationally rich if and only if all its atoms are of the form
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L,{\mathbf{a}})
for some finite field extensions
L/{\Bbbk}
and Brauer classes
{\mathbf{a}}\in\Br(L)
.

Here
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L,{\mathbf{a}})
is the derived category of
{\mathbf{a}}
-twisted
L
-vector spaces; these atoms are obtained by Galois descent of
G
-atoms of permutation type, and the class
{\mathbf{a}}
corresponds to the twisting class
[\alpha]
from Proposition
1.5
.
We call
atoms of the form

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L,{\mathbf{a}})

small atoms
,
and atoms of the form

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L)
(that is small atoms with a trivial Brauer class)
trivial atoms
. In these terms Proposition
1.9
says that rationality of a surface is equivalent to triviality of its atoms.

Example 1.11

(
[
10
]
,
[
1
]
)
.

Let
A
be a central simple algebra of degree
3
(that is dimension
3^{2}=9
) over
{\Bbbk}
. Let
{\mathbf{a}}\in\Br({\Bbbk})
be its
3
-torsion Brauer class.
The Severi–Brauer surface
X=\SB(A)
which is the corresponding twisted form of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
is birationally rich and has an atomic semi-orthogonal decomposition

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\Bbbk},2{\mathbf{a}}),{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\Bbbk},{\mathbf{a}}),{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\Bbbk})\rangle

obtained by Galois descent from the atomic decomposition for
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbb{P}}^{2})
in Example
1.4
.
All atoms of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
are small, but not all of them are trivial unless

A
is a split matrix algebra
{\mathrm{M}}_{3}({\Bbbk})
.

Let us consider the opposite Severi–Brauer surface
X^{\mathrm{op}}

which corresponds to the opposite algebra
A^{\mathrm{op}}

and has Brauer class
-{\mathbf{a}}=2{\mathbf{a}}\in\Br({\Bbbk})
.
Then the standard Cremona involution is easily seen to descend to a birational isomorphism between
X
and
X^{\mathrm{op}}
, and both categories
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{\mathrm{op}})
consist of the same atoms.

Theorem 1.12

(see Theorem
5.28
)
.

Let
X
and
X^{\prime}
be birationally rich surfaces. Then
X
and
X^{\prime}
are birational if and only if they have the same nontrivial atoms.

This result unifies some known statements about birationality of del Pezzo surfaces, considered in
[
22
]
,

[
1
]
,

[
59
]
,
[
74
]
,
[
76
]
,
[
52
]
.
As opposed to the proof of Proposition
1.9
which is uniform, our proof of Theorem
1.12

relies on some case-by-case analysis, and we need to use the biregular classification of rank one toric surfaces from
[
22
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
76
]
.

The result analogous to Theorem
1.12
cannot hold without any assumptions on the surfaces, because K-nef surfaces can be derived equivalent without being birational.
We conjecture that the result still holds for all geometrically rational surfaces.

We explain one more connection between atomic decompositions and arithmetic of surfaces.
Recall that the Amitsur group
\mathrm{Am}(X)

[
22
]
,
[
59
]
and the index
\ind(X)
are basic birational invariants of
X
. They are related to atomic decompositions as follows:

Proposition 1.13

(see Proposition
5.21
)
.

Let
X
be a birationally rich surface with atomic semi-orthogonal decomposition

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{1},{\mathbf{a}}_{1}),\ldots,{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{m},{\mathbf{a}}_{m})\rangle.

Then
|\mathrm{Am}(X)|\cdot\ind(X)=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\ind({\mathbf{a}}_{i})
, where
\ind({\mathbf{a}}_{i})
is the index of the corresponding Brauer class.

For del Pezzo surfaces this result can be deduced from the classification given in
[
1
]
. We provide a uniform proof using what we call the
derived Amitsur group
which is an invariant of derived categories of surfaces, additive for semi-orthogonal decompositions.

We have another application for a class of surfaces which are not birationally rich.

Theorem 1.14

(see Theorem
5.5
for a stronger statement)
.

If
X
and
X^{\prime}
are minimal del Pezzo surfaces of degree
4

which are birational, then they are isomorphic.

This result has been proven independently by Shramov and Trepalin
[
73
]

by an intricate direct analysis of conic bundles that appear in the link factorisation of a birational map.
Our approach is instead
first to show that
X
and
X^{\prime}
have the same atoms, and then use a Torelli theorem for degree
4
del Pezzo surfaces by the first author
[
32
]

to deduce that they are isomorphic.
We have an analog of Theorem
1.14
for any group action.

Finally, let us explain the main difficulties for applying our strategy for constructing atomic semi-orthogonal decompositions in higher dimension. Firstly, varieties appearing in higher-dimensional MMP will be singular, and while it is conjectured that derived categories of singular varieties admit unique minimal resolutions
[
17
, Conjecture 5.1]
,
[
55
, Conjecture 4.10]
, this is currently far out of reach. Furthermore, while it is a natural idea to relate derived categories of Mori fibre spaces using Sarkisov links, this seems to be a lot more difficult in higher dimension due to the lack of a complete classification of Sarkisov links that is available for surfaces due to work of Iskovskikh
[
39
]
.

2.
Preliminaries

Let
{\Bbbk}
be a field. By a
variety over
{\Bbbk}
we mean a smooth projective geometrically integral scheme over
{\Bbbk}
. We denote the category of varieties over
{\Bbbk}
by
\cVar_{{\Bbbk}}
, and
\cVar_{{\Bbbk},n}
(resp.
\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq n}
) denotes the full subcategory of varieties of dimension
n
(resp.
\leq n
).
By a
surface
we mean a variety of dimension
2
.

2.1.
Group actions and
G
-surfaces

Recall that a
group action
of a group
G
on a scheme
X
is a group homomorphism
\gamma\colon G\to\Aut(X)
, where
\Aut(X)
is the group of scheme automorphisms of
X
.

Remark 2.1
.

If
X
is a variety over a field
{\Bbbk}
(in particular, projective and geometrically integral by assumption), then

{\mathrm{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\cong{\Bbbk}
and so any group action
\gamma\colon G\to\Aut(X)
induces a group action
\check{\gamma}\colon G\to\Aut({\Bbbk})
, that is, for every
g\in G
there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec{\Bbbk}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec{\Bbbk},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\gamma}_{g}}

where
\pi\colon X\to\Spec{\Bbbk}
denotes the structure morphism.

For applications, we will mostly have the following types of group actions on varieties in mind:

(1)

By a
geometric action
we mean an injective group homomorphism
\gamma\colon G\to\Aut_{\Bbbk}(X)
, where
G
is a finite group,
{\Bbbk}
is an algebraically closed field, and
\Aut_{\Bbbk}(X)
denotes the automorphisms of
X
over
{\Bbbk}
.

(2)

By an
arithmetic action
we mean the following: let
X
be a variety over a perfect field
{\Bbbk}
. Then the absolute Galois group
G=\Gal(\bar{\Bbbk}/{\Bbbk})
acts on
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}=X\otimes_{\Bbbk}\bar{\Bbbk}
via trivial action on
X
and with the usual action of the Galois group on
\bar{\Bbbk}
. This gives an action
\gamma\colon\Gal(\bar{\Bbbk}/{\Bbbk})\to\Aut_{\Bbbk}(X_{\bar{\Bbbk}})\subset\Aut(X_{\bar{\Bbbk}})
.

(3)

A combination of the two above actions: let
X
be a variety over a perfect field
{\Bbbk}
, let
\Gal(\bar{\Bbbk}/{\Bbbk})
act tautologically on
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
, and let
G
be a finite group injecting to
\Aut_{\Bbbk}(X)\subset\Aut_{\Bbbk}(X_{\bar{\Bbbk}})
. Since these two actions on
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
commute, we get an action
\gamma\colon G\times\Gal(\bar{\Bbbk}/{\Bbbk})\to\Aut_{\Bbbk}(X_{\bar{\Bbbk}})
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two varieties over
{\Bbbk}
with a
G
-action, and let
f\colon X\to Y
be a morphism over
{\Bbbk}
. We say
f
is a
G
-morphism
or
f
is
G
-equivariant
if for every
g\in G
the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{g}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{g}^{Y}}

A
G
-action on a variety
X
induces a
G
-action on
\Pic(X)
, the set of isomorphism classes of line bundles on
X
, and on
N_{1}(X)
, the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space of
1
-cycles on
X
modulo numerical equivalence. We denote by
\Pic(X)^{G}\subset\Pic(X)
the subgroup of (isomorphism classes of)
G
-invariant line bundles, and by
N_{1}(X)^{G}\subset N_{1}(X)
the
G
-invariant subspace. The
G
-Picard rank
of
X
, denoted by
\rho^{G}(X)
, is the dimension of the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
N_{1}(X)^{G}
.

For the notion of a
G
-surface we will make stronger assumptions on the group action so that the
G
-equivariant Sarkisov Program works (see e.g.
[
58
, Section III, 16.1]
):

Definition 2.3
.

Let
X
be a variety over
{\Bbbk}
, and let
G
be a group acting on
X
. We say that
X
, together with the group action, is a
G
-variety
if the induced action on
N_{1}(X)
has finite orbits. In particular, a
G
-surface
is a
G
-variety of dimension
2
.

We remark that we will use the Definition
2.3
only for varieties of dimension
\leq 2
, and the finiteness condition is empty for varieties of dimension
\leq 1
. Also we remark that the three types of actions mentioned above satisfy Definition
2.3
.

2.2.
Birational geometry of
G
-surfaces

In this subsection, we assume
{\Bbbk}
to be algebraically closed.

Let
Y
be a
G
-surface,
Z\subset Y
be a finite
G
-invariant set and

f\colon X\to Y
be the blow-up of
Z
. Then
G
acts naturally on
X
and the map
f
is
G
-equivariant. The induced
G
-action on
N_{1}(X)
has finite orbits, hence
X
is a
G
-surface as in Definition
2.3
.

The other way, let
X
be a
G
-surface and
f\colon X\to Y
a birational morphism onto a surface
Y
contracting disjoint
(-1)
-curves. If the set of contracted
(-1)
-curves is
G
-invariant then
f
induces a
G
-surface structure on
Y
such that
f\colon X\to Y
is a
G
-morphism. Indeed, the
G
-action on
Y
is given by
g\in G\mapsto f\circ\gamma_{g}\circ f^{-1}\in\Aut(Y)
, which is indeed in
\Aut(Y)
since the contracted curves form
G
-orbits.
Moreover, the induced
G
-action on
N_{1}(Y)
has finite orbits, hence
Y
is a
G
-surface.

A
G
-surface
X
is
G
-minimal
if every birational
G
-morphism
X\to Y
to any smooth
G
-surface
Y
is an isomorphism.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
X
be a
G
-surface and
B
a smooth
G
-variety of
\dim(B)\leq 1
. Let
\pi\colon X\to B
be a contraction (that is, a surjective morphism with
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}={\mathcal{O}}_{B}
) that is
G
-equivariant.

(1)

For
r\geq 1
, the morphism
\pi
is a
G
-rank
r
fibration
if
-K_{X}
is
\pi
-ample and
\rho^{G}(X/B)=r
.

(2)

A
G
-Mori fibre space

X/B
is a
G
-rank
1
fibration.

(3)

If
B
is a curve and
-K_{X}
is
\pi
-ample, then
\pi
is a
G
-conic bundle
.

(4)

A
G
-conic bundle that is a
G
-Mori fibre space is also called a
G
-Mori conic bundle.

Remark 2.5
.

If
X/\Spec{\Bbbk}
is a
G
-rank
r
fibration, then
X
is a del Pezzo surface with
\rho^{G}(X)=r
.

Recall that we assume a
G
-surface
X
to have finite orbits for the induced action on
N_{1}(X)
.

Theorem 2.6

(
G
-equivariant MMP for surfaces, see
[
25
]
)
.

Let
Y
be a
G
-surface. Then there is a
G
-surface
X
and a birational
G
-morphism
Y\to X
such that either
K_{X}
is nef, or
X
admits the structure of a
G
-Mori fibre space
X/B
. For a
G
-Mori fibre space
X/B
, if
B=\Spec{\Bbbk}
is a point then
X
is a
G
-minimal del Pezzo surface, and if
B
is a curve then
X/B
is a
G
-Mori conic bundle.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
X_{1}/B_{1}
and
X_{2}/B_{2}
be two
G
-Mori fibre spaces. An isomorphism
\varphi\colon X_{1}\to X_{2}
is an
isomorphism of
G
-Mori fibre spaces from
X_{1}/B_{1}
to
X_{2}/B_{2}
if there is a
G
-equivariant diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Definition 2.8

(Sarkisov links)
.

Let
X_{1}/B_{1}
and
X_{2}/B_{2}
be two
G
-Mori fibre spaces. A
G
-equivariant birational map
\chi\colon X_{1}\dashrightarrow X_{2}
is a
G
-Sarkisov link from
X_{1}/B_{1}
to
X_{2}/B_{2}
if there is a
G
-equivariant commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{2}}

where
Z/B
is a
G
-rank
2
fibration, and
\chi
is not an isomorphism of
G
-Mori fibre spaces.

Remark 2.9
.

For any
G
-rank
2
fibration
Z/B
there exists a Sarkisov link with two sides determined by two extremal contractions of
Z

[
58
, Lemma 16.18]
. This applies in particular to a
G
-rank
2
del Pezzo surface over
B=\Spec({\Bbbk})
.

The following is the classification of Sarkisov links in terms of
K_{X_{1}}^{2},K_{Z}^{2},K_{X_{2}}^{2}
, obtained in
[
39
, Theorem 2.6]
in the arithmetic case, and the same computations work for more general group actions
[
25
, §7]
. Since
\rho^{G}(Z/B)=2
and
\rho^{G}(X_{i}/B_{i})=1
, exactly one of the maps
\sigma_{1},\beta_{1}
is an isomorphism, and similarly for
\sigma_{2},\beta_{2}
. Therefore links divide into four types depending on which of the four combination realises.

Proposition 2.10

(Numerical classification of Sarkisov links)
.

Let
X_{1}/B_{1}
and
X_{2}/B_{2}
be two
G
-Mori fibre spaces and
\chi\colon X_{1}\dashrightarrow X_{2}
be a
G
-Sarkisov link over a base
B
, with associated
G
-rank
2
fibration
Z/B
as in Definition
2.8
.
Then one of the following holds.

If
\chi
is a
link of type I
, that is,
\beta_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are isomorphisms, then
B_{1}=B=\Spec{\Bbbk}
,
B_{2}={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, and

(K_{X_{1}}^{2},K_{X_{2}}^{2})\in\{(9,8),(9,5),(8,6),(4,3)\}.

If
\chi
is a
link of type II
, that is,
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
are isomorphisms, then either

(1)

B
is a curve and
K_{X_{1}}^{2}=K_{X_{2}}^{2}
, or

(2)

B=\Spec{\Bbbk}
and
(K_{X_{1}}^{2},K_{Z}^{2},K_{X_{2}}^{2})
is either symmetric (that is,
K_{X_{1}}^{2}=K_{X_{2}}^{2}
) and in one of the cases

(a)

(d,1,d)
for
d\in\{9,8,6,5,4,3,2\}
(Bertini type)

(b)

(d,2,d)
for
d\in\{9,8,6,5,4,3\}
(Geiser type)

(c)

(9,6,9)
,
(9,3,9)
,
(8,4,8)
,
(6,4,6)
,
(6,3,6)
,

or asymmetric and in one of the cases (up to swapping
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
)

(d)

(9,7,8)
,
(9,4,5)
,
(8,5,6)
,
(8,3,5)
.

If
\chi
is a
link of type III
, that is,
\sigma_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
are isomorphisms, then
\chi^{-1}
is a link of type I.

If
\chi
is a
link of type IV
, that is,
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are isomorphisms, then
B=\Spec{\Bbbk}
,
B_{1}=B_{2}={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
K_{X_{1}}^{2}=K_{X_{2}}^{2}\in\{1,2,4,8\}
.

For a surface
X
, we will call
K_{X}^{2}
the
degree
of
X
.

Note that if
X_{1}/B_{1}
satisfies
K_{X_{1}}^{2}\leq 4
, then either
K_{X_{2}}^{2}=K_{X_{1}}^{2}
, or
\chi
(or
\chi^{-1})
is a link of type I with
(K_{X_{1}}^{2},K_{X_{2}}^{2})=(4,3)
.
On the other hand, if
K_{X_{1}}^{2}\geq 5
there are many Sarkisov links where
K_{X_{1}}^{2}
and
K_{X_{2}}^{2}
differ.
In this sense, we introduce the following notion:

Definition 2.11
.

We say that a
G
-surface is
G
-birationally rich
if it is
G
-birational to a

G
-Mori fibre space of degree
\geq 5
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
X/B
be a
G
-Mori fibre space.

(1)

X
is
G
-birationally rich exactly if one of the following holds:

(a)

B=\Spec{\Bbbk}
and
X
is a
G
-minimal del Pezzo surface of degree
K_{X}^{2}\in\{9,8,6,5\}
, or

(b)

B={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
X/B
is a
G
-Mori conic bundle of degree
K_{X}^{2}\in\{8,6,5\}
.

(2)

X
is not
G
-birationally rich exactly if one of the following holds:

(a)

B=\Spec{\Bbbk}
and
X
is a
G
-minimal del Pezzo surface of degree
K_{X}^{2}\in\{4,3,2,1\}
, or

(b)

B={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
X/B
is a
G
-Mori conic bundle of degree
K_{X}^{2}\in\{4,3,2,1\}
, or

(c)

B={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
X/B
is a
G
-Mori conic bundle of degree
K_{X}^{2}\leq 0
, or

(d)

B
is a curve of genus
g(B)\geq 1
and
X/B
is a
G
-Mori conic bundle of degree
K_{X}^{2}\leq 8(1-g(B))\leq 0
.

Moreover, if
X^{\prime}/B^{\prime}
is a
G
-Mori fibre space such that
X^{\prime}
is
G
-birational to
X
, then
X/B
and
X^{\prime}/B^{\prime}
are both in one of the following, mutually excluding cases:

\{\eqref{it:SarkisovLinks--BirRich--dP},\eqref{it:SarkisovLinks--BirRich--cb}\}
,
\{\eqref{it:SarkisovLinks--NotBirRich--dP},\eqref{it:SarkisovLinks--NotBirRich--P1}\}
,
\{\eqref{it:SarkisovLinks--NotBirRich--P1neg}\}
,
\{\eqref{it:SarkisovLinks--NotBirRich--curve}\}
.

Proof.

One can see that the list has all
G
-Mori fibre spaces: for the classification of these see
[
25
, §8]
, and for the formula
K_{X}^{2}=8(1-g)
for a conic bundle without singular fibres over a curve of genus
g

[
7
, Prop. III.21]
. By definition,
G
-Mori fibre spaces of types (
1a
),(
1b
) are birationally rich.
By the Sarkisov program, any
G
-birational isomorphism between
G
-Mori fibre spaces decomposes into a composition of Sarkisov links (see
[
39
]
,
[
25
]
, or
[
58
, Th. 16.28(1)]
).
Checking the list of Sarkisov links from Proposition
2.10
, one sees that every
G
-Sarkisov link starting at a Mori fibre space of some type goes to a Mori fibre space of the same type, except for links between types (
1a
),(
1b
) and between types (
2a
),(
2b
). In particular Mori fibre spaces of type (2) are not
G
-birationally rich.
∎

Remark 2.13
.

Not all
G
-Mori conic bundles are
G
-minimal surfaces. More precisely, a
G
-Mori conic bundle
X/B
is not
G
-minimal exactly if there is a
G
-Sarkisov link of type III from
X/B
. By examination of the links in Proposition
2.10
we have:

(1)

If
K_{X}^{2}\notin\{8,6,5,3\}
, then
X
is
G
-minimal.

(2)

If
K_{X}^{2}=8
, then
X={\mathbf{F}}_{n}
is a Hirzebruch surface with
n\geq 0
, and it is minimal (and hence
G
-minimal) exactly if
n\neq 1
.

(3)

If
K_{X}^{2}\in\{6,5,3\}
, then
X
is not
G
-minimal. In this case a link of type III always exists by Remark
2.9
since
X/\Spec{\Bbbk}
is a rank
2
fibration, see
[
25
, Prop. 5.2]
.

We will denote the class of
G
-minimal del Pezzo surfaces of degree
d
by
{\mathcal{D}}_{d}
and the class of rational
G
-Mori conic bundles of degree
d
by
{\mathcal{C}}_{d}
.

Corollary 2.14
.

A
G
-surface
X
is
G
-birationally rich if and only if
there is a birational
G
-equivariant morphism from
X
to a
G
-minimal surface of one of the following types:

{\mathcal{D}}_{9},\quad{\mathcal{D}}_{8},\quad{\mathcal{D}}_{6},\quad{\mathcal{D}}_{5},\quad{\mathcal{C}}_{8}.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
2.12
as the surfaces
{\mathcal{C}}_{6}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{5}
are not minimal (see Remark
2.13
), hence can be contracted, to one of the surfaces from the list.
∎

2.3.
Derived contractions and MMP contractions

Here we introduce a class of morphisms that will play a central role in the following.
The base field
{\Bbbk}
can be any perfect field.

Definition 2.15
.

We will call a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
between varieties over
{\Bbbk}
a
derived contraction
if
{\mathrm{R}}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}={\mathcal{O}}_{Y}
.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
X,Y
be smooth projective
G
-varieties of dimension
\leq 2
over an algebraically closed field
{\Bbbk}
.
We will say that a
G
-morphism
f\colon X\to Y
is
a
G
-MMP contraction
if
f
is one of the following:

(a)

a
G
-Mori fibre space of dimension
1
or
2
,

(b)

the blow-up of a finite
G
-orbit on a
G
-surface
Y
.

In other words,
f
is a
G
-MMP contraction if
f
is an extremal contraction of a
K_{X}
-negative
G
-extremal ray of the
G
-Mori cone of
X
, see
[
47
]
.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a
G
-morphism between smooth projective
G
-varieties of dimension
\leq 2
over an algebraically closed field
{\Bbbk}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

generic fibre
f
is geometrically rational,

(b)

f
is a composition of G-MMP contractions.

If this holds, then
f
is a derived contraction.

Proof.

We sketch the proof.

(a)
\Longrightarrow
(b): run relative G-MMP for
X
over
Y
and show that eventually one gets
Y
over
Y
.

(b)
\Longrightarrow
(a): straightforward check.

The final statement follows since the class of derived contractions is closed under compositions, and every
G
-MMP contraction is a derived contraction.
∎

Definition 2.18
.

We say that a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
of surfaces over a perfect field
{\Bbbk}
is a
rational derived contraction
if
f_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}\colon X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}\to Y_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
2.17

with respect to the Galois group
G=\Gal(\overline{{\Bbbk}}/{\Bbbk})
.

Remark 2.19
.

Not every derived contraction is a rational derived contraction. For example, if
X
is an Enriques surface then the projection
X\to\Spec({\Bbbk})
is a derived contraction but not a rational derived contraction.

2.4.
Divisor classes on surfaces

Let
X
be a smooth projective rational surface over an algebraically closed field
{\Bbbk}
. It is known that
X
is an iterated blow-up of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
or of a Hirzebruch surface
{\mathbf{F}}_{d}
,
d\geq 0
. The Picard group
\Pic X
of
X
is a finitely generated free abelian group and is equipped with a non-degenerate intersection form.

If
X
is an iterated blow-up of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
at
n
points, then
\Pic X
has the basis
H,E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
, where
H
is the preimage of the line on
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and
E_{i}
is the preimage of the exceptional divisor of the
i
-th blow-up. One has

H^{2}=1,E_{i}^{2}=-1,H\cdot E_{i}=E_{i}\cdot E_{j}=0\quad\text{for $i\neq j$.}

Also one has

K_{X}=-3H+E_{1}+\ldots+E_{n}.

If
X
is an iterated blow-up of a Hirzebruch surface
{\mathbf{F}}_{d}
at
n
points, then
\Pic X
has the basis
h,s,E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
, where
h
and
s
are the preimages of the fibre and the negative section of
{\mathbf{F}}_{d}
respectively, and
E_{i}
is the preimage of the exceptional divisor of the
i
-th blow-up. One has

h^{2}=0,h\cdot s=1,s^{2}=-d,E_{i}^{2}=-1,h\cdot E_{i}=s\cdot E_{i}=E_{i}\cdot E_{j}=0\quad\text{for $i\neq j$.}

Also one has

K_{X}=-2s-(2+d)h+E_{1}+\ldots+E_{n}.

We will use the following terminology

Definition 2.20
.

Let us say that
C\subset X
is an
r
-curve
if
C
is a smooth irreducible rational curve and
C^{2}=r
.
Let us say that
D\in\Pic X
is an
r
-class
if
D^{2}+D\cdot K_{X}=-2
and
D^{2}=r
.

If
C\subset X
is a smooth irreducible rational curve then by the adjunction formula one has
C^{2}+C\cdot K_{X}=-2
, hence the divisor class of an
r
-curve is an
r
-class.

Recall that a
del Pezzo surface
is a surface
X
whose anticanonical divisor
-K_{X}
is ample, and that its
degree
is the self-intersection
d=K_{X}^{2}
. One has that
1\leq d\leq 9
, and
X
is either isomorphic to the blow-up of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
at
r=9-d
points in general position, or
X\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

Remark 2.21
.

Recall that
r\leq 8
points on
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
are in general position if no three lie on a line, no six on a conic, and (if
r=8
) not all eight lie on a singular cubic such that one of them is the singular point. Equivalently, the blow-up of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
at these points is a del Pezzo surface.

More generally, for a del Pezzo surface of degree
d
, we say that
r
points are in general position if their blow-up gives again a del Pezzo surface. For
r\leq d-2
, this is the case exactly if for every
k\geq 1
, no
k
of the points lie on a
(k-2)
-curve.

Lemma 2.22

(See
[
31
, Prop. 2.9, Cor. 2.15]
,
[
60
, Lemma 4.7]
)
.

Assume
X
is a del Pezzo surface of degree
d
and
D
is an
r
-class on
X
. Then

(1)

if
r=-2
then the pair
({\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}}(D))
is completely orthogonal:
h^{i}({\mathcal{O}}(D))=h^{i}({\mathcal{O}}(-D))=0
for all
i
,

(2)

if
d\geq 3
then any
0
-class is realised by a smooth irreducible curve and defines a morphism
X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
with general fibre
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
,

(3)

if
d\geq 4
then any
1
-class is realised by a smooth irreducible curve and defines a birational morphism
X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
.

There are symmetries between certain classes of divisors as the following lemma explains.

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
X
be a del Pezzo surface of degree
d
.

(1)

Assume
D,D^{\prime}\in\Pic X
and
D+D^{\prime}=-K_{X}
. If
D
is an
r
-class then
D^{\prime}
is an
r^{\prime}
-class, where
r+r^{\prime}=d-4
.

(2)

Assume
D,D^{\prime}\in\Pic X
and
D+D^{\prime}=-2K_{X}
. If
D
is a
(d-2)
-class then
D^{\prime}
is also a
(d-2)
-class.

Proof.

Follows from the definitions, see
[
31
, Lemma 2.5]
,
[
60
, Lemma 4.7]
.
∎

For
-1\leq r\leq 1
the number of
r
-classes on a del Pezzo surface of degree
d
(different from
{\mathbf{F}}_{1}
) is finite and given in Table
1
. Explicitly, assume
d\geq 5
. Then

the
-1
-classes are

\displaystyle E_{i}\quad\text{and $H-E_{i_{1}}-E_{i_{2}}$,}

(2.1)

the
0
-classes are

\displaystyle H-E_{i}\quad\text{and $2H-E_{i_{1}}-E_{i_{2}}-E_{i_{3}}-E_{i_{4}}$ (for $d=5$),}

(2.2)

1
-classes are

\displaystyle H\quad\text{and $2H-E_{i_{1}}-E_{i_{2}}-E_{i_{3}}$ (for $d\leq 6$).}

On
{\mathbf{F}}_{0}
, the
0
-classes are
h_{1},h_{2}
(the fibres of two
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-fibrations) and there are no
1
-classes.

Throughout the paper we will denote
0
-classes by
h_{i}
and
1
-classes by
H_{i}
.

\begin{array}[]{|c||c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr d&9&8&7&6&5\\
\hline\cr r=-1&0&0&3&6&10\\
r=0&0&2&2&3&5\\
r=1&1&0&1&2&5\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 1.
Number of
r
-classes on del Pezzo surfaces (different from
{\mathbf{F}}_{1}
) of degrees
9
to
5

3.
Semi-orthogonal decompositions and their descent

3.1.
Preliminaries on triangulated categories

Here we recall necessary notions, with an accent on SOD-s, exceptional collections, and mutations. Our references in this section are
[
18
]
and
[
38
]
.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}
be a triangulated category. We denote
\Hom^{i}(T_{1},T_{2}):=\Hom(T_{1},T_{2}[i])
for
T_{1},T_{2}\in{\mathbf{T}}
,
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Recall that a full subcategory
{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{T}}
is called
triangulated
if
{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}
is closed under shifts and taking cones, and
thick
if
{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}
is triangulated and idempotent-closed in
{\mathbf{T}}
.

3.1.1.
Semi-orthogonal decompositions and their mutations

Definition 3.1
.

A
semi-orthogonal decomposition
(SOD) of
{\mathbf{T}}
is given by an ordered collection
{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}
of full triangulated subcategories in
{\mathbf{T}}
such that

(a)

\Hom(A_{j},A_{i})=0
for all
i<j
and
A_{i}\in{\mathbf{A}}_{i},A_{j}\in{\mathbf{A}}_{j}
,

(b)

{\mathbf{T}}
is generated by
{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}
as a triangulated subcategory:
{\mathbf{T}}
is the smallest triangulated subcategory in
{\mathbf{T}}
that contains all of
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}
.

Semi-orthogonal decompositions are denoted by

{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle.

For a subcategory (or a collection of objects)
{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{T}}
the
orthogonal subcategories

{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp}=\{T\mid\Hom^{i}(A,T)=0\quad\text{for all $A\in{\mathbf{A}}$ and $i$}\},\quad^{\perp}{\mathbf{A}}=\{T\mid\Hom^{i}(T,A)=0\quad\text{for all $A\in{\mathbf{A}}$ and $i$}\}

are defined, they are full and triangulated.

Any component
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}
of an SOD is determined by the other components: one has

{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={}^{\perp}\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{i-1}\rangle\cap\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle^{\perp}.

Hence the following trivial observation that will be frequently used in the paper:

Lemma 3.2
.

If

{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle=\langle{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}_{n}\rangle

are two SOD-s and
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}={\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
,
i\neq k
, then
{\mathbf{A}}_{k}={\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}_{k}
as well.

Definition 3.3
.

A full subcategory
{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{T}}
is called
left (right) admissible
if the inclusion functor

{\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{T}}
has a left (right) adjoint functors.
A full subcategory
{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{T}}
is called
admissible
if it is both left and right admissible.

For a left (right) admissible subcategory
{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{T}}
there is a semi-orthogonal decomposition

{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}},{}^{\perp}{\mathbf{A}}\rangle
(resp.
{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp},{\mathbf{A}}\rangle
).
For an admissible subcategory
{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{T}}
one has both:

\langle{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp},{\mathbf{A}}\rangle={\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}},{}^{\perp}{\mathbf{A}}\rangle.

Moreover, the orthogonals
{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp}
and
{}^{\perp}{\mathbf{A}}
are equivalent via
mutation functors

R_{{\mathbf{A}}}\colon{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp}\to{}^{\perp}{\mathbf{A}},\quad L_{{\mathbf{A}}}\colon{}^{\perp}{\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp},

where
R_{{\mathbf{A}}}
is the restriction to
{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp}
of the right adjoint
{\mathbf{T}}\to{}^{\perp}{\mathbf{A}}
to the inclusion, and

L_{{\mathbf{A}}}
is the restriction to
{}^{\perp}{\mathbf{A}}
of the left adjoint
{\mathbf{T}}\to{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp}
to the inclusion. These mutation functors are inverse to each other.

Assume a semi-orthogonal decomposition

{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle

consists of admissible subcategories. For
1\leq i\leq n-1
, one defines the
i
-th right mutation as the SOD

{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{i-1},{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1},R_{{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1}}{\mathbf{A}}_{i},{\mathbf{A}}_{i+2},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle.

Note that
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}
and
R_{{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1}}{\mathbf{A}}_{i}
are equivalent via mutation functors
R_{{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1}}
,

L_{{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1}}
.

Similarly, for
i=2,\ldots,n
one defines
i
-th left mutation as the SOD

{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{i-2},L_{{\mathbf{A}}_{i-1}}{\mathbf{A}}_{i},{\mathbf{A}}_{i-1},{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle.

As above,
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}
is equivalent to
L_{{\mathbf{A}}_{i-1}}{\mathbf{A}}_{i}
via mutation functors.

Assume that all subcategories involved are admissible, then right and left mutations define operations on the set of SOD-s of a triangulated category
{\mathbf{T}}
into
n
components, we will denote them by
R_{i}
(for
i=1,\ldots,n-1
) and
L_{i}
(for
i=2,\ldots,n
).
Operations
R_{i}
and
L_{i+1}
are inverse to each other for
i=1,\ldots,n-1
.
Moreover, mutations define an action of the
n
-th braid group on the set of SOD-s of
{\mathbf{T}}
into
n
components. We will say that two SOD-s are
mutation-equivalent
if there is a sequence of mutations sending one into another, i.e., they belong to the same orbit.

3.1.2.
Exceptional objects

Let
{\Bbbk}
be a field. Recall that a category is
{\Bbbk}
-linear
if its
\Hom
spaces are
{\Bbbk}
-vector spaces and the composition is
{\Bbbk}
-bilinear. A
{\Bbbk}
-linear category is
\Hom
-finite
if all
\Hom
-spaces are finite-dimensional. For a triangulated
{\Bbbk}
-linear category
{\mathbf{T}}
and
T_{1},T_{2}\in{\mathbf{T}}
we denote

\Hom^{\bullet}(T_{1},T_{2}):=\oplus_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Hom^{i}(T_{1},T_{2}),

this is a graded vector space. Category
{\mathbf{T}}
is called
\Ext
-finite
if the space
\Hom^{\bullet}(T_{1},T_{2})

is finite-dimensional for all
T_{1},T_{2}
in
{\mathbf{T}}
.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}
be a triangulated
{\Bbbk}
-linear category.

Definition 3.4
.

An object
E\in{\mathbf{T}}
is called
exceptional
if

•

\End(E)={\Bbbk}
, and

•

\Hom^{i}(E,E)=0
for
i\neq 0
.

More generally, an object
E\in{\mathbf{T}}
is called
weakly exceptional
if

•

\End(E)
is a finite-dimensional division
{\Bbbk}
-algebra, and

•

\Hom^{i}(E,E)=0
for
i\neq 0
.

If
E
is weakly exceptional and
\End(E)=D
we say that
E
is
D
-exceptional
. Note also that exceptional and weakly exceptional are the same if
{\Bbbk}
is algebraically closed.

Definition 3.5
.

A collection of objects
(E_{1},\ldots,E_{n})
is called
exceptional (weakly exceptional)
if all
E_{i}
are
exceptional (weakly exceptional) and

•

\Hom^{i}(E_{l},E_{k})=0
for all
i
and
k<l
.

A weakly exceptional collection of objects
(E_{1},\ldots,E_{n})
is called a
block
if

•

\Hom^{i}(E_{l},E_{k})=0
for all
i
and
k\neq l
.

If for some weakly exceptional collection
(E_{1},\ldots,E_{n})
a subcollection
(E_{p},\ldots,E_{q})
is a block, it is standard to write it vertically:

\left(E_{1},\ldots,E_{p-1},\begin{smallmatrix}E_{p}\\
\ldots\\
E_{q}\end{smallmatrix},E_{q+1},\ldots,E_{n}\right)

Definition 3.6
.

A weakly exceptional collection of objects
(E_{1},\ldots,E_{n})
in
{\mathbf{T}}
is called
full
if these objects generate
{\mathbf{T}}
as a triangulated category:
{\mathbf{T}}
is the smallest triangulated subcategory in
{\mathbf{T}}
that contains all of
E_{i}
.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}
be an
\Ext
-finite
{\Bbbk}
-linear triangulated category. For any
D
-exceptional object
E
in
{\mathbf{T}}
, the subcategory
\langle E\rangle
generated by
E
is admissible. The right adjoint functor
{\mathbf{T}}\to\langle E\rangle
is given by

{\mathbf{T}}\ni T\mapsto\Hom^{\bullet}(E,T)\otimes_{D}E=\oplus_{i}\Hom^{i}(E,T)\otimes_{D}E[-i]\in\langle E\rangle,

and the left adjoint functor is given by

{\mathbf{T}}\ni T\mapsto\Hom^{\bullet}(T,E)^{*}\otimes_{D}E=\oplus_{i}\Hom^{i}(T,E)^{*}\otimes_{D}E[i]\in\langle E\rangle.

Consequently, mutation functors
R_{E}=R_{\langle E\rangle}
and
L_{E}=L_{\langle E\rangle}
via
E
fit into exact triangles

(3.1)

\displaystyle R_{E}(T)\to T\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}\Hom^{\bullet}(T,E)^{*}\otimes_{D}E\to R_{E}(T)[1],

(3.2)

\displaystyle L_{E}(T)[-1]\to\Hom^{\bullet}(E,T)\otimes_{D}E\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}T\to L_{E}(T),

where the maps marked by
\mathrm{ev}
are canonical evaluation maps.

It follows that any weakly exceptional and full collection of objects
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
in
{\mathbf{T}}
generates a semi-orthogonal decomposition

{\mathbf{T}}=\langle\langle E_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle E_{n}\rangle\rangle.

3.1.3.
Serre functor

Let
{\mathbf{T}}
be a
\Hom
-finite
{\Bbbk}
-linear category. A
{\Bbbk}
-linear functor
S\colon{\mathbf{T}}\to{\mathbf{T}}
is called a
Serre functor
if

(a)

S
is an equivalence, and

(b)

there are isomorphisms of
{\Bbbk}
-vector spaces for all
T_{1},T_{2}\in{\mathbf{T}}

(3.3)

\Hom(T_{1},T_{2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Hom(T_{2},S(T_{1}))^{*},

natural in
T_{1},T_{2}
(where
*
denotes the dual
{\Bbbk}
-vector space).

A Serre functor, if exists, is unique up to an isomorphism. A Serre functor on a triangulated category is automatically exact. Equality (
3.3
) is known as
Serre duality
.

Assume
{\mathbf{T}}
has a Serre functor and

{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle

is an SOD with admissible components. Then there are SOD-s

{\mathbf{T}}=\langle S({\mathbf{A}}_{n}),{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n-1}\rangle,\qquad{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n},S^{-1}({\mathbf{A}}_{1})\rangle.

In particular, by Lemma
3.2

(3.4)

S({\mathbf{A}}_{n})=L_{{\mathbf{A}}_{1}}L_{{\mathbf{A}}_{2}}\ldots L_{{\mathbf{A}}_{n-1}}({\mathbf{A}}_{n}),\qquad S^{-1}({\mathbf{A}}_{1})=R_{{\mathbf{A}}_{n}}R_{{\mathbf{A}}_{n-1}}\ldots R_{{\mathbf{A}}_{2}}({\mathbf{A}}_{1}).

3.2.
Preliminaries on derived categories of coherent sheaves

Here we recall necessary background on derived categories of coherent sheaves on algebraic varieties and provide some types of mutations for exceptional collections on surfaces that will be used in the proof of main results.

Recall that all varieties are supposed to be smooth and projective.
We denote the bounded derived category of coherent sheaves on a variety
X
over a field
{\Bbbk}
by
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
. This is a
{\Bbbk}
-linear
\Ext
-finite triangulated category. It has a Serre functor given by

S_{X}(-)=-\otimes\omega_{X}[\dim X].

It follows from
[
18
]
that
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and its left or right admissible subcategories are saturated, in particular, any left or right admissible subcategory is admissible. Therefore, any component of any SOD consists of admissible subcategories, and all mutations exist.

For a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
between algebraic varieties, we will denote by
f_{*}\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
and
f^{*}\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
the
derived
push-forward and pull-back functors respectively, unless otherwise stated. Also, for two complexes of coherent sheaves,
\otimes
will denote their
derived
tensor product.

Example 3.7

(Beilinson
[
8
]
)
.

The collection of sheaves
{\mathcal{O}}(a-n),\ldots,{\mathcal{O}}(a-1),{\mathcal{O}}(a)
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
is full and exceptional for any
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Therefore, there are semi-orthogonal decompositions
{\mathcal{A}}_{a}
:

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n})=\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(a-n)\rangle,\ldots,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(a-1)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(a)\rangle\rangle.

It follows from Lemma
3.2
or from (
3.4
) that

L_{\langle{\mathcal{O}}(a-n)\rangle}\ldots L_{\langle{\mathcal{O}}(a-1)\rangle}(\langle{\mathcal{O}}(a)\rangle)\cong\langle{\mathcal{O}}(a-n-1)\rangle,

hence
{\mathcal{A}}_{a-1}
is mutation-equivalent to
{\mathcal{A}}_{a}
.
Therefore, these SOD-s are mutation-equivalent for all
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Lemma 3.8

(Bridgeland
[
19
, Prop. 2.3]
)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a rational derived contraction between smooth varieties (see Definition
2.18
). Then the derived inverse image functor

f^{*}\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

is fully faithful.

Proof.

The adjunction unit
\id_{{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)}\to f_{*}f^{*}
is given by tensoring with the map
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}\to f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}={\mathcal{O}}_{Y}
by the projection formula, hence is an isomorphism. It follows then that
f^{*}
is fully faithful:

\Hom_{X}(f^{*}F_{1},f^{*}F_{2})=\Hom_{Y}(F_{1},f_{*}f^{*}F_{2})=\Hom_{Y}(F_{1},F_{2})

for all
F_{1},F_{2}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
.
∎

For a blow-up at a smooth centre one can say more:

Proposition 3.9

(Orlov’s blow-up formula,
[
67
]
)
.

Let
Z\subset X
be smooth varieties, let
r=\codim_{X}Z
. Consider the blow-up of
X
at
Z
:

\textstyle{{\tilde{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{{\tilde{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X.}

Then one has SOD-s

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\tilde{X}})=\langle j_{*}(\pi^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\tilde{Z}}}(-r+1)),\ldots,j_{*}(\pi^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\tilde{Z}}}(-1)),p^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\rangle=\\
=\langle p^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X),j_{*}(\pi^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\tilde{Z}}}),\ldots,j_{*}(\pi^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\tilde{Z}}}(r-2))\rangle,

where the component are the images of fully faithful functors
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\tilde{X}})
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\tilde{X}})
.
q

As a special case one gets (using Lemma
3.2
)

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
X
be a surface over
{\Bbbk}
, let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}\in X
be
{\Bbbk}
-points. Let
{\tilde{X}}\xrightarrow{p}X
be the blow-up at
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
with the exceptional divisors
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
. Then the objects
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{i}}(j)
are exceptional for
i=1,\ldots,n
and
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and

\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(-1)\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{n}}(-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,p^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\right\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\left\langle p^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X),\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle\right\rangle

are mutation-equivalent SOD-s of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\tilde{X})
.

Remark 3.11
.

Let
E\in\Coh(X)
be an exceptional vector bundle, and let
T\in\Coh(X)
. Then
\Hom^{\bullet}(E,T)\cong{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X,T\otimes E^{\vee})
.
If
\Hom^{\bullet}(E,T)={\Bbbk}^{\oplus r}[-d]
(i.e. concentrated in degree
d
), then one can sometimes determine the exact triangle (
3.2
) (and hence the left mutation
L_{E}(T)
) with an exact sequence of sheaves:

(1)

If
d=0
and the evaluation morphism
\ev\colon E^{\oplus r}\to T
is surjective then
L_{E}(T)=K[1]
, where
K
fits into an exact sequence of sheaves
0\to K\to E^{\oplus r}\xrightarrow{\ev}T\to 0
.

(2)

If
d=1
then
L_{E}(T)
is given by the universal extension:
L_{E}(T)=A
is the sheaf fitting into the exact sequence of sheaves
0\to T\to A\to E^{\oplus r}\to 0
induced by
\ev\colon E^{\oplus r}\to T[1]
.

Three following lemmas are standard, we include them for the convenience of the readers.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
X
be a surface with
{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\Bbbk}
,
let
E
be a
(-1)
-curve on
X
and
{\mathcal{O}}(D)
be a line bundle on
X
. Then

(1)

the pairs
({\mathcal{O}}(D),{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(a))
and
({\mathcal{O}}_{E}(a),{\mathcal{O}}(D-E))
are exceptional if and only if
a=D\cdot E
;

(2)

three pairs below are exceptional and mutations of each other (up to a shift) as shown, where
a=D\cdot E
:

\textstyle{({\mathcal{O}}(D-E),{\mathcal{O}}(D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{2}}
\scriptstyle{R_{1}}
\textstyle{({\mathcal{O}}(D),{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(a))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{2}}
\scriptstyle{R_{1}}
\textstyle{({\mathcal{O}}_{E}(a),{\mathcal{O}}(D-E)).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{2}}
\scriptstyle{R_{1}}

Proof.

Note that all line bundles on
X
are exceptional by the assumption
{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\Bbbk}
.

For (
1
), the pair
({\mathcal{O}}_{E}(a),{\mathcal{O}}(D-E))
is exceptional if and only if

0=\Hom^{\bullet}({\mathcal{O}}(D-E),{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(a))={\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(a-E\cdot(D-E))),

which is equivalent to
a-E\cdot(D-E)=-1
,
a=D\cdot E
. Similarly for the first pair.

For (
2
), the mutations in question are given by the exact sequence (see e.g. Remark
3.11
)

(3.5)

0\to{\mathcal{O}}(D-E)\to{\mathcal{O}}(D)\to{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(a)\to 0.

∎

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
X
be a surface with
{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\Bbbk}
, let
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\subset X
be a collection of disjoint
(-1)
-curves. Then the SOD-s

\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(-1)\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{n}}(-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,{\mathbf{A}},\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle={\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\left\langle{\mathbf{A}},\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-E_{1})\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-E_{n})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle

are mutation-equivalent.

Proof.

Follows from Proposition
3.10
and Lemma
3.12
.
∎

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
X
be a del Pezzo surface of degree
K_{X}^{2}\geq 3
, let
h
be a
0
-class on
X
, and
{\mathcal{O}}(D)
be a line bundle on
X
. Then the following pairs are exceptional and are mutations of each other (up to a shift) as shown:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 39.15996pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-39.15996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{({\mathcal{O}}(D-h),{\mathcal{O}}(D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.28961pt\raise 8.73694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.89168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{R_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 93.15996pt\raise 2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 63.15996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 93.15996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{({\mathcal{O}}(D),{\mathcal{O}}(D+h))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.5766pt\raise-8.73692pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.89168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{L_{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.15997pt\raise-2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.22
(2), linear system
|h|
defines a conic bundle
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
so that
{\mathcal{O}}(h)\cong f^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(1)
. By Lemma
3.8
pull-back functor
f^{*}
is fully faithful and hence commute with mutations. We have the mutation of exceptional objects
L_{\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\rangle}(\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(1)\rangle)\cong\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(-1)\rangle
by Example
3.7
, therefore
L_{\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle}(\langle{\mathcal{O}}(h)\rangle)\cong\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h)\rangle
. The results follows now by twisting by
D
.
∎

3.3.
Three-block semi-orthogonal decompositions for del Pezzo surfaces

Here we describe semi-orthogonal decompositions for del Pezzo surfaces of degree
\geq 5
that consist of three blocks of exceptional objects and are invariant under all automorphisms of the surfaces. They have been found by Karpov and Nogin in
[
43
]
, here we recall the construction.

Lemma 3.15

(Three-block decompositions)
.

Let
{\Bbbk}
be an algebraically closed field and
X=X_{d}
be a del Pezzo surface of degree
d
over
{\Bbbk}
. We write
\{h_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for the
0
-classes and
\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
for the
1
-classes on
X
. Then the following are
3
-block decompositions of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
, and each block is preserved by
\Aut(X)
:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbb{P}}^{2})=

\displaystyle\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-2H)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-H)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\emptyset,

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\{1\},

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1})=

\displaystyle\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1}-h_{2})\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\{1,2\},

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\emptyset,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{6})=

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\{1,2,3\},

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\{1,2\},

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{5})=

\displaystyle\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\{1,\ldots,5\},

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\{1,\ldots,5\},

where
{\mathcal{E}}
is a vector bundle of rank
2
on
X_{5}
fitting into an exact sequence

(3.6)

0\to{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{i})\to{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{i})\to 0

for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,5\}
such that
h_{i}+H_{i}=-K_{X}
.
Moreover, for
d=6
or
5
these SOD-s are obtained from the SOD provided by the blow-up formula applied to the blow-up
X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
at
r=9-d
points:

(3.7)

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{d})=\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(-1)\\
\cdots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{r}}(-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-2H)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-H)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

by mutations and merging two completely orthogonal components into one.

Proof.

The decompositions for
X={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and
X={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
come from the standard Beilinson exceptional collections, see Example
3.7
.
For
d\in\{6,5\}
, decompositions with three blocks were found in
[
43
]
, and it was remarked in
[
28
]
that each block is preserved by
\Aut(X)
. However, since we use slightly different
3
-block decompositions, we recall how to obtain them from (
3.7
) by mutations.

In both cases, we first use Lemma
3.12
to perform mutations

\text{\eqref{eq_BlrP2}}\overset{L_{2}}{\leadsto}\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-2H+\sum E_{i})\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(-1)\\
\cdots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{r}}(-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-H)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle\overset{R_{2}}{\leadsto}\\
\overset{}{\leadsto}\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-2H+\sum E_{i})\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-H)\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-H+E_{1})\\
\cdots\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-H+E_{r})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle.

For
X=X_{6}
we have
r=3
,
H_{1}=2H-(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})
,
H_{2}=H
,
h_{i}=H-E_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
(see (
2.1
)) and (
2.2
)). Since
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})
and
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})
are orthogonal, we obtain exactly the desired
3
-block decomposition.

For
X=X_{5}
, we have
r=4
,
h_{i}=H-E_{i}
,
H_{i}=2H-(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}+E_{4})+E_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,4
, and
h_{5}=2H-(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}+E_{4})
,
H_{5}=H
(see (
2.1
)) and (
2.2
)).
Here, an additional mutation is needed:

\displaystyle\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{5})\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}{(-h_{1})}\\
\cdots\\
{\mathcal{O}}{(-h_{4})}\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle\overset{L_{2}}{\leadsto}\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{E}}_{5}\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}{(-h_{1})}\\
\cdots\\
{\mathcal{O}}{(-h_{4})}\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle.

We used that

\Hom^{\bullet}({\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5}),{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{5}))\cong{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X,{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{5}+h_{5}))\cong{\Bbbk}[-1]
, therefore the exact triangle obtained from (
3.6
) is a rotation of (
3.2
), giving
{\mathcal{E}}_{5}=L_{{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})}({\mathcal{O}}(-H_{5}))
for
i=5
.
Since the
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{i})
are orthogonal to each other, we obtain a
3
-block decomposition. Using the map
X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
given by the 1-class
H_{i}
, one gets a similar SOD with
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
fitting into (
3.6
). Since all these
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
generate the same subcategory in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and are exceptional, they are isomorphic and may be denoted by
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Finally, it is clear that each block is preserved by
\Aut(X)
because the set of
0
-classes as well as the set of
1
-classes are preserved, and if two of three blocks are preserved then the third one also is by Lemma
3.2
.
∎

We will denote the
3
-block decomposition of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
defined in Lemma
3.15
by
{\mathcal{S}}(X)
. These decompositions are compatible to each other in the following sense.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a birational morphism between two del Pezzo surfaces of degrees
K_{X}^{2},K_{Y}^{2}\in\{5,6,8,9\}
,
X,Y\not\cong{\mathbf{F}}_{1}
.
Then the
4
-block decomposition

\langle\ker f_{*},f^{*}{\mathcal{S}}(Y)\rangle

of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is mutation-equivalent to a refinement of
{\mathcal{S}}(X)
.

Proof.

If
K_{Y}^{2}=9
, take the mutations from Lemma
3.15
.
The cases
(K_{X}^{2},K_{Y}^{2})\in\{(5,6),(5,8),(6,8)\}
remain.

\mathbf{(6,8)}
: We have
K_{X}=-h_{1}-h_{2}-h_{3}
and we compare

(3.8)

\langle\ker f_{*},f^{*}{\mathcal{S}}(Y)\rangle=\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(-1)\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{2}}(-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1}-h_{2})\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle

with

(3.9)

\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

which is a refinement of
{\mathcal{S}}(X)
. We do mutations as follows:

\text{\eqref{eq:6-8--2}}\overset{R_{1}}{\leadsto}\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1}-h_{2})\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\\
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle,\right\rangle\overset{-K}{\leadsto}\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\\
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(h_{3})\rangle\right\rangle\overset{L_{4}}{\leadsto}\text{\eqref{eq:6-8--1}}

Here and later on we denote by
-K
the mutation of the first component to the end, which is given by tensoring with
{\mathcal{O}}(-K_{X})
, see (
3.4
). The last mutation is by Lemma
3.14
. Note that we do not track one of the components but use Lemma
3.2
to deduce that the result of our mutations coincides with (
3.9
).

\mathbf{(5,6)}
: Classes
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},H_{1},H_{2}
on
X
are the pull-backs from
Y
. We have
h_{4}=-H_{1}-K_{X},h_{5}=-H_{2}-K_{X}

by Lemma
2.23
, and we compare

(3.10)

\langle\ker f_{*},f^{*}{\mathcal{S}}(X_{6})\rangle=\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(-1)\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle

with

(3.11)

\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

which is a refinement of
{\mathcal{S}}(X_{5})
. They are mutation-equivalent via the following mutations:

\text{\eqref{eq:5-6--1}}{\overset{\text{Lemma}~\ref{lemma_OE}}{\leadsto}}\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-E)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle\overset{-K}{\leadsto}\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-E)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(h_{4})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(h_{5})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle\right\rangle\overset{L_{4}}{\leadsto}\\
\leadsto\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-E)\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle\overset{L_{2}}{\leadsto}\eqref{eq:5-6--2}.

\mathbf{(5,8)}
: This time we denote by
h_{4},h_{5}
the
0
-classes on
X
pulled back from
Y
. For
i=1,2,3
we note that
h_{4}+h_{5}
is a
2
-class,
H_{i}=h_{4}+h_{5}-E_{i}
is a
1
-class, and
h_{i}=-K_{X}-H_{i}
by Lemma
2.23
.
We compare

(3.12)

\langle\ker f_{*},f^{*}{\mathcal{S}}(Y)\rangle=\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(-1)\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{2}}(-1)\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{3}}(-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4}-h_{5})\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle

with (
3.11
), which is a refinement of
{\mathcal{S}}(X_{5})
.
We do mutations

\text{\eqref{eq:5-8--1}}\overset{R_{1}}{\leadsto}\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4}-h_{5})\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4}-h_{5}+E_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4}-h_{5}+E_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4}-h_{5}+E_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle=\\
=\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4}-h_{5})\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle\overset{R_{1}}{\leadsto}\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle\overset{-K}{\leadsto}\\
\leadsto\left\langle\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(h_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(h_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle\right\rangle\overset{L_{4}}{\leadsto}\left\langle\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle\overset{R_{2}}{\leadsto}\eqref{eq:5-6--2}.

∎

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
X
be a del Pezzo surface of degree
1
or
2
, let
\sigma\colon X\to X
be the Bertini/Geiser involution. Let
{\mathbf{A}}={\mathcal{O}}^{\perp}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and
S_{{\mathbf{A}}}
be the Serre functor on
{\mathbf{A}}
. Then

\displaystyle S_{{\mathbf{A}}}^{3}\cong\sigma^{*}[5]

for
d=1
,

\displaystyle S_{{\mathbf{A}}}^{2}\cong\sigma^{*}[3]

for
d=2
.

Proof.

If
\deg X=1
then
X
is a double cover of
{\mathbb{P}}(1,1,2)
, ramified over a curve of degree
6
(degree relative to
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}(1,1,2)}(1)
), and
\sigma
is the deck transformation. Viewing
{\mathbb{P}}(1,1,2)
as a smooth stack, the map
X\to{\mathbb{P}}(1,1,2)
is ramified exactly over the curve (and not over the stacky point). Hence the statement is a special case of
[
53
, Cor. 5.3]
, where
M={\mathbb{P}}(1,1,2)
,
k=1
,
d_{1}=3
,
\ind(M)=4
,
\ind(X)=1
,
c=1
,
{\mathcal{R}}_{M}=0
, and
{\mathbf{T}}=\id
. Note that
({\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}}(1),{\mathcal{O}}(2),{\mathcal{O}}(3))
is a full and exceptional collection in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbb{P}}(1,1,2))
by
[
20
, Remark 2.2.6]
.

If
\deg X=2
then
X
is a double cover of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
ramified over a quartic, and
\sigma
is the deck transformation. The statement is a special case of
[
53
, Cor. 5.3]
, where
M={\mathbb{P}}^{2}={\mathbb{P}}(1,1,1)
,
k=1
,
d_{1}=2
,
\ind(M)=3
,
\ind(X)=1
,
c=1
,
{\mathcal{R}}_{M}=0
, and
{\mathbf{T}}=\id
.
∎

3.4.
Group actions on categories

Here we recall necessary definitions and facts about group actions on categories and equivariant categories. We refer to
[
24
]
,
[
26
, 4.1.3]
,
[
72
]
for details.

Definition 3.18
.

Let
G
be a group and
{\mathbf{C}}
be a category. An
action
of
G
on
{\mathbf{C}}
is given by

•

a collection of autoequivalences

\rho_{g}\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}},\quad g\in G,

and

•

a collection of functorial isomorphisms

\epsilon_{g,h}\colon\rho_{g}\rho_{h}\to\rho_{gh},\quad g,h\in G,

satisfying the cocycle condition given by the commutativity of the diagram of functorial isomorphisms:

(3.13)

\textstyle{\rho_{f}\rho_{g}\rho_{h}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{f,g}\rho_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{f}\epsilon_{g,h}}
\textstyle{\rho_{fg}\rho_{h}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{fg,h}}
\textstyle{\rho_{f}\rho_{gh}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{f,gh}}
\textstyle{\rho_{fgh}}

for any
f,g,h\in\ G
.
An action is called
strict
if
\rho_{gh}=\rho_{g}\rho_{h}
and
\epsilon_{g,h}=\id
for all
g,h
.

Remark 3.19
.

The definition of a strict action may seem to be more natural and appealing than that of a general action.
Moreover, there is a result (see
[
72
, Th. 5.4]
) saying that any group action is equivalent to a strict group action. However, the framework of strict actions is too restrictive: many natural group actions on categories are not strict,
and strictifiying them produces categories with unnecessary excessive objects. For example, non-strict
G
-actions on a category
\mathsf{pt}_{{\Bbbk}}
with one object and endomorphisms
{\Bbbk}
from Example
3.29
are equivalent to strict actions on a category with
|G|
isomorphic objects, but are not equivalent to a strict action on the one-object category
\mathsf{pt}_{\Bbbk}
.

We will call a category with a
G
-action a
G
-category
.

Definition 3.20
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{D}}
be
G
-categories. A
G
-equivariant functor
from
{\mathbf{C}}
to
{\mathbf{D}}
is given by

•

a functor

\phi\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}},

and

•

a collection of functorial isomorphisms

\beta_{g}\colon\phi\rho_{g}^{{\mathbf{C}}}\to\rho_{g}^{{\mathbf{D}}}\phi,

satisfying for all
g,h\in G
the compatibility conditions that are given by the commutativity of the diagram of functorial isomorphisms

(3.14)

\textstyle{\phi\rho_{g}^{{\mathbf{C}}}\rho_{h}^{{\mathbf{C}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{g}\rho_{h}^{{\mathbf{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\phi\epsilon_{g,h}^{{\mathbf{C}}}}
\textstyle{\rho_{g}^{{\mathbf{D}}}\phi\rho_{h}^{{\mathbf{C}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{g}^{{\mathbf{D}}}\beta_{h}}
