\textstyle{\rho_{g}^{{\mathbf{D}}}\rho_{h}^{{\mathbf{D}}}\phi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{g,h}^{{\mathbf{D}}}\phi}
\textstyle{\phi\rho_{gh}^{{\mathbf{C}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{gh}}
\textstyle{\rho_{gh}^{{\mathbf{D}}}\phi.}

Definition 3.21
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{D}}
be
G
-categories and
\phi,\psi\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be
G
-equivariant functors. A
G
-morphism
(or a
G
-natural transformation
) from
\phi
to
\psi
is a morphism of functors
f\colon\phi\to\psi
such that

\beta_{g}^{\psi}\circ f\rho_{g}^{{\mathbf{C}}}=\rho_{g}^{{\mathbf{D}}}(f)\circ\beta_{g}^{\phi}

for all
g
.

Two categories with a
G
-action are called
G
-equivariantly equivalent
if there is a
G
-equivariant equivalence between them. In particular, one can speak of
G
-equivariant equivalence of
G
-actions on a fixed category. The following observation will be used below, sometimes implicitly.

Remark 3.22
.

For two equivalent categories
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
,
G
-actions on
{\mathbf{C}}
correspond to
G
-actions on
{\mathbf{D}}
, up to equivariant equivalence.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a category with an action of a group
G
. We say that a full subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
is
G
-invariant
if
\rho_{g}(C)\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
for any object
C\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
, and remark that an invariant subcategory naturally has induced
G
-action. We say that an SOD of a triangulated category
{\mathbf{T}}
is
G
-invariant
if all its components are
G
-invariant.

Remark 3.23
.

Any mutation of a
G
-invariant SOD is also a
G
-invariant SOD. Indeed, if

{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{i-2},{\mathbf{A}}_{i},R_{{\mathbf{A}}_{i}}{\mathbf{A}}_{i-1},{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1},{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle

and all
{\mathbf{A}}_{j}
are
G
-invariant then

R_{{\mathbf{A}}_{i}}{\mathbf{A}}_{i-1}={}^{\perp}\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{i-2},{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\rangle\cap\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle^{\perp}

also is
G
-invariant.

The following lemma will be crucial for our study of atoms in subsequent sections.

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}
be a triangulated category with an action of a group
G
.
If
{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle
is a
G
-invariant SOD into admissible components then the mutation functors

{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\to R_{{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1}}({\mathbf{A}}_{i}),\quad{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1}\to L_{{\mathbf{A}}_{i}}({\mathbf{A}}_{i+1})

are
G
-equivariant equivalences for all
i=1,\ldots,n-1
.
In particular, if two SOD-s

{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{n}\rangle

are mutation-equivalent then
the collections of
G
-categories
{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}
and
{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{n}
are the same up to a permutation and
G
-equivariant equivalences.

Proof.

We check the claim for the right mutation.
Recall that the mutation functor
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\to R_{{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1}}({\mathbf{A}}_{i})
is the composition of the embedding functor
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\to{\mathbf{T}}
and the right adjoint functor
{\mathbf{T}}\to{}^{\perp}{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1}
to the embedding

{}^{\perp}{\mathbf{A}}_{i+1}\to{\mathbf{T}}
. The embedding of a
G
-invariant subcategory is naturally a
G
-equivariant functor, and the adjoint functor to a
G
-equivariant functor is
G
-equivariant, see
[
72
, Prop. 3.9]
. The second claim follows by induction.
∎

Definition 3.25
.

We say that an object
E
in a
G
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
is
G
-invariant
if
\rho_{g}(E)
is isomorphic to
E
for all
g\in G
.

Definition 3.26
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a
G
-category and
E\in{\mathbf{C}}
be a
G
-invariant object. An
equivariant structure
on
E
(or a
G
-linearisation
of
E
) is given by a collection of isomorphisms for all
g

\theta_{g}\colon E\to\rho_{g}(E)

subject to conditions for all
g,h

\theta_{gh}=\epsilon_{g,h}\circ\rho_{g}(\theta_{h})\circ\theta_{g}

in
\Hom(E,\rho_{gh}(E))
.

A
G
-invariant object is said to be
G
-linearisable
if it admits a
G
-equivariant structure.
A
G
-equivariant object

(E,\theta)
in
{\mathbf{C}}
is an object
E\in{\mathbf{C}}
with fixed equivariant structure
\theta=(\theta_{g})_{g\in G}
.

A
morphism between equivariant objects

(E_{1},\theta_{1})
and
(E_{2},\theta_{2})
is given by a morphism
f\colon E_{1}\to E_{2}
such that for all
g

\rho_{g}(f)\circ\theta_{1,g}=\theta_{2,g}\circ f

in
\Hom(E_{1},\rho_{g}(E_{2}))
.
Thus, one gets the category of
G
-equivariant objects in
{\mathbf{C}}
that we denote
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}
.

Definition 3.27
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{D}}
be
{\Bbbk}
-linear categories and
\tau\in\Aut({\Bbbk})
. We say that a functor
\phi\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is
\tau
-linear
if
\phi(\lambda f)=\tau(\lambda)\phi(f)
for any
\lambda\in{\Bbbk}
and a morphism
f
in
{\mathbf{C}}
.

Let
\sigma\colon G\to\Aut({\Bbbk})
be a homomorphism. We say that a
G
-action
(\rho_{g},\epsilon_{g,h})
on a
{\Bbbk}
-linear category
{\mathbf{C}}
is
\sigma
-linear
if
the functor
\rho_{g}\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is
\sigma(g)
-linear for any
g\in G
.

We will denote the multiplicative group
{\Bbbk}^{*}
with the induced
G
-action by
{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma}
:
g\in G
takes
\lambda\in{\Bbbk}^{*}
to
\sigma(g)(\lambda)
.

In this paper we will mostly be interested in categories with group actions as in the two examples below.

Example 3.28
.

Let
X
be an algebraic variety over
{\Bbbk}
with an action of a group
G
. Then the categories
\Coh X
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
have
G
-actions given by push-forward functors
\rho_{g}=g_{*}
so
that

g_{*}h_{*}=(gh)_{*}
and we set
\epsilon_{g,h}=\id
.
If a
G
-action is
{\Bbbk}
-linear then the induced
G
-actions on
\Coh X
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
are
{\Bbbk}
-linear. More generally, if one has
{\mathrm{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\Bbbk}
then a
G
-action on
X
defines a
G
-action
\sigma
on
{\Bbbk}
, and the induced
G
-actions on
\Coh X
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
are
\sigma
-linear.

For a
G
-morphism of
G
-varieties
f\colon X\to Y
the functors
f^{*},f_{*}
between
\Coh X,\Coh Y
and between
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X),{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
are
G
-equivariant.

Example 3.29
.

Let
{\Bbbk}
be a field and
\mathsf{pt}_{\Bbbk}
be the category with one object
\bullet
such that
\End(\bullet)={\Bbbk}
. Then an action
\rho
of a group
G
on
\mathsf{pt}_{\Bbbk}
(by additive functors) is given by

•

a homomorphism
\sigma\colon G\to\Aut({\Bbbk})
giving the action of
G
on morphisms:
\sigma(g)=\rho_{g}
, and

•

a collection
\alpha_{g,h}\in{\Bbbk}^{*}
giving isomorphisms
\epsilon_{g,h}
,

where
\alpha_{g,h}
are subject to relations (
3.13
):

\alpha_{f,gh}\cdot\rho_{f}(\alpha_{g,h})=\alpha_{fg,h}\cdot\alpha_{f,g}

for
f,g,h
. These relations mean exactly that
\alpha
is a
2
-cocycle of
G
in the
G
-module
{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma}
.
Vice versa, any homomorphism
\rho
together with a
2
-cocycle
\alpha
in
{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma}
define a
G
-action on
\mathsf{pt}_{\Bbbk}
as above.

Lemma 3.30
.

Two actions of
G
on
\mathsf{pt}_{\Bbbk}
given by
(\sigma,\alpha)
and
(\sigma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
are
G
-equivariantly equivalent if and only if for some
\tau\in\Aut({\Bbbk})
one has
\sigma^{\prime}=\tau\sigma\tau^{-1}
and
[\alpha^{\prime}]=\tau([\alpha])\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma^{\prime}})
(where
\tau\colon{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma}\to{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism of
G
-modules). In this case the equivalence is
\tau
-linear.

In particular, two such actions are
G
-equivariantly
{\Bbbk}
-linearly
equivalent if and only if
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
and
[\alpha]=[\alpha^{\prime}]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma})
.

Proof.

A
G
-equivariant equivalence
\phi\colon\mathsf{pt}_{\Bbbk}\to\mathsf{pt}_{\Bbbk}
is given by a functor
\phi
, which acts on morphisms by a field automorphism
\tau
, and a collection of isomorphisms
\beta_{g}\in\Aut(\bullet)={\Bbbk}^{*}
satisfying the compatibility condition (
3.14
):

\alpha^{\prime}_{g,h}\cdot\sigma(g)(\beta_{h})\cdot\beta_{g}=\beta_{gh}\cdot\tau(\alpha_{g,h}),

which means exactly that
\tau(\alpha)/\alpha^{\prime}
is the coboundary of
\beta
.
∎

Definition 3.31
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a
{\Bbbk}
-linear category with a
\sigma
-linear action of a group
G
and
E\in{\mathbf{C}}
be a
G
-invariant object with
\End(E)={\Bbbk}
. The
(twisting) cohomology class associated to
E
is the class
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,{\Bbbk}_{\sigma}^{*})
obtained in the following way.

The full subcategory
\tilde{\mathbf{E}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
of objects isomorphic to
E
is
G
-invariant and so the action on
{\mathbf{C}}
restricts to a
G
-action on
\tilde{\mathbf{E}}
.
The full subcategory
{\mathbf{E}}\subset\tilde{{\mathbf{E}}}
with one object
E
is equivalent to
\tilde{{\mathbf{E}}}
, and so the
G
-action on
\tilde{{\mathbf{E}}}
induces a
G
-action on
{\mathbf{E}}
(defined up to a
{\Bbbk}
-linear
G
-equivalence).
The category
{\mathbf{E}}
is the one from Example
3.29
, hence a cohomology class
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma})
is well-defined by Lemma
3.30
.

Alternatively, we can define the twisting class as follows. For any
g\in G
, fix an isomorphism
\theta_{g}\colon E\to\rho_{g}(E)
. Take
\alpha_{g_{1},g_{2}}\in{\Bbbk}^{*}
such that

(3.15)

\theta_{g_{1}g_{2}}\cdot\alpha_{g_{1},g_{2}}=\epsilon_{g_{1},g_{2}}\circ\rho_{g_{1}}(\theta_{g_{2}})\circ\theta_{g_{1}}.

One can check that
\alpha
is a
2
-cocycle and its cohomology class is as above.

Lemma 3.32
.

Under the assumptions of Definition
3.31
,
E
has a
G
-equivariant structure if and only if
[\alpha]=1
in
{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma})
.

Proof.

Changing the
\theta
-s by scalars, the
\alpha
-s change by a coboundary by (
3.15
). By definition,
E
has an equivariant structure if and only if one can choose
\theta
-s so that all
\alpha_{g_{1},g_{2}}=1
in (
3.15
), so the claim follows.
∎

Hence, the cohomology class
[\alpha]
is the obstruction for
E
to possess an equivariant structure.
If an invariant object fails to be equivariant, one can fix this by introducing

Definition 3.33
.

Let
G
be a group,
\sigma\colon G\to\Aut{\Bbbk}
be a homomorphism and
\alpha\in Z^{2}(G,{\Bbbk}_{\sigma}^{*})
be a
2
-cocycle.

(1)

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a
{\Bbbk}
-linear additive category with a
\sigma
-linear
G
-action. An
\alpha
-twisted
G
-equivariant object
is given by an object
E\in{\mathbf{C}}
and a collection
(\theta_{g})_{g\in G}
of isomorphisms
\theta_{g}\colon E\to\rho_{g}(E)
satisfying (
3.15
) for all
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
. Morphisms between
\alpha
-twisted
G
-equivariant objects are defined as in Definition
3.26
, so they form a category that we denote by
{\mathbf{C}}^{G,\alpha}
.

(2)

As a special case, let
X
is an algebraic variety over
{\Bbbk}
with a
\sigma
-linear
G
-action. Then the category
\Coh(X)
has a
\sigma
-linear
G
-action as in Example
3.28
. So
\alpha
-twisted
G
-equivariant coherent sheaves are defined, and we denote the category of these by
\Coh^{G,\alpha}(X)
.

(3)

By taking
X=\Spec{\Bbbk}
above, one defines
\alpha
-twisted representations of
G
over
{\Bbbk}
, and we will denote the category of these by
\Rep(G,\alpha)
.

One can see that the category
\Coh^{G,\alpha}X
depends, up to an equivalence, only on the cohomology class
[\alpha]
.
The following properties follow from the above definition, see also
[
28
, Prop. 1.2]
.

Lemma 3.34
.

Under the assumptions of Definition
3.33
(1), let
C\in{\mathbf{C}}^{G,\alpha_{1}}
and
V\in\Rep(G,\alpha_{2})
. Then

(1)

C\otimes_{{\Bbbk}}V\in{\mathbf{C}}^{G,\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}
.

Under the assumptions of Definition
3.33
(2), let
F_{1}\in\Coh^{G,\alpha_{1}}(X),F_{2}\in\Coh^{G,\alpha_{2}}(X)
. Then

(2)

F_{1}\otimes F_{2}\in\Coh^{G,\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}(X)
(this is a non-derived tensor product),

(3)

F_{1}^{\vee}\in\Coh^{G,\alpha_{1}^{-1}}(X)
.

Finally, let
X_{1},X_{2}
be as in Definition
3.33
(2) and
f\colon X_{1}\to X_{2}
be a
G
-equivariant morphism. Assume
F_{1}\in\Coh^{G,\alpha}(X_{1}),F_{2}\in\Coh^{G,\alpha}(X_{2})
. Then

(4)

f_{*}(F_{1})\in\Coh^{G,\alpha}(X_{2})
,

(5)

f^{*}(F_{2})\in\Coh^{G,\alpha}(X_{1})
.

Example 3.35
.

Let
n\geq 1
,
V
be an
n
-dimensional
{\Bbbk}
-vector space, and
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\Bbbk}}={\mathbb{P}}(V)
.
Let
\Aut({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\Bbbk}})
be the group of all (not only
{\Bbbk}
-linear) scheme automorphisms of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\Bbbk}}
. Let
\Aut^{\mathrm{slin}}({\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{{\Bbbk}})
be the group of semi-linear
(i.e., preserving linear functions) scheme automorphisms of
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{{\Bbbk}}
.
There is an exact sequence of groups

(3.16)

1\to{\Bbbk}^{*}\to\Aut^{\mathrm{slin}}({\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{{\Bbbk}})\to\Aut({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\Bbbk}})\to 1,

let
\xi\in Z^{2}(\Aut({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\Bbbk}}),{\Bbbk}^{*})
be a corresponding cocycle. There is a natural
\xi
-twisted representation of
\Aut({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\Bbbk}})
in
V
, the space of linear functions on
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{{\Bbbk}}
. Also one can see that for any
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(-k)
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\Bbbk}}
is
\xi^{k}
-twisted
\Aut({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\Bbbk}})
-equivariant. Indeed, for
k=-1
one remarks that the tautological line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(-1)
is an invariant subbundle in the constant bundle
{\mathcal{O}}\otimes V
and uses Lemma
3.34
(1), then for general
k
this follows by Lemma
3.34
(2,3).

Now let
G
be a group acting on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\Bbbk}}
. Restricting
\xi
to
G
along the homomorphism
G\to\Aut({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\Bbbk}})
, we get a cocycle
\alpha\in Z^{2}(G,{\Bbbk}^{*})
(note that
{\Bbbk}^{*}
carries a
G
-action given by the
G
-action on
{\mathrm{H}}^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{\Bbbk},{\mathcal{O}})={\Bbbk}
). The cocycle
\alpha
corresponds to the central extension

(3.17)

1\to{\Bbbk}^{*}\to\tilde{G}\to G\to 1

induced from (
3.16
). As above, the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(-k)
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\Bbbk}}
is
\alpha^{k}
-twisted
G
-equivariant for any
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
The cohomology class
[\alpha]
vanishes if and only if (
3.17
) splits (hence the action of
G
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
factors through
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
) and if and only if the line bundles
{\mathcal{O}}(k)
are all (untwisted)
G
-equivariant.

We finally remark that
[\xi^{n}]=[\alpha^{n}]=1
, for example, because the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}}(-n)\cong\omega_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}}
is always equivariant.

Now let us consider an example more general than Example
3.29
.

Example 3.36
.

Let
A
be a commutative
{\Bbbk}
-algebra, and
A^{*}
be the group of its invertible elements. Let

\mathsf{pt}_{A}
be the category with one object
\bullet
such that
\End(\bullet)=A
.
As in Example
3.29
, a
G
-action on
\mathsf{pt}_{A}
is the same as a homomorphism
\rho
from
G
to
\Aut(A)
(the group of ring automorphisms of
A
) and a cocycle
\alpha=(\alpha_{g,h})\in Z^{2}(G,A^{*}_{\rho})
. Indeed, let us check that the map
g\mapsto\rho_{g}\in\Aut(A)
, provided by a
G
-action on
\mathsf{pt}_{A}
, is a homomorphism. By definition
3.18
, for
g,h\in G
and
a\in\End(\bullet)
one has
\alpha_{g,h}\cdot\rho_{g}(\rho_{h}(a))=\rho_{gh}(a)\cdot\alpha_{g,h}\in A
, and by commutativity of
A
one gets

\rho_{g}(\rho_{h}(a))=\rho_{gh}(a)
.

Lemma 3.37
.

Two actions of
G
on
\mathsf{pt}_{A}
given by
(\rho,\alpha)
and
(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
are
G
-equivariantly equivalent if and only if for some
\tau\in\Aut(A)
one has
\rho^{\prime}=\tau\rho\tau^{-1}
and
[\alpha^{\prime}]=\tau([\alpha])\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,A^{*}_{\rho^{\prime}})
(where
\tau\colon A^{*}_{\rho}\to A^{*}_{\rho^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism of
G
-modules).

In particular, two such actions are
G
-equivariantly
{\Bbbk}
-linearly
equivalent if and only if
the above is satisfied for some
{\Bbbk}
-linear
automorphism
\tau\in\Aut_{{\Bbbk}}(A)
.

Proof.

The same as for Lemma
3.30
.
∎

We will be interested in the special case when
A={\Bbbk}^{n}={\Bbbk}\times\ldots\times{\Bbbk}
. We fix a homomorphism
\sigma\colon G\to\Aut({\Bbbk})
and consider only
\sigma
-linear
G
-actions on
\mathsf{pt}_{A}
.

Lemma 3.38
.

Let
A={\Bbbk}^{n}
with idempotents
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
.
A
\sigma
-linear action of
G
on
\mathsf{pt}_{A}
is given by
a homomorphism
\rho\colon G\to S_{n}
to the symmetric group and a
2
-cocycle of
G
in
({\Bbbk}^{*})_{\rho,\sigma}^{n}
, where the

G
-action on
({\Bbbk}^{*})_{\rho,\sigma}^{n}
is the following:
g(\lambda\cdot e_{i})=\sigma(g)(\lambda)\cdot e_{\rho(i)}
.
Two such actions given by

(\rho,\alpha)
and
(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
are
G
-equivariantly
{\Bbbk}
-linearly equivalent if and only if for some
\tau\in S_{n}
one has
\rho^{\prime}=\tau\rho\tau^{-1}
and
[\alpha^{\prime}]=\tau([\alpha])\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n}_{\rho^{\prime},\sigma})
.

Proof.

Follows from Lemma
3.37
. Indeed, a
\sigma(g)
-linear automorphism
\rho_{g}
of
{\Bbbk}^{n}
is uniquely determined by its action on the idempotents
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
, hence an element in
S_{n}
that we will also denote
\rho_{g}
. One gets a homomorphism
\rho\colon G\to S_{n}
as the composition
G\to\Aut({\Bbbk}^{n})\to S_{n}
. For the second statement note that a
{\Bbbk}
-linear automorphism of
{\Bbbk}^{n}
is given by a permutation
\tau\in S_{n}
.
∎

The following definition generalises Definition
3.31
.

Definition 3.39
.

Suppose that
{\mathbf{C}}
is a
{\Bbbk}
-linear category with a
\sigma
-linear action of a group
G
. Assume there are objects
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\in{\mathbf{C}}
such that

(3.18)

\Hom(E_{i},E_{i})={\Bbbk},\quad\Hom(E_{i},E_{j})=0\quad\text{for $i\neq j$},

and
G
permutes
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
: for all
g\in G
and
i
one has
\rho_{g}(E_{i})\cong E_{j}
for some
j
.
Take
E=E_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus E_{n}
, then
E
is
G
-invariant and
\End(E)={\Bbbk}^{n}
. Arguing as in Definition
3.31
, we get a
G
-module structure
({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n}_{\rho,\sigma}
on
({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n}=\Aut(E)
(where
\rho\colon G\to S_{n}
is the
G
-action on objects
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
) and a
twisting cohomology class

[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n}_{\rho,\sigma})
, associated to

\{E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\}
.

Below we explain that the class
[\alpha]
determines the action on the subcategory in
{\mathbf{C}}
generated by
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
uniquely up to an equivalence.
For our convenience, we restrict to the more special case of triangulated categories.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}
be a triangulated
{\Bbbk}
-linear category with a
\sigma
-linear action of a group
G
(for some homomorphism
\sigma\colon G\to\Aut({\Bbbk})
). We will use the following terminology.

Definition 3.40
.

A weakly exceptional block of objects
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
in
{\mathbf{T}}
is called a
G
-block
if
G
permutes
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
: for any
g\in G
and
i
one has
\rho_{g}(E_{i})\cong E_{j}
for some
j
. A
G
-block is
transitive
if the action of
G
on
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
is transitive: for any
i,j
there is
g\in G
such that
\rho_{g}(E_{i})\cong E_{j}
.

Assume
{\mathbf{T}}
is generated by a
G
-transitive exceptional block
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
. A twisting cohomology class
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n}_{\rho,\sigma})
is introduced in Definition
3.39
, where the homomorphism
\rho\colon G\to S_{n}
gives the
G
-action on the objects
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
.

Proposition 3.41
.

A triangulated
{\Bbbk}
-linear category with a
\sigma
-linear
G
-action and generated by an exceptional
G
-block is determined by a transitive
G
-action on
\{1,\ldots,n\}
given by a homomorphism
\rho\colon G\to S_{n}
and a cohomology class
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,({\Bbbk}^{*})_{\rho,\sigma}^{n})
. More precisely, let
{\mathbf{T}}_{1},{\mathbf{T}}_{2}
be two triangulated
{\Bbbk}
-linear categories with
\sigma
-linear
G
-actions and generated by exceptional
G
-blocks. For
i=1,2
, let
\rho_{i}\colon G\to S_{n_{i}}
be the corresponding homomorphisms and
[\alpha_{i}]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,({\Bbbk}^{*})_{\rho_{i},\sigma}^{n_{i}})
be the corresponding cohomology classes. Then
{\mathbf{T}}_{1}
is
{\Bbbk}
-linearly
G
-equivariantly exact equivalent to
{\mathbf{T}}_{2}
if and only if

(3.19)

n_{1}=n_{2},\quad
for some
\tau\in S_{n_{1}}
one has

\rho_{2}=\tau\rho_{1}\tau^{-1},\quad
and
[\alpha_{2}]=\tau([\alpha_{1}])\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n}_{\rho_{2},\sigma})
.

Proof.

First assume that
\phi\colon{\mathbf{T}}_{1}\to{\mathbf{T}}_{2}
is a
{\Bbbk}
-linear
G
-equivariant exact equivalence.
For
i=1,2
let

E_{i}
be the direct sum of objects in the block,

{\mathbf{E}}_{i}\subset{\mathbf{T}}_{i}
be the full subcategory with one object
E_{i}
, and

\tilde{{\mathbf{E}}}_{i}\subset{\mathbf{T}}_{i}
be the full subcategory with objects isomorphic to
E_{i}
.

Changing
\phi
by a shift if necessary one can assume that
\phi(E_{1})
is in
\tilde{{\mathbf{E}}}_{2}
. It follows then that
\phi
restricts to a
{\Bbbk}
-linear
G
-equivariant equivalence between
\tilde{{\mathbf{E}}}_{1}
and
\tilde{{\mathbf{E}}}_{2}
. Then the induced
G
-actions on
{\mathbf{E}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{E}}_{2}
are

{\Bbbk}
-linearly equivalent. By Lemma
3.38
, (
3.19
) follows.

Now assume that (
3.19
) holds. By Lemma
3.38
it follows that
{\mathbf{E}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{E}}_{2}
are
G
-equivariantly
{\Bbbk}
-linearly equivalent, let
\phi\colon{\mathbf{E}}_{1}\to{\mathbf{E}}_{2}
be such equivalence.
For
i=1,2
let

\hat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{i}\subset{\mathbf{T}}_{i}
be the full subcategory with objects isomorphic to finite direct sums of shifts of
E_{i}
.

For any
F\in\hat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{i}
fix an isomorphism
F\cong\oplus_{j}E_{1}[d_{j}]
and define

\phi(F)
to be
\oplus_{j}\phi(E_{1})[d_{i}]
.
We leave it to the reader to define
\phi
on morphisms and to deduce an equivariant equivalence

\hat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{1}\to\hat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{2}
. Now remark that
{\mathbf{T}}_{i}
is an idempotent closure of
\hat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{i}
and extend
\phi
to an equivariant equivalence between
{\mathbf{T}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{T}}_{2}
.
∎

There is a different way to introduce a twisting cohomology class associated to a
G
-block.
As before, assume that objects
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
of a
{\Bbbk}
-linear category
{\mathbf{C}}
form one orbit under
\sigma
-linear
G
-action on
{\mathbf{C}}
and satisfy (
3.18
). One can pick an object, e.g.
E_{1}
, and consider its stabilising subgroup
H\subset G
, that is,
H=\{h\in G\mid\rho_{h}(E_{1})\cong E_{1}\}
. Then
E_{1}
is
H
-invariant, and there is the associated cohomology class

[\alpha_{H}]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H,{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma}).

This class
[\alpha_{H}]
determines the action on the subcategory generated by
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
up to an equivariant equivalence. Indeed, the
G
-module
({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n}_{\rho,\sigma}
is induced from the
H
-module
{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma}
, and by Shapiro’s Lemma one has

{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n}_{\rho,\sigma})\cong{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H,{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma}).

Therefore
[\alpha_{H}]
determines
[\alpha]
uniquely, and the latter determines the action. We obtain the following

Corollary 3.42
.

A triangulated
{\Bbbk}
-linear category with a
\sigma
-linear
G
-action and generated by a transitive exceptional
G
-block is determined by a subgroup of finite index
H\subset G
and a cohomology class
[\alpha_{H}]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H,{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma})
. In particular, the twisting class
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n}_{\rho,\sigma})
associated with the block is trivial if and only if the twisting class
[\alpha_{H}]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H,{\Bbbk}^{*}_{\sigma})
associated with one object is trivial.

3.5.
Galois descent for semi-orthogonal decompositions

Below we recall how to do Galois descent for semi-orthogonal decompositions, specifically for exceptional blocks. Such results are not new, see e.g.
[
30
]
,
[
1
]
,
[
3
]
but we find it useful to give definitions and some proofs for three reasons: for completeness, to take care of the case of infinite field extension, and to provide a treatment as elementary as possible.

Let
{\Bbbk}
be a field, and let
{\mathbb{L}}/{\Bbbk}
be a Galois extension with
Galois group
G=\Gal({\mathbb{L}}/{\Bbbk})
. Let
X
be a variety over
{\Bbbk}
and
X_{{\mathbb{L}}}=X\times_{{\Bbbk}}\Spec{\mathbb{L}}
be the scalar extension. Denote by
p\colon X_{{\mathbb{L}}}\to X
the natural morphism. For a thick subcategory
{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
define
p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
as the thick subcategory generated by the objects of the form
p^{*}A,A\in{\mathbf{A}}
.
For a thick subcategory
{\mathbf{B}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
define
p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
as the full subcategory whose objects are such
A\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
that
p^{*}A\in{\mathbf{B}}
.

Lemma 3.43
.

In the above assumptions, the correspondence
{\mathbf{A}}\mapsto p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
,
{\mathbf{B}}\mapsto p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
gives a bijection between thick subcategories in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and
G
-invariant thick subcategories in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
.

Proof.

First assume that
{\mathbb{L}}/{\Bbbk}
is finite. Note two functorial isomorphisms:

(3.20)

\displaystyle p_{*}p^{*}(-)

\displaystyle\cong(-)\otimes_{\Bbbk}{\mathbb{L}}\cong(-)^{\oplus|G|},

(3.21)

\displaystyle p^{*}p_{*}(-)

\displaystyle\cong\oplus_{g\in G}\rho_{g}(-),

where the first one is the projection formula and the second one is
[
3
, Lemma 2.13]
.
We define, for a
G
-invariant thick subcategory
{\mathbf{B}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
, the subcategory
{\tilde{p}}_{*}{\mathbf{B}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
as the thick subcategory generated by the object
p_{*}B
,
B\in{\mathbf{B}}
(this does not work for infinite field extensions because
p_{*}
does not preserve coherent sheaves).

We claim that
{\tilde{p}}_{*}{\mathbf{B}}=p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
. For
{\tilde{p}}_{*}{\mathbf{B}}\subset p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
: note that any generator
p_{*}B
of
{\tilde{p}}_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
is in
p^{*}{\mathbf{B}}
because
p^{*}p_{*}B=\oplus_{g}\rho_{g}(B)\in{\mathbf{B}}
by (
3.21
) (as
{\mathbf{B}}
is
G
-invariant). For
p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}\subset{\tilde{p}}_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
: assume
A\in p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
, then
p^{*}A\in{\mathbf{B}}
and
p_{*}p^{*}A\in{\tilde{p}}_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
, hence
A\in{\tilde{p}}_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
as a direct summand in
p_{*}p^{*}A
by (
3.20
).

We check that
{\mathbf{A}}=p_{*}p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
. Inclusion
{\mathbf{A}}\subset p_{*}p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
is evident. For inclusion
p_{*}p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{A}}
:
p_{*}p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}={\tilde{p}}_{*}p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
is generated by objects
p_{*}p^{*}A\cong A\otimes L
which belong to
{\mathbf{A}}
.

Next we check
{\mathbf{B}}=p^{*}p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
. For
{\mathbf{B}}\subset p^{*}p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
: note that any
B
is a direct summand in
\oplus_{g}\rho_{g}(B)\cong p^{*}p_{*}B
which is in
p^{*}{\tilde{p}}_{*}{\mathbf{B}}=p^{*}p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
. Inclusion
p^{*}p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}\subset{\mathbf{B}}
is obvious.

Now we consider the general case and do not assume that
{\mathbb{L}}/{\Bbbk}
is finite.
Not surprisingly, everything will follow from the fact that any object/morphism in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
is defined over some subfield
{\mathbb{F}}\subset{\mathbb{L}}
such that
{\mathbb{F}}/{\Bbbk}
is Galois and finite.
Inclusions
{\mathbf{A}}\subset p_{*}p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
and
p^{*}p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}\subset{\mathbf{B}}
follow from definitions as before. For
p_{*}p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{A}}
: assume
A\in p_{*}p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
, then
p^{*}A\in p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
. By definition, this means that there exists a generation diagram for
p^{*}A
:

(3.22)

0=B_{0}\xrightarrow{b_{1}}B_{1}\xrightarrow{b_{2}}B_{2}\to\ldots\xrightarrow{b_{n}}B_{n}\begin{smallmatrix}\xrightarrow{c}\\
\xleftarrow{d}\end{smallmatrix}p^{*}A,

where
Cone(b_{i})=p^{*}A_{i}
for
A_{i}\in{\mathbf{A}}
and
cd=\id_{p^{*}A}
.
One can find a finite Galois extension
{\mathbb{F}}/{\Bbbk}
such that (
3.22
) is defined over
{\mathbb{F}}
: that is, (
3.22
) is isomorphic to the pullback of the similar generating diagram for
p_{{\mathbb{F}}}^{*}A
in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{F}}})
(where
p_{{\mathbb{F}}}\colon X_{{\mathbb{F}}}\to X
is the natural morphism). Then
p_{{\mathbb{F}}}^{*}A
is in
p_{{\mathbb{F}}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
and therefore
A
is in
(p_{{\mathbb{F}}})_{*}p_{{\mathbb{F}}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
, which is
{\mathbf{A}}
by the above considerations of the finite case.

Now we check that
{\mathbf{B}}\subset p^{*}p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
. Let
B\in{\mathbf{B}}
. One can find a finite Galois extension
{\mathbb{F}}/{\Bbbk}
such that
B
is defined over
{\mathbb{F}}
. Let
p_{{\mathbb{F}}}\colon X_{{\mathbb{F}}}\to X
and
q\colon X_{{\mathbb{L}}}\to X_{{\mathbb{F}}}
be the natural morphisms, then
B=q^{*}B^{\prime}
for some
B^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{F}}})
. Let
B^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,B^{\prime}_{n}
be the
\Gal({\mathbb{F}}/{\Bbbk})
-orbit of
B^{\prime}
, then
q^{*}B^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,q^{*}B^{\prime}_{n}
is the
G
-orbit of
B
, hence
q^{*}B^{\prime}_{i}\in{\mathbf{B}}
for all
i
. One has

p^{*}(p_{{\mathbb{F}}})_{*}B^{\prime}\cong q^{*}p_{{\mathbb{F}}}^{*}(p_{{\mathbb{F}}})_{*}B^{\prime}\cong\oplus_{i}q^{*}B^{\prime}_{i}\in{\mathbf{B}}

by (
3.21
). It follows that
(p_{{\mathbb{F}}})_{*}B^{\prime}\in p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
(by definition) and

\oplus_{i}q^{*}B^{\prime}_{i}\cong p^{*}(p_{{\mathbb{F}}})_{*}B^{\prime}\in p^{*}p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
. Therefore
B\cong q^{*}B^{\prime}\in p^{*}p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}
as a direct summand, and we are done.
∎

Remark 3.44
.

One can prove that the subcategory
p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
is in fact the closure under direct summands of the subcategory with objects
p^{*}A
,
A\in{\mathbf{A}}
(no adding of cones is needed), see the proof of Proposition
3.46
below.

Proposition 3.45
.

In the above assumptions there is a bijection between SOD-s of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and
G
-invariant SOD-s of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
which takes
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle
to

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})=\langle p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle
, and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{n}\rangle
to

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{n}\rangle
.

Proof.

Follows from Lemma
3.43
and two easy observations: two objects
F_{1},F_{2}
in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
are semi-orthogonal if and only if
p^{*}F_{1},p^{*}F_{2}
are semi-orthogonal in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
; an object
F
generates
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
if and only if
p^{*}F
generates
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
.
∎

One would like to relate subcategories of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and
G
-invariant subcategories of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
as abstract triangulated categories.
We restrict ourselves to the case of finite Galois extensions.

Recall that, for a category
{\mathbf{B}}
with a
G
-action we denote by
{\mathbf{B}}^{G}
the category of
G
-equivariant objects in
{\mathbf{B}}
, see Definition
3.26
.

For a
{\Bbbk}
-linear idempotent complete category
{\mathbf{A}}
and a field extension
{\Bbbk}\subset{\mathbb{L}}
, one can define the scalar extension
{\mathbf{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
as the idempotent completion of the following category
\tilde{{\mathbf{A}}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
: objects are the same as in
{\mathbf{A}}
, and
\Hom
-spaces are
\Hom_{{\mathbf{A}}}(-,-)\otimes_{\Bbbk}{\mathbb{L}}
, see e.g.
[
33
, §5.1]
. Note that
\tilde{{\mathbf{A}}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
and
{{\mathbf{A}}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
carry natural
\Gal({\mathbb{L}}/{\Bbbk})
-actions.

Proposition 3.46

(See
[
33
, §8.3]
)
.

Let
{\Bbbk}\subset{\mathbb{L}}
be a finite Galois extension,
G=\Gal({\mathbb{L}}/{\Bbbk})
,
X
be a variety over
{\Bbbk}
and
X_{{\mathbb{L}}}=X\times_{\Bbbk}\Spec{\mathbb{L}}\xrightarrow{p}X
be a scalar extension. Let
{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
be a thick subcategory and
{\mathbf{B}}=p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
be the corresponding
G
-invariant thick subcategory. Then
{\mathbf{A}}
is equivalent to
{\mathbf{B}}^{G}
, and
{\mathbf{B}}
is equivalent to
{\mathbf{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
.

Proof.

For the first statement we recall that
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is equivalent to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})^{G}

(e.g. by
[
29
, Th. 7.3]
applied to the morphism
p\colon X_{{\mathbb{L}}}\to X
).
Consider the commutative diagram, where
u
is the forgetful functor:

\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})^{G}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}

Now, the equivalence
{\mathbf{A}}\cong{\mathbf{B}}^{G}
follows from diagram chase:

{\mathbf{A}}=(p^{*})^{-1}({\mathbf{B}})=\phi^{-1}u^{-1}({\mathbf{B}})=\phi^{-1}({\mathbf{B}}^{G})
.

For the second statement, note that
\tilde{{\mathbf{A}}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
is equivalent to the full subcategory
{\tilde{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
with objects
p^{*}A
,
A\in{\mathbf{A}}
. So we are to check that
p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
is obtained from
{\tilde{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
by adding direct summands only. Let
\overline{{\tilde{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}}
be the closure of
{\tilde{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
under direct summands. We need to check that
\overline{{\tilde{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}}
is closed under taking shifts (obvious) and cones.

Let
B_{1},B_{2}\in\overline{{\tilde{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}}
and
f\colon B_{1}\to B_{2}
. Then there are
B^{\prime}_{1},B^{\prime}_{2}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
,
A_{1},A_{2}\in{\mathbf{A}}
such that
p^{*}A_{i}\cong B_{i}\oplus B^{\prime}_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Let
{\overline{f}}=(\begin{smallmatrix}f&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix})\colon p^{*}A_{1}\to p^{*}A_{2}
be the morphism, then
Cone(f)
is a direct summand in
Cone({\overline{f}})
. It follows from (
3.21
) that
{\overline{f}}
is a direct summand in
p^{*}p_{*}{\overline{f}}
and hence
Cone({\overline{f}})
is a direct summand in
Cone(p^{*}p_{*}{\overline{f}})\cong p^{*}Cone(p_{*}{\overline{f}})
. Observe that
p_{*}{\overline{f}}
is a morphism
p_{*}p^{*}A_{1}\to p_{*}p^{*}A_{2}
and by (
3.20
) we have
p_{*}p^{*}A_{i}\in{\mathbf{A}}
for
i=1,2
, so that
Cone(p_{*}{\overline{f}})\in{\mathbf{A}}
as well. Hence
Cone(p^{*}p_{*}{\overline{f}})\in{\tilde{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
and therefore
Cone(f)\in\overline{{\tilde{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}}
. We conclude that
\overline{{\tilde{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}}
is thick and so
\overline{{\tilde{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{A}}}=p^{*}{\mathbf{A}}
as needed.
∎

Next we specialise to the case of subcategories generated by exceptional blocks, and discuss descent for these in more details. Good references on this subject include
[
1
, Section 2]
and
[
3
, Section 2.2]
.

Recall Definitions
3.4
and
3.5
of
G
-blocks and
G
-transitive blocks of (weakly) exceptional objects.

Proposition 3.47
.

Take the assumptions of Lemma
3.43
(field extension
{\mathbb{L}}/{\Bbbk}
can be infinite).

(1)

{\mathbf{A}}
is generated by a block of weakly exceptional objects if and only if
{\mathbf{B}}
is generated by a
G
-block of weakly exceptional objects. The block generating
{\mathbf{A}}
has one object if and only if the block generating
{\mathbf{B}}
is
G
-transitive.

Assume additionally that a group
H
acts on
X
by
{\Bbbk}
-linear automorphisms, so that
H\times G
acts on
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
.

(2)

{\mathbf{A}}
is generated by an
H
-block of weakly exceptional objects if and only if
{\mathbf{B}}
is generated by an
H\times G
-block of weakly exceptional objects. The block generating
{\mathbf{A}}
is
H
-transitive if and only if the block generating
{\mathbf{B}}
is
H\times G
-transitive.

Proof.

For the purpose of the proof, let us call an object
E
of
{\Bbbk}
-linear triangulated category
semi-exceptional
if
\Hom^{i}(E,E)=0
for
i\neq 0
and
\End(E)
is a semi-simple finite-dimensional
{\Bbbk}
-algebra. One can see that (provided that the category is idempotent complete, which is always true in our examples) an object is semi-exceptional if and only if it has the form
E_{1}^{d_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus E_{n}^{d_{n}}
for some block
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
of weakly exceptional objects.

With this terminology, the first statement in (1) is equivalent to the following: an object
E
in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is semi-exceptional if and only if
p^{*}E\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
is semi-exceptional. This, in turn, follows from the fact that a
{\Bbbk}
-algebra
A
is semi-simple if and only if
A\otimes_{{\Bbbk}}{\mathbb{L}}
is semi-simple. We leave the details to the reader. Then the second statement in (1) is due to the fact that
{\mathbf{A}}
has non-trivial thick subcategories if and only if
{\mathbf{B}}
has non-trivial thick
G
-invariant subcategories, see Lemma
3.43
.

Item (2) follows easily:
{\mathbf{A}}
is
H
-invariant if and only if
{\mathbf{B}}
is
H
-invariant for the first part, and the second part is proved as in part (1).
∎

Lemma 3.48
.

Take the assumptions as in Lemma
3.43
and suppose that
{\mathbb{L}}/{\Bbbk}
is finite.
Suppose
{\mathbf{A}}
is generated by a
D
-exceptional object
E
for a division
{\Bbbk}
-algebra
D
, and

p^{*}E\cong F^{d}
for some
{\mathbb{L}}
-exceptional object
F\in{\mathbf{B}}
with
d\geq 1
(so that
F
is
G
-invariant and generates
{\mathbf{B}}
).
Let
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,{\mathbb{L}}^{*})
be the class associated with
F
(by Definition
3.31
).
Then
[\alpha]
corresponds to the class
[D]
of the central simple algebra
D
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.46
we have that
\langle F\rangle^{G}\cong\langle E\rangle
. Consider the composition of equivalences

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\Rep_{{\mathbb{L}}}(G,\alpha^{-1}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\langle F\rangle^{G}\xrightarrow{\sim}\langle E\rangle\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\modd D)\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(D^{\mathrm{op}}\mmod),

where the first functor is given by
V\mapsto F\otimes V
, see Lemma
3.34
and
[
28
, Lemma 2.10]
.
Abelian category
\Rep_{{\mathbb{L}}}(G,\alpha^{-1})
of twisted representations is
equivalent to the module category of the skew product
{\mathbb{L}}\#_{\alpha^{-1}}G
defined as follows.
As a vector space
{\mathbb{L}}\#_{\alpha^{-1}}G={\mathbb{L}}\otimes_{{\Bbbk}}{\Bbbk}[G]
and the multiplication is given by

(\lambda\otimes g)(\mu\otimes h)=(\lambda\cdot g(\mu)\cdot\alpha^{-1}_{g,h}\otimes gh).

Then algebra
{\mathbb{L}}\#_{\alpha^{-1}}G
is central over
{\Bbbk}
and simple, and its Brauer class corresponds to
[\alpha^{-1}]
under the isomorphism
\Br({\mathbb{L}}/{\Bbbk})\cong{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,{\mathbb{L}}^{*})
(see
[
40
, Ch. 8.4]
). By definitions, left
{\mathbb{L}}\#_{\alpha^{-1}}G
-modules are
\alpha^{-1}
-twisted representations of
G
over
{\mathbb{L}}
. Hence we get an equivalence

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(({\mathbb{L}}\#_{\alpha^{-1}}G)\mmod)\cong{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(D^{\mathrm{op}}\mmod),

and therefore
{\mathbb{L}}\#_{\alpha^{-1}}G
is Morita-equivalent to
D^{\mathrm{op}}
. Consequently,
[\alpha^{-1}]=[D^{\mathrm{op}}]
, and
[\alpha]=[D]
as needed.
∎

Assume now that the field
{\Bbbk}
is perfect.
Triangulated
{\Bbbk}
-linear categories generated by a weakly exceptional object
E
are determined by the division algebra
\End(E)
. According to Corollary
3.42
, triangulated
\bar{\Bbbk}
-linear categories with twisted
G=\Gal(\bar{\Bbbk}/{\Bbbk})
-action and generated by a
G
-transitive exceptional block are classified by a subgroup
H\subset G
of finite index and a cohomology class in
{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H,\bar{\Bbbk}^{*})
. In view of Proposition
3.47
(2), these two descriptions are related:

Proposition 3.49
.

For a perfect field
{\Bbbk}
and a variety
X
over
{\Bbbk}
, let
G=\Gal(\bar{\Bbbk}/{\Bbbk})
, and
p\colon X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}\to X
be the natural morphism. Let
E\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
be a
D
-exceptional object, where
D
is a division
{\Bbbk}
-algebra.
Let
{\mathbb{F}}\subset D
be the centre of
D
,
n=[{\mathbb{F}}:{\Bbbk}]
, and
d^{2}=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}D
. Then

p^{*}E\cong E_{1}^{d}\oplus\ldots\oplus E_{n}^{d},

where
(E_{1},\ldots,E_{n})
is a
G
-transitive exceptional block in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{\bar{\Bbbk}})
.
Moreover, let
H\subset G
be the stabiliser of
E_{1}
and
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H,\bar{\Bbbk}^{*})
be the class associated with the
H
-action on
E_{1}
(by Definition
3.31
). Then
{\mathbb{F}}\cong\bar{\Bbbk}^{H}
and
[\alpha]
corresponds to the class in
\Br({\mathbb{F}})
of the central simple
{\mathbb{F}}
-algebra
D
.

Proof.

By the proof of Proposition
3.47
,
p^{*}E
is a semi-exceptional object:

p^{*}E\cong E_{1}^{d_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus E_{n^{\prime}}^{d_{n^{\prime}}}
for some
G
-transitive exceptional block
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n^{\prime}}
on
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
.
One has
d_{1}=\ldots=d_{n^{\prime}}=:d^{\prime}
because
p^{*}E
is
G
-invariant. Computing the centre in

D\otimes_{{\Bbbk}}\bar{\Bbbk}\cong\End(p^{*}E)\cong M_{d^{\prime}}(\bar{\Bbbk})^{\times n^{\prime}}

one sees that
n=n^{\prime}
, and by dimension count one gets
d=d^{\prime}
.

Let
{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}=(\bar{\Bbbk})^{H}
be the invariant subfield so that
H=\Gal(\bar{\Bbbk}/{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})
and
[{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}:{\Bbbk}]=n
. Decompose

p
as
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}\xrightarrow{q}X_{{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{p_{{\mathbb{F}}}}X
. By Proposition
3.47
, the
H
-transitive block
E_{1}
corresponds to a weakly exceptional object
E^{\prime}
on
X_{{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}}
. Moreover, for some
r
one has
q^{*}(E^{{}^{\prime}r})\cong E_{1}^{d}
.
Next we compute

D=(D\otimes_{{\Bbbk}}\bar{\Bbbk})^{G}\cong(\End(E_{1}^{d})\times\ldots\times\End(E_{n}^{d}))^{G}=\End(E_{1}^{d})^{H}=(\End(E^{{}^{\prime}r})\otimes_{{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}}\bar{\Bbbk})^{H}=\End(E^{{}^{\prime}r})\cong M_{r}(\End(E^{\prime})).

It follows that
r=1
and
\End(E^{\prime})\cong D
. Note that
{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}\subset Z(\End(E^{\prime}))
so
{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}
embeds into
Z(D)={\mathbb{F}}
.
Recall that
\deg_{{\Bbbk}}{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}=\deg_{{\Bbbk}}{\mathbb{F}}=n
, hence
{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}\cong{\mathbb{F}}
.

We see that the field extension from
{\Bbbk}
to
{\mathbb{F}}=Z(\End(E))
splits from
E
a weakly exceptional direct summand
E^{\prime}
with multiplicity
1
and such that
\End(E^{\prime})\cong\End(E)
.

Now we explain the last statement about
[\alpha]
. One can choose a finite Galois extension
{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{L}}
such that
E_{1}
is defined over
{\mathbb{L}}
, and let
E^{\prime}_{1}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
be the corresponding
{\mathbb{L}}
-exceptional object. Let
H^{\prime}=\Gal({\mathbb{L}}/{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime})
, then
E^{\prime}_{1}
is
H^{\prime}
-invariant, moreover, the associated class
[\alpha^{\prime}]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H^{\prime},{\mathbb{L}}^{*})
goes to
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H,\bar{\Bbbk}^{*})
under the epimorphism
H\to H^{\prime}
.
Now the claim follows from Lemma
3.48
which we apply to the field extension
{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{L}}
and the objects
E^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{F}}^{\prime}}),E^{\prime}_{1}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})
.
∎

Example 3.50
.

Let
X
be a Severi–Brauer variety over
{\Bbbk}
, that is, a twisted form of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
for some
n\geq 2
. Let
A
be the corresponding central simple algebra over
{\Bbbk}
of degree
n
and
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,\bar{\Bbbk}^{*})
the cohomology class corresponding to the Brauer class
[A]
.
Then the twisting class associated with
{\mathcal{O}}(-1)
(see Example
3.35
) coincides with
[\alpha]
, see e.g.
[
10
, Cor. 4.7]
.
The
G
-invariant subcategory
\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-1)\rangle\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{\bar{\Bbbk}})
descends to a subcategory
{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
, generated by a
D
-exceptional object
E
, where
D
is a division algebra representing the class
[A]
(see Proposition
3.49
). Note that the index
r=\ind[A]=\deg D
divides
n=\deg A
and that
E
is a vector bundle of rank
r
. In the notation of Proposition
3.49
we have
{\mathbb{F}}={\Bbbk}
,
n=1
,
d=r
, and
p^{*}E\cong{\mathcal{O}}(-1)^{r}
.

4.
Atomic theories

4.1.
Basics

To construct an atomic theory means to specify some SOD-s (called atomic) of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
for each variety
X
in a certain class. This class should satisfy some assumptions that we discuss below.

Definition 4.1
.

By an
atomic domain
over a field
{\Bbbk}
we will mean a subcategory (usually not full) in the category
\cVar_{{\Bbbk}}
of varieties over
{\Bbbk}
such that all morphisms in the subcategory are derived contractions (see Definition
2.15
).

If
G
is a group, a
G
-atomic domain
over
{\Bbbk}
is a subcategory in the category
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk}}
of varieties over
{\Bbbk}
with a
G
-action and
G
-equivariant morphisms such that all morphisms in the subcategory are derived contractions.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be an atomic domain over
{\Bbbk}
.
An
atomic theory
for
{\mathcal{V}}
assigns, to any variety
X
in
{\mathcal{V}}
,
a class of semi-orthogonal decompositions of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
, called
atomic decompositions
, such that

(A1)

atomic decompositions form one orbit under mutations,

(A2)

for
X,Y
in
{\mathcal{V}}
and a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
{\mathcal{V}}
, there are atomic decompositions

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n},{\mathbf{A}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{m}\rangle,\quad{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle,

such that
f^{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{j}={\mathbf{A}}_{j}
(are equal as subcategories) for
j=n+1,\ldots,m
.

More generally, we define the equivariant version of atomic theories. We do not assume that group actions are
{\Bbbk}
-linear.
For a group
G
and a
G
-variety
X
, there is a natural
G
-action on the categories
\Coh(X)
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
given by

\rho_{g}(-)=g_{*}(-)

(where
\epsilon_{g,h}
are canonical isomorphisms). We recall that a subcategory
{\mathbf{T}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is
G
-invariant
if
\rho_{g}({\mathbf{T}})={\mathbf{T}}
for all
g\in G
.
An SOD of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is
G
-invariant
if it consists of
G
-invariant subcategories.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
G
be a group and
{\mathcal{V}}
be a
G
-atomic domain over
{\Bbbk}
.
A
G
-equivariant atomic theory
for
{\mathcal{V}}
assigns, to any
G
-variety
X
in
{\mathcal{V}}
,
a class of
G
-invariant semi-orthogonal decompositions of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
, called
atomic decompositions
, such that

(A1)

atomic decompositions form one orbit under mutations,

(A2)

for any
X,Y
in
{\mathcal{V}}
and any
G
-equivariant morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
{\mathcal{V}}
, there exist atomic decompositions

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n},{\mathbf{A}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{m}\rangle,\quad{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle,

such that
f^{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{j}={\mathbf{A}}_{j}
(are equal as subcategories) for
j=n+1,\ldots,m
.

Remark 4.4
.

By Remark
3.23
, to define
G
-equivariant atomic theory on some
X
, it suffices to specify just
one

G
-invariant SOD of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
— all other atomic SOD-s will be its mutations.

We believe that atomic theories for different groups should be compatible in a way that we explain next.

Definition 4.5
.

We say that an SOD
{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle
is a
refinement
of an SOD
{\mathbf{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle
if there exist integers
0=a_{0}<a_{1}<\cdots<a_{n-1}<a_{n}=m

such that

{\mathbf{A}}_{i}=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{a_{i-1}+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{a_{i}}\rangle,\qquad i=1,\ldots,n,

where the equality is meant as subcategories of
{\mathbf{T}}
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
G
be a group and
H\subset G
be a subgroup. Let
{\mathcal{V}}_{G}
be a
G
-atomic domain and
{\mathcal{V}}_{H}
be an
H
-atomic domain over
{\Bbbk}
.
We say that a
G
-atomic theory for
{\mathcal{V}}_{G}
is
subgroup compatible
with an
H
-atomic theory for
{\mathcal{V}}_{H}
if the following holds:

(a)

the restriction functor
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk}}\to H-\cVar_{{\Bbbk}}
sends
{\mathcal{V}}_{G}
to
{\mathcal{V}}_{H}
, and

(b)

for any
X\in{\mathcal{V}}_{G}
, there exists a
G
-atomic decomposition
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle
, and an
H
-atomic decomposition
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle
such that
\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle
is a refinement of
\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle
.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
H
be a group and
{\Bbbk}\subset{\mathbb{F}}
be a field extension. Let
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\Bbbk}}
and
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathbb{F}}}
be
H
-atomic domains over
{\Bbbk}
and
{\mathbb{F}}
respectively, where we suppose that
H
acts on varieties
{\Bbbk}
- (resp.
{\mathbb{F}}
-) linearly.
We say that an
H
-atomic theory for
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\Bbbk}}
is
field extension compatible
with an
H
-atomic theory for
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathbb{F}}}
if the following holds:

(a)

the scalar extension functor sends
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\Bbbk}}
to
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathbb{F}}}
, and

(b)

for any
X\in{\mathcal{V}}_{{\Bbbk}}
, there exist
H
-atomic decompositions
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{F}}})=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle
such that
\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle
refines
\langle q^{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,q^{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle
, where
q\colon X_{{\mathbb{F}}}\to X
is the natural morphism.

Assume that the field
{\Bbbk}
is algebraically closed and an equivariant atomic theory for some
G
-atomic domain
{\mathcal{V}}
over
{\Bbbk}
is given.

Definition 4.8
.

For
X\in{\mathcal{V}}
, the
atoms
of
X
are (
G
-equivalence classes of)
G
-categories
{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}
, where
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\rangle
is an atomic SOD.

By Lemma
3.24
, the atoms are well-defined.

Definition 4.9
.

Let us say that a
G
-atom
{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is
of permutation type

if
{\mathbf{A}}
is generated by a
G
-transitive block
of exceptional objects.

As a category, a permutation type atom
{\mathbf{A}}
is equivalent to a direct sum of copies of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\Bbbk})
hence it has a quite straightforward structure, in particular its Serre functor is trivial so that
\Hom(F,G)\cong\Hom(G,F)^{*}
for all
F,G\in{\mathbf{A}}
. Furthermore, every thick triangulated subcategory
{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{A}}

(not necessarily
G
-invariant)
is also generated by a block of exceptional objects.

By Proposition
3.41
, a
G
-atom of permutation type is characterized by a
G
-action on
{\Bbbk}
, a
G
-action on a finite set
Z
of objects, and a
twisting class

[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,({\Bbbk}^{*})^{Z})
.

Definition 4.10
.

We say that an atom
{\mathbf{A}}
of permutation type is
non-twisted
if the twisting class
[\alpha]
vanishes.

Lemma 4.11
.

[
11
, 6.2, 6.3]

Let
p\colon\widetilde{X}\to X
be the blow-up of a finite
G
-orbit
Z
on a smooth projective
G
-surface
X
, assume
p
is a morphism from
{\mathcal{V}}
. Then the
G
-atoms of
\widetilde{X}
are the
G
-atoms of
X
together with one non-twisted permutation type atom

G
-equivalent to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)
.

Proof.

By the blow-up formula given in Proposition
3.10

we have

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\tilde{X}})=\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(-1)\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{n}}(-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,p^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\right\rangle=\left\langle p^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X),\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle\right\rangle.

The
G
-category
\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(-1)\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{n}}(-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle
is semi-orthogonally indecomposable hence must be a
G
-atom by condition
(A2)
from Definition
4.3
, and the remaining atoms match. Use
G
-equivalences

\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(-1)\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{n}}(-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle\cong\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle\cong{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)
to see that this
G
-atom is of permutation type and non-twisted.
∎

As a warm-up, we construct atomic theory for curves.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
G
be a group,
{\Bbbk}
be algebraically closed and
{\mathcal{V}}_{G}=G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 1}^{DC}
be the class of smooth projective irreducible
G
-varieties over
{\Bbbk}
of dimension
\leq 1
and derived contractions between them. Then a
G
-equivariant atomic theory for
{\mathcal{V}}_{G}
exists and is unique. Explicitly, decompositions

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\Spec{\Bbbk})

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\Spec{\Bbbk})\rangle

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{\perp},\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\rangle\rangle,

for a rational curve
X
,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\rangle,

for an irrational curve
X
.

are atomic.

Proof.

Note that all derived contractions in
{\mathcal{V}}_{G}
are either isomorphisms or of the form

X\to\Spec{\Bbbk}
, where
X
is a rational curve.
Relations (A2) from Definition
4.3
impose that

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\rangle
splits as an atom in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
for
X
a rational curve (and
nothing more). Also note that for a curve
X
the category

•

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{\perp}
for rational
X
,

•

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
for irrational
X

is indecomposable (see
[
66
]
for irrational curves), so further splitting is not possible.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

It is immediate that the
G
-atomic decompositions in Lemma
4.12
do not actually depend on
G
. Hence, if
H\subset G
is a subgroup then
G
- and
H
-atomic theories in dimension
\leq 1
are subgroup compatible.

In Sections
4.2
and
4.3
, we construct a
G
-atomic theory for
G
-surfaces which we believe is
canonical
. Our main result can be rephrased as follows:

Theorem 4.14

(Theorem
4.26
, Proposition
4.27
)
.

Let
{\Bbbk}
be an algebraically closed field.
For any group
G
there exists a
G
-equivariant atomic theory for smooth projective irreducible
G
-varieties over
{\Bbbk}
of dimension
\leq 2
and rational derived contractions.
Moreover, for any subgroup
H\subset G
, the constructed theories are subgroup compatible.

We sketch the construction of the canonical atomic theory in dimension
2
that we perform in the next sections.

(1)

We use the atomic theory in dimension
\leq 1
constructed in Lemma
4.12
.

(2)

We define explicitly atomic SOD-s of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
for any
G
-Mori fibre space
X/B
, where
X
is a surface. We denote these SOD-s by
{\mathcal{B}}(X/B)
.

(3)

Next we define atomic SOD-s for an arbitrary surface
Y
. We choose a birational contraction
f\colon Y\to X
to a
G
-Mori fibre space
X/B
, pull
{\mathcal{B}}(X/B)
back to
Y
and augment the SOD
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)=\langle\ker f_{*},f^{*}{\mathcal{B}}(X/B)\rangle
by splitting
\ker f_{*}
as follows. We choose a decomposition of
f
into blow-ups of
G
-orbits
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
and write
\ker f_{*}=\langle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z_{1}),\ldots,{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z_{n})\rangle
inductively using the blow-up formula. We denote the SOD for
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
defined thus by
{\mathcal{A}}(f\colon Y\to X;{\mathcal{B}}(X/B))
.

(4)

We prove next that the atomic SOD-s
{\mathcal{B}}(X/B)
that we defined for Mori fibre spaces are compatible with each other under isomorphisms and under Sarkisov links.

(5)

Then we deduce that
{\mathcal{A}}(f\colon Y\to X;{\mathcal{B}}(X/B))
is independent (up to mutation) on the choices of
f\colon Y\to X
, its decomposition into blow-ups, and the Mori fibre space structure
X/B
. It follows that the SOD-s
{\mathcal{A}}:={\mathcal{A}}(f\colon Y\to X;{\mathcal{B}}(X/B))
form an atomic theory.

(6)

Finally we check subgroup compatibility.

In Section
4.4
we deduce the following statement
from Theorem
4.14
by Galois descent.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
{\Bbbk}
be a perfect field.
For any group
H
, there exists an
H
-equivariant atomic theory for smooth projective geometrically integral
H
-varieties over
{\Bbbk}
of dimension
\leq 2

with
{\Bbbk}
-linear group action
.
These theories are subgroup compatible and compatible under algebraic field extensions.

Remark 4.16
.

Note that the group action in Theorem
4.15
is supposed to be
{\Bbbk}
-linear while in Theorem
4.14
it is not. So neither theorem is a special case of the other.

4.2.
Atomic theory for surfaces: extending theory from Mori fibre spaces

In this section we assume that
{\Bbbk}
is algebraically closed and fix a group
G
. Recall that by a
G
-variety we mean a smooth projective irreducible variety over
{\Bbbk}
with a
G
-action (which is not required to be
{\Bbbk}
-linear). Moreover, we require that the
G
-action on
N_{1}(X)
has finite orbits. By a
G
-morphism of
G
-varieties we mean a morphism over
{\Bbbk}
commuting with the group action. Further in this section we will always work in
G
-equivariant category, assuming (sometimes implicitly) that varieties are
G
-varieties and morphisms are
G
-morphisms.

We have seen that in dimension
\leq 1
there is unique atomic theory for the class of all derived contractions. However, we believe that in dimension
\leq 2
it is reasonable to restrict to the smaller atomic domain formed only by
rational
derived contractions, see Definition
2.18
and Proposition
2.17
.
That is, we consider only morphisms coming from the MMP: birational contractions and structure morphisms of Mori fibre spaces (and their compositions).
We denote the atomic domain of
G
-varieties of dimension
\leq 2
over
{\Bbbk}
and rational derived contractions by
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}
.

We will denote the category of
G
-surfaces of Kodaira dimension
\geq 0
by

G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},2}^{\kappa\geq 0}
, while
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\kappa<0}
will denote the category of
G
-surfaces birational to a Mori fibre space and
all

G
-varieties of dimension
\leq 1
. We will denote the corresponding atomic domains with morphisms being rational derived contraction by

G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},2}^{\kappa\geq 0,\MMP}
and
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\kappa<0,\MMP}
respectively.
Note that these two subdomains are disjoint in
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}
, i.e. there are no rational derived contractions between them:

G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}=G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},2}^{\kappa\geq 0,\MMP}\bigsqcup G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\kappa<0,\MMP}.

Therefore, atomic theories for these two subdomains can be constructed independently.

First, we observe that, given a
G
-equivariant birational contraction of surfaces
f\colon Y\to X
, there is an SOD given by the blow-up formula:

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)=\langle\ker f_{*},p^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\rangle

and a natural SOD of
\ker f_{*}
into components corresponding to blow-ups of
G
-orbits. Here is the construction.

For the blow-up
f\colon X\to Y
at one
G
-orbit, let
E_{1},\ldots,E_{d}\subset Y
be the exceptional divisors. We introduce the notation (see Proposition
3.9
)

{\mathbf{K}}_{f}:=\ker f_{*}=\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(-1),\ldots,{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{d}}(-1)\rangle,

this is a
G
-invariant subcategory in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
, equivalent to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\Bbbk}^{\times d})
.
Next, if
f=f_{1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{n}
is a composition of blow-ups of
G
-orbits then one has a
G
-invariant SOD (again by Proposition
3.9
)

(4.1)

\ker f_{*}=\langle{\mathbf{K}}_{f_{n}},f_{n}^{*}{\mathbf{K}}_{f_{n-1}},\ldots,(f_{2}\ldots f_{n})^{*}{\mathbf{K}}_{f_{1}}\rangle.

Explicitly, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, let
E_{ij}
(
j=1\ldots d_{i}
) be the exceptional divisors of the blow-up
f_{i}
.
Then (
4.1
) is

(4.2)

\ker f_{*}=\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{n1}}(-1)\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{nd_{n}}}(-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\ldots,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{11}}(-1)\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1d_{1}}}(-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle\right\rangle,

where we identify the sheaves and their pull-backs on
Y
(by abuse of notation).

The following lemma says that this SOD of
\ker f_{*}
does not depend on the chosen factorisation of
f
into blow-ups, up to mutations of (mutually) orthogonal components.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
f\colon Y\to X
be a birational
G
-morphism between smooth
G
-surfaces, and let

f_{1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{n}=f=f^{\prime}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ f^{\prime}_{n}

be two decompositions of
f
into a sequence of blow-ups of
G
-orbits.
Then, the two SOD-s of
\ker f_{*}

\displaystyle\langle{\mathbf{K}}_{f_{n}},f_{n}^{*}{\mathbf{K}}_{f_{n-1}},\ldots,(f_{2}\ldots f_{n})^{*}{\mathbf{K}}_{f_{1}}\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle{{\mathbf{K}}_{f^{\prime}_{n}}},{f^{\prime}_{n}}^{*}{\mathbf{K}}_{f^{\prime}_{n-1}},\ldots,(f^{\prime}_{2}\ldots f^{\prime}_{n})^{*}{\mathbf{K}}_{f^{\prime}_{1}}\rangle

are the same up to mutations of orthogonal components.

Proof.

Any two decompositions
f=f_{1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{n}=f=f^{\prime}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ f^{\prime}_{n}
can be obtained from each other by the following operations: if
f_{i}
,
f_{i+1}
are two blow-ups of two distinct
G
-orbits, one can blow these orbits up in different order:
{\tilde{f}}_{i+1}
,
{\tilde{f}}_{i}
and replace
f_{1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{i}\circ f_{i+1}\circ\ldots\circ f_{n}
with
f_{1}\circ\cdots\circ{\tilde{f}}_{i+1}\circ{\tilde{f}}_{i}\circ\ldots\circ f_{n}
. Then the corresponding kernels are orthogonal:
{\mathbf{K}}_{f_{i}}={\tilde{f}}_{i+1}^{*}{\mathbf{K}}_{{\tilde{f}}_{i}}
is orthogonal to
f_{i}^{*}{\mathbf{K}}_{f_{i+1}}={\mathbf{K}}_{{\tilde{f}}_{i+1}}
, and two corresponding SOD-s of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
are obtained from each other by a mutation of two orthogonal components. Hence the claim follows by induction.
∎

Lemma
4.17
motivates the following notation for SOD-s.

Definition 4.18
.

Let
f\colon Y\to X
be a birational
G
-morphism between
G
-surfaces.
We denote by

{\mathcal{K}}(f\colon Y\to X),\quad\text{or simply by}\quad{\mathcal{K}}(f)

any of the
G
-invariant SOD-s of
\ker f_{*}
from Lemma
4.17
.
Furthermore, if
{\mathcal{B}}
is a
G
-invariant SOD of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
we write (where
{\mathcal{E}}
stands for extension)

{\mathcal{E}}(f\colon Y\to X;{\mathcal{B}}):=\langle{\mathcal{K}}(f),f^{*}{\mathcal{B}}\rangle,

which is a
G
-invariant SOD of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
.

Note that
{\mathcal{E}}(f\colon Y\to X;{\mathcal{B}})
is well-defined up to mutations of orthogonal components in
{\mathcal{K}}(f)
.
Also note that, for birational morphisms
Z\xrightarrow{g}Y\xrightarrow{f}X
and an SOD
{\mathcal{B}}
of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

(4.3)

{\mathcal{E}}(fg\colon Z\to X,{\mathcal{B}})\quad\text{is mutation-equivalent to}\quad{\mathcal{E}}(g\colon Z\to Y;{\mathcal{E}}(f\colon Y\to X;{\mathcal{B}})).

Recall from Lemma
4.12
that for smooth projective varieties of dimension
\leq 1
over an algebraically closed field
{\Bbbk}
there exists unique atomic theory, and the following are atomic decompositions:

(1)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(\Spec{\Bbbk}):=\langle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\Spec{\Bbbk})\rangle
,

(2)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(C):=\langle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(C)\rangle
if
C
is a curve of genus
g(C)\geq 1
, and

(3)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}):=\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-1)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\rangle
.

The next theorem states that, in order to define an atomic theory for surfaces and rational derived contractions, it suffices to define atomic SOD-s only for the Mori fibre spaces and to check compatibility conditions between them. Then the theory extends uniquely to all surfaces by splitting kernels of birational contractions as in Definition
4.18
. Note that this theorem applies only to the surfaces that are birational to a Mori fibre space — construction of an atomic theory for other surfaces is much easier, see Proposition
4.20
.

Theorem 4.19
.

Assume that for every
G
-Mori fibre space
\pi\colon X\to B
a
G
-invariant SOD
{\mathcal{B}}(X/B)
of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is chosen such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

compatibility with atomic decomposition on the base:

{\mathcal{B}}(X/B)
is obtained from
\langle\ker\pi_{*},\pi^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B)\rangle
by splitting the kernel into several components.

(ii)

compatibility with isomorphisms of
G
-Mori fibre spaces:

if
\phi\colon X_{1}\to X_{2}
is an isomorphism between
G
-Mori fibre spaces
X_{1}/B_{1}
and
X_{2}/B_{2}
(see Definition
2.7
), then
\phi^{*}{\mathcal{B}}(X_{2}/B_{2})
is equal to
{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1}/B_{1})
.

(iii)

compatibility with Sarkisov links:

for any
G
-Sarkisov link
\chi\colon X_{1}\dashrightarrow X_{2}
(see Definition
2.8
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

the following SOD-s for
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)

are mutation-equivalent:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(p\colon Z\to X_{1},{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1}/B_{1}))=

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{K}}(p),p^{*}{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1}/B_{1})\rangle,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(q\colon Z\to X_{2},{\mathcal{B}}(X_{2}/B_{2}))=

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{K}}(q),q^{*}{\mathcal{B}}(X_{2}/B_{2})\rangle.

Then, the following hold:

(1)

For any
G
-surface
Y
and any two
G
-equivariant birational contractions
f\colon Y\to X
and
f^{\prime}\colon Y\to X^{\prime}
to
G
-Mori fibre spaces
X/B,X^{\prime}/B^{\prime}

the SOD-s

{\mathcal{E}}(f\colon Y\to X;{\mathcal{B}}(X/B))\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{E}}(f^{\prime}\colon Y\to X^{\prime};{\mathcal{B}}(X^{\prime}/B^{\prime}))

of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
are mutation-equivalent.

(2)

For each
Y\in G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\kappa<0,\MMP}
define
{\mathcal{A}}(Y)
as follows:

•

if
\dim Y\leq 1
set
{\mathcal{A}}(Y):={\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(Y)
,

•

if
\dim Y=2
set
{\mathcal{A}}(Y):={\mathcal{E}}(f\colon Y\to X;{\mathcal{B}}(X/B))
,
where
f\colon Y\to X
is a birational morphism onto a
G
-Mori fibre space
X/B
.

Then
Y\mapsto{\mathcal{A}}(Y)
defines a
G
-atomic theory on
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\kappa<0,\MMP}
.

Proof.

We first show (
1
).
Consider the composition
\phi=f^{\prime}f^{-1}
as a birational map
X\dashrightarrow X^{\prime}
. One of the following holds (see
[
58
, Th. 16.28(1), Rem. 16.73]
for the rational case):

(a)

\phi
is an isomorphism of Mori fibre spaces, or

(b)

\phi
can be decomposed into a sequence of Sarkisov links
dominated by
Y
: that is, there exists a commutative
merry-go-round
diagram

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{k}}
\scriptstyle{f_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{g_{k}}
\scriptstyle{g_{n}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{q_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots}
\textstyle{X_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{k}}
\scriptstyle{q_{k}}
\textstyle{X_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots}
\textstyle{X_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{n}}
\scriptstyle{q_{n}}
\textstyle{X_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{k-1}}
\textstyle{B_{k}}
\textstyle{B_{n-1}}
\textstyle{B_{n},}

where
X_{i-1}\leftarrow Z_{i}\to X_{i}
are Sarkisov links and
f_{0}=f,f_{n}=f^{\prime}
.

In case
(a)
the claim is straightforward:

{\mathcal{E}}(f^{\prime}\colon Y\to X^{\prime};{\mathcal{B}}(X^{\prime}/B^{\prime}))\cong{\mathcal{E}}(f\colon Y\to X;{\mathcal{E}}(\phi\colon X\to X^{\prime};{\mathcal{B}}(X^{\prime}/B^{\prime})))={\mathcal{E}}(f\colon Y\to X;{\mathcal{B}}(X/B)),

where the first mutation equivalence is by (
4.3
) and the second equality is by assumption
(ii)
.

In case
(b)
one has mutation equivalences:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(f_{0}\colon Y\to X_{0},{\mathcal{B}}(X_{0}/B_{0}))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1}\colon Y\to Z_{1},{\mathcal{E}}(p_{1}\colon Z_{1}\to X_{0},{\mathcal{B}}(X_{0}/B_{0})))

by (
4.3
)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1}\colon Y\to Z_{1},{\mathcal{E}}(q_{1}\colon Z_{1}\to X_{1},{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1}/B_{1})))

by assumption
(iii)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1}\colon Y\to X_{1},{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1}/B_{1}))

by (
4.3
)

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{E}}(f_{n}\colon Y\to X_{n},{\mathcal{B}}(X_{n}/B_{n})).

This proves (
1
).

We now prove (
2
), that is, we check condition
(A2)
of Definition
4.3
: for any
G
-varieties
X,Y
in
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\kappa<0,\MMP}
and any
G
-equivariant rational derived contraction
f\colon Y\to X
, there exist atomic decompositions

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n},{\mathbf{A}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{m}\rangle,\quad{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle,

such that
f^{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{j}={\mathbf{A}}_{j}
for
j=n+1,\ldots,m
.
Any such
f
is a composition of several
G
-MMP contractions (by Proposition
2.17
), so it suffices to check condition
(A2)
only when
f
is a
G
-MMP contraction:

If
\dim(Y)\leq 1
, then it holds
by assumption since
{\mathcal{A}}
extends the atomic theory in dimension
\leq 1
.

If
f\colon Y\to X
is a
G
-Mori fibre space, then one can define
{\mathcal{A}}(Y)
as
{\mathcal{B}}(Y/X)
and then
(A2)
holds by assumption
(i)
.

If
f\colon Y\to X
is the blow-up at one
G
-orbit, one can choose a birational contraction
f_{0}\colon X\to X_{0}/B_{0}
to a Mori fibre space and define
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
as
{\mathcal{E}}(f_{0},{\mathcal{B}}(X_{0}/B_{0}))
and
{\mathcal{A}}(Y)
as
{\mathcal{E}}(f_{0}f,{\mathcal{B}}(X_{0}/B_{0}))
. Then
(A2)
follows from (
4.3
):

{\mathcal{A}}(Y)={\mathcal{E}}(f_{0}f,{\mathcal{B}}(X_{0}/B_{0}))\cong{\mathcal{E}}(f,{\mathcal{E}}(f_{0},{\mathcal{B}}(X_{0}/B_{0})))={\mathcal{E}}(f,{\mathcal{A}}(X))=\langle\ker f_{*},f^{*}{\mathcal{A}}(X)\rangle.

∎

We finish this section with a (much easier) counterpart of Theorem
4.19
for surfaces birational to a surface
X
with
K_{X}
nef.
Recall that these are the surfaces of Kodaira dimension
\geq 0
and that we denote the atomic domain formed by
G
-surfaces of Kodaira dimension
\geq 0
and rational derived contractions between them by
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},2}^{\kappa\geq 0,\MMP}
.

Proposition 4.20
.

For any
G
-surface
X
with
K_{X}
nef, we set all
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
to be its unique atom.
For each
Y\in G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},2}^{\kappa\geq 0,\MMP}
define

{\mathcal{A}}(Y):={\mathcal{E}}(f\colon Y\to X;{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X))
,
where
f\colon Y\to X
is a birational
G
-morphism onto a
G
-surface
X
with
K_{X}
nef.
Then
Y\mapsto{\mathcal{A}}(Y)
defines a
G
-atomic theory on
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},2}^{\kappa\geq 0,\MMP}
.

Proof.

Note that if
Y
is birational to a surface with nef canonical divisor, then there exists a unique birational contraction
f\colon Y\to X
to a unique surface
X
with nef canonical class
[
4
, Proposition III.4.6]
.
Hence
{\mathcal{A}}(Y)
is well-defined up to mutations by Lemma
4.17
.

To prove that SOD-s
{\mathcal{A}}(Y)
form an atomic theory, we need to check condition
(A2)
from Definition
4.3
. Note that any morphism
f\colon Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
in
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},2}^{\kappa\geq 0,\MMP}
is a composition of blow ups, therefore we can argue as in the proof of Theorem
4.19
.
∎

4.3.
Atomic theory for surfaces: defining theory for Mori fibre spaces

In this section we verify that the conditions of Theorem
4.19
are satisfied and thus obtain an atomic theory for surfaces and rational derived contractions. That is, for any
G
-Mori fibre space
X/B
of dimension
2
we specify a
G
-invariant semi-orthogonal decomposition of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
(which we call
standard
) so that three conditions of Theorem
4.19
are fulfilled: the chosen SOD-s are compatible with atomic SOD-s of
B
, compatible with isomorphisms of Mori fibre spaces, and compatible with Sarkisov links (up to mutations).

We work with the same assumptions as in the previous section.

Definition 4.21

(standard decompositions for Mori fibre spaces)
.

Let
G
be a group,
X
be a
G
-surface, and
\pi\colon X\to B
be a
G
-Mori fibre space. We consider the following SOD
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B)
of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
.

If
X
is
not
G
-birationally rich
(see Definition
2.11
), we set

{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B):=\langle\ker\pi_{*},\pi^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B)\rangle,

which consists of
2
or
3
pieces depending on
{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B)
, namely

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/\Spec{\Bbbk}):=

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{\perp}{},\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\rangle\rangle\quad\text{if $X$ is a $G$-minimal del Pezzo surface of degree $\leq 4$,}

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/{\mathbb{P}}^{1}):=

\displaystyle\langle\ker\pi_{*},\langle\pi^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(-1)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\rangle\rangle\quad\text{if $X/{\mathbb{P}}^{1}$ is a $G$-Mori conic bundle of degree $\leq 4$,}

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B):=

\displaystyle\langle\ker\pi_{*},\pi^{*}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(B)\rangle\quad\text{if $B$ is a curve of genus $g(B)\geq 1$.}

If
X
is
G
-birationally rich
, we use the notation from Lemma
3.15
.
If
B\cong\Spec{\Bbbk}
, we set
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/\Spec{\Bbbk})={\mathcal{S}}(X)
to be the
3
-block decomposition of
Karpov–Nogin, namely

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}({\mathbb{P}}^{2}/\Spec{\Bbbk}):=

\displaystyle\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-2H)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-H)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}/\Spec{\Bbbk}):=

\displaystyle\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1}-h_{2})\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X_{6}/\Spec{\Bbbk}):=

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X_{5}/\Spec{\Bbbk}):=

\displaystyle\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle.

If
B\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, we set

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}({\mathbf{F}}_{n}/{\mathbb{P}}^{1}):=

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-s-h)\right\rangle,\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-s)\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X_{6}/{\mathbb{P}}^{1}):=

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X_{5}/{\mathbb{P}}^{1}):=

\displaystyle\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

where
h
(respectively
h_{3}
, respectively
h_{5}
) is the
0
-class giving
\pi\colon X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, and
s\in\Pic({\mathbf{F}}_{n})
is the negative section.

Note that all these decompositions are
G
-invariant.
We will call
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B)

standard atomic SOD
of a
G
-Mori fibre space
X/B
.

Remark 4.22
.

While the definition of
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B)
was done case by case for birationally rich surfaces, there is an alternative uniform way to define it in these cases. Unless
X={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
or
{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
,
n\geq 1
, one has:

{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B)=\langle{\mathbf{A}},\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{r})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\pi^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B)\rangle,

where
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}
are the
0
-classes contracted to a point by
\pi\colon X\to B
, and
{\mathbf{A}}
is the orthogonal complement.

Lemma 4.23
.

For any
G
-Mori fibre space
X/B
the SOD
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B)
has the form

(4.4)

\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{m},\pi^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B)\rangle.

Therefore, the SOD-s
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B)
satisfy condition
(i)
from Theorem
4.19
.
Furthermore, we have

\ker\pi_{*}=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle.

Proof.

The first part of the statement follows immediately from Definition
4.21
.
The last claim holds by Lemma
3.8
.
∎

We sometimes denote the SOD of
\ker\pi_{*}
given in Lemma
4.23
by
{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi)
so that
Definition
4.21
takes the form

{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B)=\langle{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi),\pi^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B)\rangle.

Lemma 4.24
.

The semi-orthogonal decompositions
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B)
satisfy condition
(ii)
in Theorem
4.19
: they are compatible with isomorphisms of Mori fibre spaces.

Proof.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

be an isomorphism between two
G
-Mori fibre spaces
X_{1}/B_{1}
and
X_{2}/B_{2}
.
Then
\psi^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B_{2})
coincides with
{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B_{1})
, so it is enough to compare
{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi_{1})
with
{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi_{2})
in each case of Definition
4.21
.
If
X
is not
G
-birationally rich, then
{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi_{i})=\ker\pi_{i}
has one component and the claim follows directly.
If
X
is
G
