-birationally rich, then
{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi_{1})
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi_{2})
both split into two components. One checks that at least one of them is preserved by all isomorphisms
X_{1}\to X_{2}
since it is defined invariantly in terms of certain divisor classes. Hence, the other component is preserved, too.
So the pull-back
\phi^{*}{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X_{2}/B_{2})
equals
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X_{1}/B_{1})
.
∎

Proposition 4.25
.

The semi-orthogonal decompositions
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B)
satisfy condition
(iii)
in Theorem
4.19
. That is, let
\chi\colon X_{1}\dashrightarrow X_{2}
be a
G
-Sarkisov link

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{2}}

Then the following SOD-s of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)
are mutation-equivalent:

\langle{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma_{1}),\sigma_{1}^{*}{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X_{1}/B_{1})\rangle\cong\langle{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma_{2}),\sigma_{2}^{*}{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X_{2}/B_{2})\rangle.

To prove Proposition
4.25
, we go through the list of Sarkisov links and find mutations for each case. While some cases only require mutations as in Section
3.2
, some others are more involved. We have moved the proof to Appendix
A
because it is quite lengthy.

In fact, our proof of Proposition
4.25
shows a slightly stronger statement. For a rational derived contraction
\beta
in dimension
\leq 1
, denote by
{\mathcal{K}}(\beta)
the trivial SOD of
\ker\beta_{*}
. Then the following SOD-s
of
\ker f_{*}
, where
f=\beta_{1}\pi_{1}\sigma_{1}=\beta_{2}\pi_{2}\sigma_{2}\colon Z\to B
, are mutation-equivalent:

\langle{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma_{1}),\sigma_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi_{1}),\sigma_{1}^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{K}}(\beta_{1})\rangle\cong\langle{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma_{2}),\sigma_{2}^{*}{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi_{2}),\sigma_{2}^{*}\pi_{2}^{*}{\mathcal{K}}(\beta_{2})\rangle.

Theorem 4.26
.

Let
{\Bbbk}
be an algebraically closed field and
G
be a group.
For any
G
-Mori fibre space
X/B
over
{\Bbbk}
of dimension
2
, let
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B)
be as in Definition
4.21
.
Consider the following assignment:

for
Y\in G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}

set
{\mathcal{A}}(Y):={\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(Y)
if
\dim Y\leq 1
, and if
\dim Y=2
let
f\colon Y\to X
be any
G
-birational morphism onto a
G
-surface
X
with nef canonical divisor, or onto a
G
-Mori fibre space
X/B
, and set

{\mathcal{A}}(Y):=\begin{cases}{\mathcal{E}}(f\colon Y\to X,{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X))&\text{if $K_{X}$ nef,}\\
{\mathcal{E}}(f\colon Y\to X,{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B))&\text{if $X/B$ is a $G$-Mori fibre space}.\end{cases}

Then the semi-orthogonal decompositions
{\mathcal{A}}(Y)
of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)
do not depend on the choice of
f
(up to mutations) and define an atomic theory for
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}
.

Proof.

Recall that the atomic domain

G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}
is the disjoint union of its subdomains
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},2}^{\kappa\geq 0,\MMP}
(surfaces birational to surfaces with nef canonical class) and
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\kappa<0,\MMP}
(surfaces birational to a Mori fibre space, all curves and points). Therefore it suffices to check the claims for these subdomains.

By Theorem
4.19
, SOD-s
{\mathcal{A}}(Y)
for
Y\in G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\kappa<0}
form an atomic theory on
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\kappa<0,\MMP}
: indeed
the three conditions of Theorem
4.19
are satisfied by the SOD-s
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}
by Lemma
4.23
, Lemma
4.24
, and Proposition
4.25
respectively. Also, by Proposition
4.20
, SOD-s
{\mathcal{A}}(Y)
for
Y\in G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},2}^{\kappa\geq 0}
form an atomic theory on
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},2}^{\kappa\geq 0,\MMP}
.
∎

Proposition 4.27
.

Let
G
be a group and
H\subset G
a subgroup. The
G
-atomic theory
{\mathcal{A}}_{G}
for
G-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}
and the
H
-atomic theory
{\mathcal{A}}_{H}
for
H-\cVar_{{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}
constructed in Theorem
4.26
are subgroup compatible.

Proof.

We have already observed in Remark
4.13
that the
G
- and
H
-atomic theories in dimension
\leq 1
are subgroup compatible. Let
X
be a
G
-surface. We need to show that the
H
-atomic decomposition
{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(X)
is (mutation-equivalent) to a refinement of the
G
-atomic decomposition
{\mathcal{A}}_{G}(X)
.

We first reduce to the case when
X
is
G
-minimal:
Let
f\colon X\to Z
be a birational
G
-morphism onto a
G
-minimal surface
Z
. We write
{\mathcal{K}}_{H}(f)
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{G}(f)
for the SOD of
\ker f_{*}
from Definition
4.18
for the respective action. Note that
{\mathcal{K}}_{H}(f)
is (mutation-equivalent to) a refinement of
{\mathcal{K}}_{G}(f)
.
Indeed,
this holds by Lemma
4.17
, because a blow-up of a finite
G
-orbit is a composition of blow-ups of finite
H
-orbits.
We have
{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(X)=\langle{\mathcal{K}}_{H}(f),{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(Z)\rangle
and similarly for
G
. So if
{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(Z)
is a refinement of
{\mathcal{A}}_{G}(Z)
, then
{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(X)
refines
{\mathcal{A}}_{G}(X)
.

We will now check the condition for a
G
-minimal surface
X
.
If
K_{X}
is nef,
{\mathcal{A}}_{G}(X)={\mathcal{A}}_{H}(X)={\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and there is nothing to verify.
It remains to consider the case when
X
is
G
-minimal with a
G
-Mori fibre space structure
\pi\colon X\to B
.
By running
H
-MMP for
X
over
B
, one can find an
H
-equivariant birational morphism
f\colon X\to Y
over
B
such that
Y/B^{\prime}
is an
H
-Mori fibre space and
B^{\prime}/B
is an isomorphism or a map
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to\Spec{\Bbbk}
. There is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

We will check that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(X)=\langle{\mathcal{K}}_{H}(f),f^{*}{\mathcal{B}}_{H}^{\rm std}(Y/B^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathcal{K}}_{H}(f),f^{*}{{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathrm{std}}_{H}}(\pi^{\prime}),f^{*}(\pi^{\prime})^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B^{\prime})\rangle

is (up to mutations) a refinement of

{\mathcal{A}}_{G}(X)={\mathcal{B}}_{G}^{\rm std}(X/B)=\langle{{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathrm{std}}_{G}}(\pi),\pi^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B)\rangle.

If
X
is not
G
-birationally rich, then
{{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathrm{std}}_{G}}(\pi)=\ker\pi_{*}
consists of one component, and the result follows since
\beta^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B)
is a right sub-SOD in
{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq 1}(B^{\prime})
(by the axioms of atomic theory in dimension
\leq 1
).

Assume now that
X
is
G
-birationally rich.
We consider two cases: either

(1)

B\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and then
B=B^{\prime}
, or

(2)

B=\Spec{\Bbbk}
.

Since
X
is
G
-minimal, case (
1
) happens only if
X
is a Hirzebruch surface
{\mathbf{F}}_{n}
(by Proposition
2.12
and Remark
2.13
). So
f
is an isomorphism over
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and there is nothing to prove.
In case (
2
), we have that
X
is either a del Pezzo surface of degree
\{5,6,9\}
, or
X\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
(again by Proposition
2.12
). We can assume that
Y
is
H
-minimal and so
Y
is also such surface. We have that
{\mathcal{B}}_{G}^{\rm std}(X/B)={\mathcal{S}}(X)
is the
3
-block decomposition from Lemma
3.15
. Note that in almost all cases one has
B^{\prime}=\Spec{\Bbbk}
and so
{\mathcal{B}}_{H}^{\rm std}(Y/B^{\prime})={\mathcal{S}}(Y)
.
The only exception is
Y\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, where it is also possible that
B^{\prime}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and then
{\mathcal{B}}_{H}^{\rm std}(Y/B^{\prime})={\mathcal{B}}_{H}^{\rm std}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}/{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
, which has
4
pieces, refines
{\mathcal{S}}(Y)={\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
, which has
3
pieces.
The result now follows:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(X)=\langle{\mathcal{K}}_{H}(f),\,

\displaystyle f^{*}{\mathcal{B}}_{H}^{\rm std}(Y/B^{\prime})\rangle,

which refines

\displaystyle\langle\ker(f_{*}),\,

\displaystyle f^{*}{\mathcal{B}}_{H}^{\rm std}(Y/B^{\prime})\rangle,

which refines

\displaystyle\langle\ker(f_{*}),\,

\displaystyle f^{*}{\mathcal{S}}(Y)\rangle,

\displaystyle\text{which refines}\quad{\mathcal{S}}(X)={\mathcal{B}}_{G}^{\rm std}(X/B)={\mathcal{A}}_{G}(X)

by Lemma
3.16
up to mutations.
∎

4.4.
Atomic theory for surfaces over non-closed fields

We explain here, using descent techniques from Section
3.5
, that for a perfect field
{\Bbbk}
, building atomic theory for varieties over
{\Bbbk}
is the same as building Galois-equivariant atomic theory for varieties over
\bar{\Bbbk}
. And the latter was constructed in Section
4.3
, so atomic theory for surfaces over any perfect field follows.

Proposition 4.28
.

We assume that
{\mathbb{L}}/{\Bbbk}
is a Galois field extension and
G=\Gal({\mathbb{L}}/{\Bbbk})
.
Let
H
be a group and
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\Bbbk}}
be an
H
-atomic domain over
{\Bbbk}
, where we assume that
H
acts on varieties
{\Bbbk}
-linearly. For any
X\in{\mathcal{V}}_{{\Bbbk}}
, there is an action of
H\times G
on
X_{{\mathbb{L}}}=X\times_{\Bbbk}\Spec{\mathbb{L}}
, coming from commuting
{\Bbbk}
-linear actions of
H
on
X
and of
G
on
\Spec{\mathbb{L}}
. Assume
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
is an
H\times G
-atomic domain of varieties over
{\mathbb{L}}
such that scalar extension
X\mapsto X_{\mathbb{L}},f\mapsto f_{\mathbb{L}}
is a functor from
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\Bbbk}}
to
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbb{L}}
. Then
H\times G
-equivariant atomic theory for
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
yields
H
-equivariant atomic theory for
{\mathcal{V}}_{\Bbbk}
.

Proof.

Let
X
be in
{\mathcal{V}}_{\Bbbk}
. Recall the bijection between SOD-s of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and
G
-invariant SOD-s of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{\mathbb{L}})
from Proposition
3.45
. Note that this bijection sends
H
-invariant SOD-s to
H\times G
-invariant SOD-s.
For any
X
in
{\mathcal{V}}_{\Bbbk}
, let us say that an
H
-invariant SOD
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\Bbbk}})=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{n}\rangle

is atomic if and only if the
H\times G
-invariant SOD
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{{\mathbb{L}}})=\langle p^{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,p^{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{n}\rangle
is an atomic SOD for
X_{\mathbb{L}}
.
We check that such SOD-s form an
H
-equivariant atomic theory on
{\mathcal{V}}_{\Bbbk}
.

For condition
(A1)
in Definition
4.3
, note that the correspondence in Proposition
3.45
respects mutations: if
\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{n}\rangle
is a mutation of
\langle{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}_{n}\rangle
then
\langle p^{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,p^{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{n}\rangle
is a mutation of
\langle p^{*}{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,p^{*}{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}_{n}\rangle
.
For condition
(A2)
in Definition
4.3
,
let
f\colon Y\to X
be a morphism in
{\mathcal{V}}_{\Bbbk}
, then
f_{\mathbb{L}}\colon Y_{\mathbb{L}}\to X_{\mathbb{L}}
is a morphism in
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbb{L}}
. Choose
atomic SOD-s

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y_{\mathbb{L}})=\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n},{\mathbf{A}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{m}\rangle
,
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{\mathbb{L}})=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle
,
such that
(f_{\mathbb{L}})^{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{j}={\mathbf{A}}_{j}
for
j=n+1,\ldots,m
, and consider SOD-s

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)=\langle p_{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,p_{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{n},p_{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{n+1},\ldots,p_{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{m}\rangle
,
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{n+1},\ldots,p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle
. Then for all
j=n+1,\ldots,m
one has

p^{*}(f^{*}p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{j})=(f_{\mathbb{L}})^{*}p^{*}p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{j}=f^{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{j}={\mathbf{A}}_{j}=p^{*}(p_{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{j}),

hence
f^{*}p_{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{j}=p_{*}{\mathbf{A}}_{j}
as required.
∎

Now we are ready for

Proof of Theorem
4.15
.

We apply Proposition
4.28
to
{\mathbb{L}}=\bar{\Bbbk}
,
G=\Gal(\bar{\Bbbk}/{\Bbbk})
.

Take
{\mathcal{V}}_{\Bbbk}
be the atomic domain of
H
-varieties over
{\Bbbk}
of dimension
\leq 2
, where the
H
-action is
{\Bbbk}
-linear, and rational derived contractions, and take
{\mathcal{V}}_{\bar{\Bbbk}}=(H\times G)-\cVar_{\bar{\Bbbk},\leq 2}^{\MMP}
.
For any variety
X
over
{\Bbbk}
, the action of
G
on
N_{1}(X_{\bar{\Bbbk}})
has finite orbits (since any irreducible curve on
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
is defined over a finite field extension of
{\Bbbk}
). Therefore, for any
H
-surface
X
over
{\Bbbk}
, the surface
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
is an
H\times G
-surface (recall that by Definition
2.3
of a
G
-variety, the
G
-action on
N_{1}
must have finite orbits).
Recall that scalar extension preserves rational derived contractions and hence defines a functor
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\Bbbk}}\to{\mathcal{V}}_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
. Theorem
4.14
provides an atomic theory for
{\mathcal{V}}_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
. Now the first claim follows from Proposition
4.28
.

For compatibility, in view of Proposition
4.28
it is enough to check compatibility of equivariant atomic theories on
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
under group restriction, which holds by Theorem
4.14
. If
H^{\prime}\subset H
is a subgroup, use compatibility of
(H\times G)
- and
(H^{\prime}\times G)
-equivariant atomic SOD-s for
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{\bar{\Bbbk}})
. If
{\Bbbk}\subset{\mathbb{F}}
is an algebraic field extension, let
G^{\prime}=\Gal(\bar{\Bbbk}/{\mathbb{F}})\subset G
, then use compatibility of
(H\times G)
- and
(H\times G^{\prime})
-equivariant atomic SOD-s of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{\bar{\Bbbk}})
.
∎

5.
Applications to rationality and birationality

In this section we work with the atomic theory for
G
-surfaces constructed in Theorem
4.14
and obtain applications to the birational geometry of surfaces.
In subsections
5.1
and
5.2
we consider the general case of
G
-surfaces over an algebraically closed field. Then, in subsection
5.3
we consider the
geometric case

when
{\Bbbk}
is algebraically closed
and
G
is a finite group acting faithfully and
{\Bbbk}
-linearly on a surface
X
defined over
{\Bbbk}
,
and in subsection
5.4
we consider the
arithmetic case
,
when
{\Bbbk}
is a perfect field,

X
is surface over
{\Bbbk}
and

G=\Gal(\overline{{\Bbbk}}/{\Bbbk})
acts on
X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
.

5.1.
Atoms of permutation type

Let
{\Bbbk}
be an algebraically closed field and
X
be a
G
-surface over
{\Bbbk}
.

The atomic theory from Theorem
4.14
associates to
X
a finite collection of triangulated
G
-categories, called the
G
-atoms
of
X
.
Recall (Definition
4.9
) that a
G
-atom is of
permutation type
if it is generated by a
G
-transitive block of exceptional objects. To any atom of permutation type a twisting cohomology class
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,({\Bbbk}^{*})^{n})
is associated (see Definition
3.39
), and the atom is called
non-twisted
if this class vanishes (Definition
4.10
). Also, if
H\subset G
is the stabiliser of one of the objects in the block, a twisting cohomology class
[\alpha_{H}]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H,{\Bbbk}^{*})
is defined. By Corollary
3.42
, the atom is non-twisted if and only if
[\alpha_{H}]
vanishes.

The main application of the atomic theory is the following:

Proposition 5.1
.

G
-birational surfaces
X
and
Y
have the same collection of atoms (with multiplicities), after removing all non-twisted permutation type atoms.

Proof.

If
X
and
Y
are
G
-birational then they can be connected by a chain of blow-ups of finite
G
-orbits (taken in different directions). For one blow-up
{\tilde{Z}}\to Z
the
G
-atoms of
{\tilde{Z}}
and
Z
are the same up to one non-twisted atom of permutation type by Lemma
4.11
.
∎

We will characterize surfaces that have only permutation type atoms.
Recall that
X
is
G
-birationally rich
if it is
G
-birational to a
G
-del Pezzo surface of degree
\geq 5

or to a
G
-conic bundle of degree
\geq 5
(Definition
2.11
).
Note that for the trivial group and an algebraically closed field, birationally rich means the same thing as rational.

Proposition 5.2
.

A
G
-surface
X
has only permutation type atoms if and only if
X
is
G
-birationally rich.

Proof.

Note that having only atoms of permutation type is a birational property, because any blow-up (resp. blow-down)
adds (resp. removes) atoms of permutation type by Lemma
4.11
.
Therefore, we can assume that
X
is
G
-minimal.

If
K_{X}
is nef, then there is only one atom
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
and it cannot be generated by a fully orthogonal exceptional collection because its Serre functor
S(-)=-\otimes\omega_{X}[2]
is non-trivial.

If there is a
G
-Mori fibre space structure
\pi\colon X\to B
, we show that
{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi)
, which is the SOD of
\ker\pi_{*}
such that
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X/B)=\langle{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi),\pi^{*}{\mathcal{A}}(B)\rangle
, consists of permutation type atoms if and only if
X
is
G
-birationally rich.

Indeed, if
X
is
G
-birationally rich, then one sees in Definition
4.21
that all atoms are of permutation type.
If
X
is not
G
-birationally rich, then
{\mathcal{K}}^{\rm std}(\pi)=\ker\pi_{*}
is one atom. We claim that
\ker\pi_{*}
contains
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(C)
of some curve
C
, and is therefore not generated by one block (because every thick triangulated subcategory of a category generated by a block is generated by a block). Indeed, if
B
is a curve, then
\ker\pi_{*}
contains a copy of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(B)
by Orlov’s theorem on projective bundles
[
67
]
. If
B=\Spec{\Bbbk}
, then
X
is a del Pezzo surface of degree
\leq 4
, so
\pi
factorises through a contraction
X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
(which is not
G
-equivariant), and therefore
\ker\pi_{*}
contains a copy of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1})
.
∎

Proposition 5.3
.

A
G
-minimal del Pezzo surface of degree
5
has only non-twisted permutation type atoms.

Note that the other types of birationally rich

G
-del Pezzo surfaces
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
can have twisted or non-twisted permutation type atoms.

Proof.

We need to analyse the

G
-atoms of the del Pezzo surface
X
of degree
5
in more detail. Let
H_{1},\ldots,H_{5}
be the
1
-classes and
h_{1},\ldots,h_{5}
be the
0
-classes on
X
respectively, one has
h_{i}+H_{i}=-K_{X}
by Lemma
2.23
.
The group
G
acts transitively (by
G
-minimality of
X
) on these two sets.
Recall that (by Definition
4.21
) the
G
-atoms of
X
are
\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle
(non-twisted),

\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1}),\ldots,{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\rangle
, and
\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
, where
{\mathcal{E}}
is a vector bundle of rank
2
fitting into
the exact sequence (
3.6
). Let
H\subset G
be the stabiliser of
h_{1}
(or of
H_{1}
). We claim that the twisting classes
[\alpha_{{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})}],[\alpha_{{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})}]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H,{\Bbbk}^{*})
are trivial. Indeed,
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})
is the pull-back of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(-1)
under an
H
-equivariant map
X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
given by
|h_{1}|
. Hence
[\alpha_{{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})}^{2}]=1
by Lemma
3.34
(5) and Example
3.35
. Similarly
[\alpha_{{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})}^{3}]=1
. But
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})\cong\omega_{X}
is a
G
-equivariant bundle, hence
[\alpha_{{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})}][\alpha_{{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})}]=1
and both classes must vanish.
Therefore the
G
-atom
\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1}),\ldots,{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\rangle
is non-twisted (see Corollary
3.42
).

It remains to check that the twisting class

[\alpha_{\mathcal{E}}]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,{\Bbbk}^{*})
corresponding to the
G
-atom
\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
is also trivial.
By (
3.6
),
\Lambda^{2}{\mathcal{E}}\cong\omega_{X}
and is
G
-linearisable, we deduce that

[\alpha_{{\mathcal{E}}}]^{2}
is trivial (using
{\mathcal{E}}\cong{\mathcal{E}}^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{X}
and Lemma
3.34
(2,3)).
On the other hand,
\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
as an
H
-atom is non-twisted. Indeed, there is an
H
-equivariant birational contraction
X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
given by
|H_{1}|
, hence
H
-atoms of
X
are non-twisted or are pulled back from
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
. As above,
H
-twisting classes pulled back from
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
have order dividing
3
. Combining this with
[\alpha_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{2}]=1
we get that the restriction of
[\alpha_{{\mathcal{E}}}]
onto
H
is trivial. Therefore by the restriction-corestriction formula one has
[\alpha_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{5}]=1
since
[G:H]=5
. It now follows that
[\alpha_{{\mathcal{E}}}]=1
.
∎

We relate permutation atoms and the standard obstructions to rationality of
G
-surfaces,
formulated in terms of the
G
-action on
\Pic(X)
.
Recall that the Bogomolov–Prokhorov obstruction
(see
[
63
, §2.2]
for the arithmetic case,
[
13
]
for the geometric case, and
[
51
]
for a more general case)
is
the first cohomology group

{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(G,\Pic(X))

and the Amitsur obstruction
(see
[
59
]
for the arithmetic case
and

[
11
, §6]
for the geometric case)
is defined as

(5.1)

\Am(X,G)=\Coker(\Pic^{G}(X)\to\Pic(X)^{G}),

where
\Pic(X)^{G}\subset\Pic(X)
denotes the subset of classes of
G
-invariant line bundles, and
\Pic^{G}(X)
the set of isomorphism classes of
G
-linearised line bundles.
It is easy to see that
\Am(X,G)
and (in the case of finite or profinite groups)
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(G,\Pic(X))
are
G
-birational invariants of
X
.

Recall that a
permutation
G
-module
is a finitely generated free abelian group with a
G
-action that admits an integral basis which is permuted by
G
.

Proposition 5.4
.

(1)

If all
G
-atoms of
X
are of permutation type, then
\Pic(X)
is a stably permutation
G
-module. More precisely,
\Pic(X)\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
with the trivial action on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
is a permutation
G
-module. In particular, we have
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(H,\Pic(X))=0
for all finite subgroups
H\subset G
.

(2)

If all
G
-atoms of
X
are of non-twisted permutation type, then
\Am(X,H)=0
for all
H\subset G
.

Proof.

(
1
)
By Proposition
5.2
,
X
must be
G
-birationally rich, in particular, it is a rational surface. We have an isomorphism,
see e.g.

[
41
, Lemma 4.2]

{\mathrm{K}}_{0}(X)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\Pic(X)\oplus{\mathbb{Z}},\quad[F]\mapsto(\rank(F),c_{1}(F),\chi(F)).

By construction it commutes with the
G
-action, where
G
acts trivially on the two
{\mathbb{Z}}
summands.
Thus it suffices to check that
{\mathrm{K}}_{0}(X)
is a permutation module. We have

{\mathrm{K}}_{0}(X)\cong{\mathrm{K}}_{0}({\mathbf{A}}_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus{\mathrm{K}}_{0}({\mathbf{A}}_{n}),

where
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}
are
G
-atoms of permutation type,
and each
{\mathrm{K}}_{0}({\mathbf{A}}_{i})
is a permutation
G
-module by definition of a permutation type atom.
The final statement follows when
G
is finite as in
[
13
, Corollary 2.5.2]
.

(
2
) Since the atomic theory is compatible with passing to a subgroup,
all
H
-atoms of
X
are of non-twisted permutation type, and
we can assume that
H=G
.
Moreover, we can assume
X
to be
G
-minimal by Proposition
5.1
.
By Proposition
5.2
,
X
is
G
-birationally rich, hence
by Corollary
2.14
we have to check the cases
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{5}
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
.
We will check that in each case
\Pic(X)^{G}
is generated by classes of
G
-linearisable line bundles.

Assume first that
X
is a
G
-del Pezzo surface. We have
\Pic(X)^{G}\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}={\mathbb{Q}}\cdot K_{X}
. Since the canonical bundle always admits a
G
-linearisation, if
K_{X}
is indivisible, which holds in the cases
{\mathcal{D}}_{5}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
, then
\Am(X,G)=0
. In the case

{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
, if atoms are non-twisted, then
{\mathcal{O}}(-1)
is
G
-linearisable, and
\Am(X,G)=0
. Similarly, in the

{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
case, if atoms are non-twisted, the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(-1,-1)
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is
G
-linearisable, and
\Am(X,G)=0
.

Finally let us consider the
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
case, that is
X={\mathbf{F}}_{n}
. Let
h,s\in\Pic(X)
be the standard basis. The assumption that
G
-atoms of
X
are non-twisted implies that both
{\mathcal{O}}(-h)
and
{\mathcal{O}}(-s)
are
G
-linearisable, hence
\Am(X,G)=0
.
∎

We will now give an application for surfaces which are not
G
-birationally rich.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
X
and
X^{\prime}
be
G
-minimal del Pezzo surfaces of degree
4
.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

X
and
X^{\prime}
are
G
-isomorphic;

(b)

X
and
X^{\prime}
are
G
-birational;

(c)

X
and
X^{\prime}
have the same
G
-atoms.

Proof.

Implication
(a)

\implies

(b)
is trivial. Implication
(b)

\implies

(c)
follows from Proposition
5.1
and Definition
4.21
for atoms of a
G
-minimal del Pezzo surface of degree
4
. For the implication
(c)

\implies

(a)
see
[
32
]
.
∎

5.2.
Invariants of birational maps

Let
{\Bbbk}
be an algebraically closed field.
Let
\mathrm{Atoms}_{G}
denote
the set of equivalence classes of
atoms of
G
-surfaces.
For every
G
-surface
X

we can consider

\mathrm{At}(X)\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\mathrm{Atoms}_{G}],

the sum of equivalence classes of
G
-atoms of any atomic semi-orthogonal decomposition of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
.
For any two
G
-birational surfaces
X
and
Y
, the difference

\mathrm{At}(Y)-\mathrm{At}(X)
consists of non-twisted permutation type
G
-atoms, which we can identify with the corresponding
finite transitive
G
-sets. We define

a(X,Y):=\mathrm{At}(Y)-\mathrm{At}(X)\in\mathrm{Burn}(G).

Here
\mathrm{Burn}(G)
is the Burnside group of
G
, which is the free abelian group on
isomorphism classes of
finite transitive
G
-sets.

On the other hand, for every
G
-birational map
\phi
between
G
-surfaces
X\dashrightarrow Y

there is an invariant

[
60
,
61
,
49
]

c^{G}(\phi)=\ExDiv(\phi^{-1})-\ExDiv(\phi)\in\mathrm{Burn}(G)

where
\ExDiv(-)
denotes the finite
G
-set of exceptional divisors of a given birational map.
This is the unique invariant which is additive, i.e.
c^{G}(\psi\circ\phi)=c^{G}(\psi)+c^{G}(\phi)
and such that if
\phi\colon{\tilde{X}}\to X
is a blow-up of a
G
-orbit
Z
, then

c^{G}(\phi)=-[Z].

For varieties of dimension
\geq 3
this invariant, restricted to birational
automorphisms
of a variety, is in general non-zero
[
61
]
.
On the other hand, for surfaces
we get the following, generalizing the same result in the arithmetic case proven in
[
60
]
.

Theorem 5.6
.

For all
G
-birational maps
\phi\colon X\dashrightarrow Y
we have
c^{G}(\phi)=a(X,Y)
. In particular, we have
c^{G}(\mathrm{Bir}^{G}(X))=0
for any
G
-surface
X
.

Proof.

Since both
c^{G}(\phi)
and
a(X,Y)
are additive for compositions, it suffices to check the claim for a blow-up
\phi\colon\widetilde{X}\to X
of a
G
-orbit of points
Z\subset X
. In this case we have

c^{G}(\phi)=-[Z]=a(\widetilde{X},X)

and the result follows for all birational maps by additivity.
The final claim follows since
a(X,X)=0
.
∎

5.3.
Geometric case:
G
-linearisability and stable
G
-linearisability

Assume
{\Bbbk}
is an algebraically closed field and
G
is a finite group acting faithfully and
{\Bbbk}
-linearly on a surface
X
over
{\Bbbk}
. Recall that a permutation type atom of
X
is equivalent to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\Bbbk}^{n}{-}\mathrm{mod})
with a
{\Bbbk}
-linear
G
-action, such atoms are classified by a finite transitive
G
-set and a cohomology class in
{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H,{\Bbbk}^{*})
for the stabiliser
H\subset G
of an element in the set, see Corollary
3.42
. In particular, non-twisted permutations type atoms are classified by finite transitive
G
-sets.

Example 5.7
.

Let
X={\mathbb{P}}^{2}_{{\Bbbk}}
with a
{\Bbbk}
-linear
G
-action given by an injective homomorphism
G\to\PGL_{3}({\Bbbk})
.
Then
the
G
-atomic decomposition of
X
is

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-2)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-1)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\rangle.

By Example
3.35

the twisting class
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(G,{\Bbbk}^{*})
of the permutation atom
\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-1)\rangle
is given as the restriction of the extension class in
{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(\PGL_{3}({\Bbbk}),{\Bbbk}^{*})
defined by the exact sequence

1\to{\Bbbk}^{*}\to\GL_{3}({\Bbbk})\to\PGL_{3}({\Bbbk})\to 1.

Thus
[\alpha]
is trivial if and only if the action of
G
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
factors through
G\to\GL_{3}({\Bbbk})
.
In this case all
G
-atoms of
X
are of non-twisted permutation type (again by Example
3.35
).

We say that a
G
-surface
X
is
projectively
G
-linearisable

if

X
is
G
-birational to
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
with some
G
-action.
Furthermore,
X
is
G
-linearisable
if it is
G
-birational to
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
with an action that factors through
\GL_{3}({\Bbbk})
.
Finally,
X
is
stably
G
-linearisable
if there is
m>0
such that
X\times{\mathbb{P}}^{m}
(with trivial
G
-action on
{\mathbb{P}}^{m}
) is
G
-birational to
{\mathbb{P}}^{m+2}
with an action that factors through
\GL_{m+3}({\Bbbk})
.
These are the same conventions as in
[
37
]
,
[
14
]
,
[
50
]
,
[
51
]
, however in
[
68
]
the term linearisable is used for what we call projectively linearisable.

Proposition 5.8
.

Assume that
X
is a projectively
G
-linearisable surface. Then

(1)

All atoms of
X
are of permutation type.

(2)

X
is
G
-linearisable if and only if the atoms of
X
are non-twisted.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.11

these properties are birational invariant.
Thus we can assume
X={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
,
in which case (
1
) and (
2
) are true by Example
5.7
.
∎

Example 5.9
.

We describe an example of a
G
-minimal del Pezzo surface that has only non-twisted permutation atoms but is not projectively
G
-linearisable.
Let
X
be a del Pezzo surface
of degree
5
over an algebraically closed field
{\Bbbk}
of characteristic zero.
We have
\Aut(X)=S_{5}
and there are
5
subgroups
G\subset S_{5}
, up to conjugation, such that
\rho^{G}(X)=1
. The latter condition is equivalent to
G
acting transitively on the
5
conic bundle structures on
X
.
The possible subgroups are listed in
[
25
, Theorem 6.4]
:

S_{5},\;A_{5},\;F,\;D_{5},\;C_{5}.

Here
F
is the order
20
subgroup of
S_{5}
generated by
(1\,2\,3\,4\,5)
and
(2\,3\,5\,4)
. Let
G
be one of these groups.

By Proposition
5.3
all atoms of
X

are non-twisted of permutation type.
In particular,
X
is projectively
G
-linearisable if and only if it is
G
-linearisable by
Proposition
5.8
.

G
-linearisability of
X
is well-understood, see
[
68
, §4.2]
and the references therein.
It turns out that
X
is not

G
-linearisable with respect to the first three groups
S_{5}
,
A_{5}
,
F
and is

G
-linearisable with respect to the last two groups
D_{5}
and
C_{5}
.

Conjecture 5.10
.

If a
G
-surface
X
has only non-twisted permutation atoms, then
X
is stably
G
-linearisable.

Let us explain the evidence for this conjecture.
First note that by Proposition
5.4
, the standard obstructions to stable
G
-linearisability, namely the Bogomolov–Prokhorov obstruction and the Amitsur obstruction, both vanish.
By the classification of birationally rich surfaces (see Corollary
2.14
), the conjecture needs to be checked in the cases
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{5}
, and
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
, and it indeed holds in many cases.
In the
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
case,
X
is linearisable by Example
5.7
.
See
[
14
, Proposition 12]
for the
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
case.
Some partial results are known for the
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
case
[
37
]
,
[
50
]
.
The result is true by

[
69
, Proposition 4.7]
in the
{\mathcal{D}}_{5}
case.

5.4.
Arithmetic case: rationality and birationality over non-closed fields

In this section, we consider the arithmetic case, that is
we work over a perfect field
{\Bbbk}
with the absolute Galois group
G=\Gal(\overline{{\Bbbk}}/{\Bbbk})
.

Atomic theory for surfaces over
{\Bbbk}
is constructed in §
4.4
using descent, see Theorem
4.15
. That is, we consider
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
with the natural
G
-action and use
G
-atomic theory on
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
. The atoms of
X
are then descended from
G
-atoms of

X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
using Proposition
3.45
.
In particular, a permutation type
G
-atom on
X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
generated by exceptional objects
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
descends to an atom of
X
generated by a single weakly exceptional object
E
such that the scalar extension of
E
is
(E_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus E_{n})^{\oplus r}
for some
r
. The atom is non-twisted if and only if
r=1
, see Proposition
3.49
.

Definition 5.11
.

We say that an atom
{\mathbf{A}}
of a surface
X
over
{\Bbbk}
is
small
if
{\mathbf{A}}
is generated by a single weakly exceptional object, and is
big
otherwise.

Let
{\mathbf{A}}
be a small atom and
E\in{\mathbf{A}}
be its weakly exceptional generator (which is unique up to shift).
Then
D_{\mathbf{A}}:=\End(E)
is a finite-dimensional division
{\Bbbk}
-algebra, and we have an equivalence

(5.2)

{\mathbf{A}}\cong{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\modd D_{\mathbf{A}}).

The centre
L_{\mathbf{A}}
of
D_{\mathbf{A}}
is a finite field extension of
{\Bbbk}
. We denote by
{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{A}}=[D_{\mathbf{A}}]\in\Br(L_{\mathbf{A}})
the Brauer class of the central simple algebra
D_{\mathbf{A}}
. In this section we will distinguish between a Brauer class
{\mathbf{a}}\in\Br(L)
and its second cohomology class
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(\Gal(\bar{L}/L),\bar{L}^{*})
. In particular, we will write the Brauer group additively, so that its trivial element is
0
.

By Proposition
3.47
, small atoms of
X
are exactly the ones descended from
G
-atoms of
X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
of permutation type. By Proposition
3.49
, the twisting cohomology class
[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(H,\bar{\Bbbk}^{*})
of the
corresponding
G
-atom of permutation type matches the Brauer class
{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{A}}
.

Recall that the
index

\ind([A])
of the class
[A]\in\Br(L)
of a central simple
L
-algebra
A
(and the index of
A
) is the degree
of the unique division algebra which is Morita-equivalent to
A
.

The equivalence class of a small atom

{\mathbf{A}}
is determined by the isomorphism class of the division algebra
D_{{\mathbf{A}}}
(using (
5.2
)), hence by the pair
(L_{{\mathbf{A}}},{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{A}})
. We will often denote such an atom as
(L_{{\mathbf{A}}},{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{A}})
or
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{{\mathbf{A}}},{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{A}})
. Further,
we associate two positive integers to
{\mathbf{A}}
:

d_{1}=[L_{\mathbf{A}}:{\Bbbk}]
and
d_{2}=\deg_{L_{\mathbf{A}}}D_{{\mathbf{A}}}=\ind{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{A}}
. We will say that such atom is of type
(d_{1},d_{2})
.

Surfaces with only small atoms have the following characterization.

Proposition 5.12
.

A surface
X
is birationally rich if and only if all its atoms are small.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
5.2

and Proposition
3.47
.
∎

Recall that a surface
X
over
{\Bbbk}
is
toric

if there is a torus
T
defined over
{\Bbbk}
(that is, an algebraic group
T
over
{\Bbbk}
with
T_{\bar{\Bbbk}}\cong(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{2}
) acting on
X
with a dense open orbit
U\subset X
, and the action on
U
is free.
By
[
27
, Theorem 5.1]

this is equivalent to an
a priori
weaker statement that
X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
is toric.

Lemma 5.13
.

A surface
X
is birationally rich if and only if it admits a toric model.
If this is the case, we can choose a toric model of type
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
(a Severi–Brauer surface),
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
, or
C\times C^{\prime}
where
C
and
C^{\prime}

are conics.

Proof.

Let
X
be a minimal birationally rich surface.
By Corollary
2.14
,
the
G
-surface
X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}

can be of type
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{5}
, or
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
.
In the cases
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
, or
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
the surface
X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
is toric, hence
X
is toric by the above remark.
In the
{\mathcal{D}}_{5}
case
X
has a rational point
[
75
]
, so
X
is rational by
[
63
, Theorem 3.15]
, hence
X
has a
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
model. It remains to check that in the
{\mathcal{C}}_{8}
case

X
is birational to a product of two conics.
Let
X\to C
be a twisted form of the Hirzebruch surface
{\mathbf{F}}_{n}
.
If
n
is odd, then
X
is a rational surface
[
63
, Theorem 1.10(i)]
.
If
n>0
is even, then
X
can be nonrational in which case
X\cong{\mathbb{P}}_{C}({\mathcal{O}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}(n))
where
C
is a nonrational conic

[
63
, Theorem 1.10(ii)]
.
In this case, blowing up a closed point of degree two
on the positive section of
X
, we obtain
a Sarkisov link of type II which replaces
n
by
n-2
.
Eventually, we get a twisted form of the conic bundle
{\mathbf{F}}_{0}={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
in which case
X\cong C\times C^{\prime}
, see e.g.
[
74
, Lemma 7.3]
.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

Small atoms are often related to morphisms to Severi–Brauer varieties which can be described in terms of invariant divisor classes
[
59
]
.
Namely, assume that a small atom
{\mathbf{A}}
of a surface
X
over
{\Bbbk}
descends from a permutation-type atom of
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
, which is generated by the
G
-orbit of a line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(D)
. Assume further that
{\mathcal{O}}(D)
is the pull-back of
{\mathcal{O}}(k)
, where
k=\pm 1
, under a morphism
f\colon X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}\xrightarrow{|kD|}{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
. Then the index of the corresponding Brauer class
{\mathbf{a}}\in\Br(L_{\mathbf{A}})
divides
n
. Indeed, let

H\subset G
be the stabiliser of
{\mathcal{O}}(D)
, so that
(\bar{\Bbbk})^{H}\cong L_{\mathbf{A}}
. Then
f
is
H
-equivariant for some Galois
H
-action on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
, and
{\mathbf{a}}
matches the twisting class of the
H
-invariant object
{\mathcal{O}}(k)
by Lemma
3.34
(5). By Example
3.50
, the index of
{\mathbf{a}}
divides
n
.

Example 5.15
.

Let us describe the small atoms of the toric models from Lemma
5.13
explicitly.
Here we will use the following notation: we write
(L_{m},{\mathbf{a}}_{n})
to represent the atom
{\mathbf{A}}
, where the field
L_{m}=L_{{\mathbf{A}}}
has degree
m
over
{\Bbbk}
and the index of the class
{\mathbf{a}}_{n}={\mathbf{a}}_{{\mathbf{A}}}
divides
n
.
We refer to the formulas from Definition
4.21
for the corresponding
G
-atoms of
the surfaces
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
. Note that the degree
[L_{{\mathbf{A}}}:{\Bbbk}]
is the number of objects in the corresponding
G
-block,
see Proposition
3.49
.

(1)

Let
X
be a Severi–Brauer surface corresponding to a central simple algebra
A/{\Bbbk}
with the Brauer class
{\mathbf{a}}_{3}
.
Then the atoms of
X
are
({\Bbbk},0)
,
({\Bbbk},{\mathbf{a}}_{3})
,
({\Bbbk},2{\mathbf{a}}_{3})
(the index of
{\mathbf{a}}_{3}
divides
3
by Example
3.50
), see also Example
3.35
.

(2)

Let
X
be a minimal del Pezzo surface of degree
8
. Then the atoms of
X
are
({\Bbbk},0)
,

(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{2})
,
({\Bbbk},{\mathbf{a}}_{4})
. Here the index of
{\mathbf{a}}_{2}
divides
2
by Remark
5.14
since the corresponding atom descends from the
G
-block
\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1}),{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\rangle
and
|h_{1}|
defines a map
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Likewise, the index of
{\mathbf{a}}_{4}
divides
4
by Remark
5.14
since this atom descends from the
G
-block
\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1}-h_{2})\rangle
and
|h_{1}+h_{2}|
defines a map
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
.
Note that
X
is the Weil restriction:

(5.3)

X\cong{\mathrm{R}}_{L_{2}/{\Bbbk}}C

where
C
is the conic corresponding to
{\mathbf{a}}_{2}\in\Br(L_{2})
, see
[
22
, Proof of Proposition 5.3]
or

[
74
, Lemma 7.3]
.
Therefore, if
{\mathbf{a}}_{2}=0
then
X\cong{\mathrm{R}}_{L_{2}/{\Bbbk}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L_{2}}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{{\Bbbk}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{{\Bbbk}}
and
{\mathbf{a}}_{4}=0
.

(3)

Let
X
be a minimal del Pezzo surface of degree
6
. Then its atoms are

({\Bbbk},0)
,

(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3})
,

(L_{3},{\mathbf{a}}_{2})
, which are descended from
\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle
,
\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1}),{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})\rangle
,
\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1}),{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2}),{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\rangle
.
Hence
X_{L_{2}}
admits a contraction to a Severi–Brauer surface as in Remark
5.14
, and
{\mathbf{a}}_{3}
is the corresponding Brauer class. Similarly,
X_{L_{3}}
admits a conic bundle structure over a conic, whose Brauer class is
{\mathbf{a}}_{2}
. The two atoms

(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3})
,

(L_{3},{\mathbf{a}}_{2})

are not independent, see Example
5.26
.

(4)

Let
X=C\times C^{\prime}
where the Brauer classes of the conics are
{\mathbf{a}}_{2}
and
{\mathbf{a}}_{2}^{\prime}
. Then the atoms of
X
are
({\Bbbk},0)
,

({\Bbbk},{\mathbf{a}}_{2})
,

({\Bbbk},{\mathbf{a}}_{2}^{\prime})
,

({\Bbbk},{\mathbf{a}}_{2}+{\mathbf{a}}_{2}^{\prime})
.

Definition 5.16
.

A small atom
{\mathbf{A}}
of
X
is
trivial
if the corresponding
G
-atom of
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
is non-twisted of permutation type. By Proposition
3.49
, an atom
{\mathbf{A}}
is trivial if and only if
the division algebra
D_{{\mathbf{A}}}
is commutative:
D_{\mathbf{A}}=L_{\mathbf{A}}
, or equivalently if
[D_{\mathbf{A}}]=0\in\Br(L_{{\mathbf{A}}})
.

We have the following immediate corollary of Proposition
5.1
.

Lemma 5.17
.

If
X
and
Y
are birational, then they have the same atoms up to removing trivial atoms.

It turns out that, for birationally rich surfaces, the converse to Lemma
5.17
is also true, see Theorem
5.28
below, but is harder to prove.
Our next goal is to prove the following particular case (Theorem
5.22
): surfaces with trivial atoms are rational.
We first need some preparation.

Recall that the
index

\ind(X)
of a projective variety
X
is defined as the greatest common divisor of degrees of closed points of
X
, or equivalently as the unique positive integer characterized by

\Ima(\deg\colon\CH_{0}(X)\to{\mathbb{Z}})=\ind(X)\cdot{\mathbb{Z}}.

If
X
has a rational point, then
\ind(X)=1
, but the converse is false in general.
We need the following
well-known result, which can be checked case-by-case for the surfaces given in Lemma
5.13
, see e.g.
[
1
]
. We provide a short uniform proof based on the properties of tori.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
X
be a birationally rich surface.
The following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

X
is rational,

(b)

X
is stably rational,

(c)

X({\Bbbk})\neq\emptyset
,

(d)

\mathrm{ind}(X)=1
.

Proof.

It is clear that (a)
\implies
(b)
\implies
(c)
\implies
(d).
Let us prove the converse implications.
We know that a birationally rich surface
X
always
admits a toric model (Lemma
5.13
). Assume that
\ind(X)=1
. We will prove that the open dense orbit
U
, and hence
X
, has a rational point by the following standard argument.
Let
[U]\in{\mathrm{H}}^{1}({\Bbbk},T)
be the class of torsor
U

[
70
, §5.2]
.
Let
L/{\Bbbk}
be a finite extension
such that
X(L)\neq\emptyset

then it is known that

U(L)\neq\emptyset
, i.e.
U_{L}
is a trivial
T_{L}
-torsor

[
77
, Proposition 4]
.
Then

[L:{\Bbbk}]\cdot[U]=\mathrm{Nm}_{L/{\Bbbk}}[U_{L}]=0.

Representing
\ind(X)
as a finite
integral linear combination
of
[L_{i}:{\Bbbk}]
for finite field extensions
L_{i}/{\Bbbk}
(which are residue fields of certain closed points on
X
) we get that
\ind(X)\cdot[U]=0
.
Since we assume that
\ind(X)=1
, we obtain that
[U]=0
so that
U\cong T
has a rational point.
This proves that (d) implies (c).

To finish the proof, it suffices to check that (c) implies (a).
First, note that if
X({\Bbbk})\neq\emptyset
, then
X
has an open dense subset isomorphic to a torus again by

[
77
, Proposition 4]
.
It remains to note that all
2
-dimensional tori are rational by a theorem of
Voskresenskii

[
78
]
.
∎

Recall that
G=\Gal(\overline{{\Bbbk}}/{\Bbbk})
.
The Amitsur group
of a smooth projective variety is defined (see
[
59
]
and
[
22
, §5]
) as

\Am(X):=\Coker(\Pic(X)\to\Pic(X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}})^{G})\subset\Br({\Bbbk}),

cf. (
5.1
) in the geometric case.
Let us
define the
derived Amitsur group
as

(5.4)

\DAm(X):=\Coker\left({\mathrm{K}}_{0}(X)\to{\mathrm{K}}_{0}(X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}})^{G}\right).

Here
{\mathrm{K}}_{0}(-)
is the Grothendieck group. We note that

\DAm(X)
is an invariant of the derived category
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
. The cokernel in (
5.4
)
has been considered in various contexts, in particular for Severi–Brauer varieties
[
42
]
and
toric varieties
[
64
]
.

Proposition 5.19
.

For a smooth projective geometrically rational surface
X
we have a short exact sequence

0\to{\mathbb{Z}}/\ind(X)\to\DAm(X)\to\Am(X)\to 0.

Proof.

We apply the Riemann–Roch theorem without denominators
[
34
, Example 15.1.5]
for
X
and
X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
.
The result follows by applying the snake lemma to the following diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{K}}_{0}(X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}})^{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\rank,c_{1})\hskip 7.97224pt}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\Pic(X_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}})^{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\CH_{0}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\deg}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{K}}_{0}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\rank,c_{1})\hskip 7.97224pt}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\Pic(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Note that
\CH_{0}(X_{\bar{\Bbbk}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
since
X_{\bar{\Bbbk}}
is rational.
Since the right vertical arrow is injective, the sequence of cokernels gives the desired exact sequence. ∎

We also define the derived Amitsur group of a central simple algebra by the same formula
(
5.4
). Similarly, we can define the derived Amitsur group of any triangulated subcategory in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
.

Lemma 5.20
.

If
L
is a finite field extension of
{\Bbbk}
and
A
is a central simple algebra over
L
, then
\DAm({\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(A))\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/n
where
n
is the index of
A
.

Proof.

We can assume that
A
is a division algebra, so that
\dim_{L}(A)=n^{2}
.
Let
d=[L:{\Bbbk}]
so that

A\otimes_{{\Bbbk}}\overline{{\Bbbk}}\cong M_{n}(\overline{{\Bbbk}})^{\times d}.

We have the following diagram of infinite cyclic groups and injective maps between them:

\textstyle{{{\mathrm{K}}_{0}(M_{n}(\overline{{\Bbbk}})^{\times d}})^{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\dim_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}}
\textstyle{{{\mathrm{K}}_{0}(A)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\otimes\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\dim_{{\Bbbk}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}

The image of the top horizontal arrow is
dn{\mathbb{Z}}
, and the image of the bottom horizontal arrow is
dn^{2}{\mathbb{Z}}
, thus the index of the image of the left vertical arrow is
n
.
∎

Recall that by Proposition
5.12
birationally rich surfaces have only small atoms
{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{m}
,
and that each small atom has an index, defined as the index of the corresponding division algebra.
In particular, an atom is trivial if and only if it has index one.

Proposition 5.21
.

For a birationally rich surface
X
with atoms
{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots{\mathbf{A}}_{m}
, one has the formula

(5.5)

\ind(X)\cdot|\Am(X)|=|\DAm(X)|=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\ind({\mathbf{A}}_{i}).

Proof.

The first equality follows from Proposition
5.19
.
The second equality follows from

\DAm(X)=\DAm({\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X))\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\DAm({\mathbf{A}}_{i}),

equivalences (
5.2
) and Lemma
5.20
.
∎

Theorem 5.22
.

A surface
X
is rational if and only if all its atoms are
trivial.

Proof.

If
X
is rational, then its atoms are trivial by Lemma
5.17
because the atoms of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
are trivial (see Example
5.15
).
Conversely, assume that the atoms of
X

are trivial.
By Proposition
5.2
,
X
is birationally rich.
By Proposition
5.21
,
we have
\ind(X)=1
.
Finally, by Lemma
5.18

X
is rational.
∎

Remark 5.23
.

Interesting semi-orthogonal decompositions for higher-dimensional arithmetic toric varieties have been constructed in
[
3
]
and
[
2
]
, and it is quite likely that those decompositions are atomic in an appropriate sense.
However, as shown in
[
2
]
, we should not expect that the ’if’ implication of Theorem
5.22
holds in higher dimension.

Corollary 5.24
.

If
{\Bbbk}
is a finite field (or more generally, any field
{\Bbbk}
such that for all finite extensions
L/{\Bbbk}
we have
\Br(L)=0
), then every birationally rich surface over
{\Bbbk}
is rational.

Proof.

Since
X
is birationally rich, each of its atom
{\mathbf{A}}
has the form
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(D_{\mathbf{A}})
, where
D_{\mathbf{A}}
is a finite-dimensional division
{\Bbbk}
-algebra with centre
L_{\mathbf{A}}
. By the assumption on
{\Bbbk}
, we have that
D_{\mathbf{A}}=L_{\mathbf{A}}
is commutative, i.e. the atom is trivial.
Now the result follows from Theorem
5.22
.
∎

We say that an atom
{\mathbf{A}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is
split
by an algebraic field extension
L/{\Bbbk}
if the atomic refinement of the subcategory
{\mathbf{A}}_{L}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{L})
consists only of trivial atoms (see Definition
4.5
and Theorem
4.15
).

Corollary 5.25
.

For any surface
X
and an algebraic field extension
L/{\Bbbk}
, the base change
X_{L}
is a rational surface if and only if
L
splits all atoms of
X
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
5.22
.
∎

Example 5.26
.

Let
X
be a minimal del Pezzo surface of degree
6
.
Then its atomic decomposition is

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)=\langle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3}),{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{3},{\mathbf{a}}_{2}),{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\Bbbk})\rangle

for some field extensions
L_{i}/{\Bbbk}
of degree
i
and a Brauer classes
{\mathbf{a}}_{j}\in\Br(L_{i})
of index dividing
j
(see Example
5.15
).
By the index formula (
5.5
)
we have

\ind(X)=\ind({\mathbf{a}}_{2})\cdot\ind({\mathbf{a}}_{3})\in\{1,2,3,6\}.

The atoms are not independent: we check below that
L_{2}
splits the atom
(L_{3},{\mathbf{a}}_{2})
, and
L_{3}
splits the atom
(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3})
. For the first, extend the base field to
L_{2}
.
Note that
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3})_{L_{2}}\cong{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3})\times{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3}^{\prime})
and must split into two atoms by Proposition
5.12
.
Then the atomic decomposition of
X_{L_{2}}
is

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{L_{2}})=\langle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3}),{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3}^{\prime}),{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{6},({\mathbf{a}}_{2})_{L_{6}}),{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(L_{2})\rangle,

where
L_{6}=L_{3}\otimes_{{\Bbbk}}L_{2}
is a degree
6
field extension of
{\Bbbk}
and
({\mathbf{a}}_{2})_{L_{6}}
is the image of
{\mathbf{a}}_{2}
under
\Br(L_{3})\to\Br(L_{6})
.
We claim that
({\mathbf{a}}_{2})_{L_{6}}=0
. Otherwise,
X_{L_{2}}
has an atom of type
(3,2)
.
Let
X_{L_{2}}\to Z
be a contraction to a minimal surface,
then
Z
also has this atom. Hence
Z
must be a minimal del Pezzo surface of degree
6
(because other possible minimal surfaces do not have such atoms by Corollary
2.14
and Example
5.15
), therefore
X_{L_{2}}=Z
. But
X_{L_{2}}
has four atoms, a contradiction.
Note that by Example
5.15
(3) there is a contraction from
X_{L_{2}}
to a Severi–Brauer surface, which has
(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3})
and
(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}_{3}=-{\mathbf{a}}_{3})
as atoms.

Similarly one can show that the atom
(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{3})
is split by

L_{3}
, that is,
({\mathbf{a}}_{3})_{L_{6}}=0
.

Proposition 5.27
.

(1)

If
X
is a birationally rich surface which is not rational, then it is birational to
a minimal surface
of
exactly
one of the following types:
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
, or
C\times C^{\prime}
,
where
C
and
C^{\prime}
are conics. Moreover, the type is determined by the non-trivial atoms of
X
.

(2)

Let
X
and
Y
be two minimal surfaces of the same
type (rational or irrational) among
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
, or
C\times C^{\prime}
.
Assume that
X
and
Y
have the same atoms.
Then in cases
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
,
X
and
Y
are isomorphic.
In cases
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
and
C\times C^{\prime}
,
X
and
Y
are birational.

Proof.

(
1
) By Lemma
5.13

X
has a model of type

{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
, or
C\times C^{\prime}
.
Atoms of surfaces of these types are given in Example
5.15
.
Since
X
is not rational, at least one of these atoms is nontrivial by Theorem
5.22
.
Therefore, the type of
X
is uniquely determined by the presence of the following atoms, where we write
([L:{\Bbbk}],\ind({\mathbf{a}}))
for an atom
(L,{\mathbf{a}})
:

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr{\mathcal{D}}_{9}&{\mathcal{D}}_{8}&{\mathcal{D}}_{6}&C\times C^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr(1,3)&(2,2)&(3,2)\text{ or }(2,3)&(1,2)\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Note that
in the case
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
, the atom
(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}}_{2})
is not trivial as otherwise also
({\Bbbk},{\mathbf{a}}_{4})
is trivial by Example
5.15
(2).

(
2
) We consider the cases separately:

•

{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
:
X
is isomorphic to
\SB(A)
or
\SB(A^{\mathrm{op}})
, where
A
is the degree
3
central simple algebra corresponding to
{\mathbf{a}}\in\Br({\Bbbk})
, and these two surfaces are birational by the standard Cremona transformation;

•

{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
:
X
and
Y
are isomorphic by (
5.3
);

•

{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
:

X
and
Y
are isomorphic by a result of Blunk
[
12
]
, as explained in

[
27
, Example 10.2]
,
see also

[
22
, Theorem 4.2]
;

•

C\times C^{\prime}
:
let
C
and
C^{\prime}
correspond to Brauer classes
{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}\in\Br({\Bbbk})
, then the atoms of
C\times C^{\prime}
are

({\Bbbk},0),({\Bbbk},{\mathbf{a}}),({\Bbbk},{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}),({\Bbbk},{\mathbf{a}}+{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}).

If
{\mathbf{a}}+{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}
is the class of some conic
C^{\prime\prime}
then the surfaces
C\times C^{\prime}

C\times C^{\prime\prime}
, and
C^{\prime}\times C^{\prime\prime}
are birational by

[
48
, Lemma 11]
,
[
76
, Theorem 1.5]
.
If
{\mathbf{a}}+{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}
is not the class of a conic then
C
,
C^{\prime}
, and the product
C\times C^{\prime}
are uniquely defined by the atoms.

∎

Theorem 5.28
.

Let
X
and
Y
be birationally rich surfaces. Then
X
and
Y
are birational if and only if they have the same atoms, up to removing trivial atoms.

Proof.

Birational surfaces have the same atoms, up to removing trivial atoms, by Lemma
5.17
.

Conversely, let
X
and
Y
satisfy the condition on the atoms. We assume that not all atoms in
X
and
Y
are trivial, otherwise
X
and
Y
are both rational by Theorem
5.22
.
Applying Proposition
5.27
(1), we can assume that
X
and
Y
are minimal surfaces of one of the types
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
,
C\times C^{\prime}
, and they are of the same type.

In cases
{\mathcal{D}}_{9}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{8}
, and
C\times C^{\prime}
, the trivial atoms are all equivalent to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\Bbbk})
, see Example
5.15
. Thus if the nontrivial atoms of
X
and
Y
are the same, then in fact,
X
and
Y
have the same atoms and the result follows from Proposition
5.27
(2).

It remains to prove the result in the case
{\mathcal{D}}_{6}
. Note that by the index formula (
5.5
),
\ind(X)=\ind(Y)\in\{1,2,3,6\}
.
In the case
\ind(X)=\ind(Y)=1
both
X
and
Y
are rational by Lemma
5.18
. In the case
\ind(X)=\ind(Y)=6
both
X
and
Y
have two non-trivial atoms which are the same, and the third atom is
({\Bbbk},0)
, so Proposition
5.27
(2) applies.
In the remaining cases we construct a Sarkisov link of type II from
Y
to a surface which has the same atoms as
X
.

Let us assume that
\ind(X)=\ind(Y)=2
. Then the atoms of
X
are
({\Bbbk},0)
,
(L_{2},0)
,
(L_{3},{\mathbf{a}})

and atoms of
Y
are

({\Bbbk},0)
,
(L_{2}^{\prime},0)
,
(L_{3},{\mathbf{a}})
.
By Example
5.26
,
L_{2}
splits the atom
(L_{3},{\mathbf{a}})
, hence by Corollary
5.25
,
Y_{L_{2}}
is rational.
In particular, there exist closed points on
Y
with residue field isomorphic to
L_{2}
.
We claim that we can find such a point
y\in Y
which is in general position so that the blow-up
\Bl_{y}(Y)
is again a del Pezzo surface.
We check the general position condition from Remark
2.21
.
Since

{\Bbbk}
is an infinite field by Corollary
5.24
, we can find such a point
y\in Y
away from the union of all the
(-1)
-curves (in fact points of degree
2
are not contained in the union of
(-1)
-curves, see
[
52
, Proposition 5.13(1)]
). Furthermore,
y
can not be contained in a
\overline{{\Bbbk}}
-conic (i.e. a
0
-curve) because such conic would have to be unique. Indeed different conics are either disjoint or intersect in one point, hence this conic would be defined over
{\Bbbk}
, which contradicts to the assumption that
Y
has Picard rank one.
We blow up
y\in Y
to get a del Pezzo surface
Z
of degree
4
and Picard rank two which gives a
(6,4,6)
Sarkisov link of type II from
Y
to another del Pezzo surface
X^{\prime}
of degree
6
(see Remark
2.9
and Proposition
2.10
).
Let us check that
X^{\prime}
and
X
have the same atoms. Indeed, the blow-up adds an atom
(L_{2},0)
and the blow-down removes the atom
(L_{2}^{\prime},0)
. By Proposition
5.27
(2),

X
and
X^{\prime}
are isomorphic.
Therefore
X
and
Y
are birational.

The case
\ind(X)=\ind(Y)=3
is similar. The atoms of
X
are
({\Bbbk},0)
,
(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}})
,
(L_{3},0)

and atoms of
Y
are

({\Bbbk},0)
,
(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}})
,
(L_{3}^{\prime},0)
.
By Example
5.26
,
L_{3}
splits the atom
(L_{2},{\mathbf{a}})
, hence by Corollary
5.25
,
Y_{L_{3}}
is rational.
In particular, there exist closed points on
Y
with residue field isomorphic to
L_{3}
. We can find such a closed point
y\in Y
with residue field
L_{3}
away from the union of all the
(-1)
-curves. This point can not be contained in any conic or
1
-curve defined over
\overline{{\Bbbk}}
, because this curve would be unique, hence defined over
{\Bbbk}
which contradicts to the assumption that
Y
has Picard rank one. Let us assume that
L_{3}\not\cong L_{3}^{\prime}
(otherwise
X
and
Y
are isomorphic by Proposition
5.27
(2)). We show that no two points of the Galois orbit
y_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
can be contained in a
\overline{{\Bbbk}}
-conic which is the final generality condition that we need to check in order to blow-up
y\in Y
and construct a Sarkisov link. If this condition is violated, the three points of
y_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
are three vertices of a triangle formed by a Galois orbit of
\overline{{\Bbbk}}
-conics.
Hence the Galois orbit of points is isomorphic to the Galois orbit of conics, and this means that
L_{3}\cong L_{3}^{\prime}
, because
L_{3}
corresponds to the Galois set
y_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
and
L_{3}^{\prime}
corresponds to the Galois set of the three classes of conics
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
,
h_{3}
on
Y_{\overline{{\Bbbk}}}
by construction of atoms for
Y
(see Definition
4.21
). As we assume
L_{3}\not\cong L_{3}^{\prime}
, the point
y
is in general position, and the corresponding
(6,3,6)
Sarkisov link of type II transforms
Y
to a surface isomorphic to
X
.
∎

Without any assumptions on
X
and
Y
the result of Theorem
5.28
cannot hold because even in the case
{\Bbbk}=\bar{\Bbbk}

there are non-birational
K-nef surfaces, for example K3 or abelian surfaces, which are derived equivalent.

Conjecture 5.29
.

The statement of Theorem
5.28
holds for all geometrically rational surfaces.

The cases to be checked are
G
-minimal
del Pezzo surfaces
{\mathcal{D}}_{d}
and
G
-Mori conic bundles
{\mathcal{C}}_{d}
,
with
d\leq 4
.
When both
X
and
Y

are minimal del Pezzo surfaces of degree
4

with the same non-trivial atoms, they are
isomorphic by Theorem
5.5
.

Conjecture 5.30
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface such that

X\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is rational. Then

X
is rational.

The motivation for this conjecture is as follows.
First of all, over an algebraically closed field the conjecture holds by classification of surfaces, hence we can assume that
X
is geometrically rational.
By Lemma
5.18
, for birationally rich surfaces rationality is the same as stable rationality, so the conjecture holds.
Thus let us consider the case when
X
is not birationally rich, in particular, not rational.
In this case, by Proposition
5.12
,

X
has a big atom
{\mathbf{A}}
. It is easy to see from construction of the atomic theory that for non-birationally rich geometrically rational surfaces this atom is
not equivalent to an admissible subcategory in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(C)
for any curve
C
.
Now let us assume that there is an atomic theory in dimension three which extends our atomic theory in dimension two.
Then existence of the trivial conic bundle
X\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to X

implies that
X\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
also has an atom equivalent to
{\mathbf{A}}
. On the other hand, this atom cannot appear by smooth blow-ups relating
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
to
X
because
{\mathbf{A}}
is not equivalent to an admissible subcategory in a variety of dimension
\leq 1
.

Example 5.31
.

Let
\mathrm{char}({\Bbbk})\neq 2
.
Consider a smooth affine surface
U\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{3}_{{\Bbbk}}
given by

x^{2}-dy^{2}=P(t)

where
d\in{\Bbbk}^{*}\setminus({\Bbbk}^{*})^{2}
and
P\in{\Bbbk}[t]
is an irreducible separable cubic polynomial with Galois group
S_{3}
and discriminant
d
.
It is a particular type of generalized Châtelet surfaces
[
23
, IV]
.
The morphism
U\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1}=\Spec{\Bbbk}[t]
admits a relatively minimal smooth conic bundle compactification
X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
with four singular fibres
[
6
, Proof of Théorème 2]
.
Thus we have
K_{X}^{2}=4
, so

X
is not birationally rich, in particular
X
is irrational.

However,
X
is stably rational.
Namely by

[
6
, Théorème 1]

X\times{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
is rational,
and it was later shown that

X\times{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
is also rational
[
71
, Corollary 12, Proposition 13]
.
It is currently unknown if
X\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is rational;
Conjecture
5.30
predicts that this is not the case.

Appendix A
Proof of Proposition
4.25

In this section, we prove Proposition
4.25
, which states:

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\chi\colon X_{1}\dashrightarrow X_{2}
be a
G
-Sarkisov link

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{2}}

Then the following SOD-s of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)
are mutation-equivalent:

\langle{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma_{1}),\sigma_{1}^{*}{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X_{1}/B_{1})\rangle\cong\langle{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma_{2}),\sigma_{2}^{*}{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X_{2}/B_{2})\rangle.

Recall that

{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma_{i})=\ker\sigma_{i,*}
(as in Definition
4.18
). See Definition
4.21
for
{{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm std}}(X_{i}/B_{i})
.

Proof.

We use notation from Section
2.4
.
In particular, we denote by
E
,
E_{i}
exceptional divisors, by
h
,
h_{i}
the
0
-classes and by
H
,
H_{i}
the
1
-classes.
We denote the mutation of the first component to the end by
-K
and the mutation of the last component to the beginning by
K
. We denote by (
3.13
) the mutation described in Lemma
3.13
.
We go through each case of Sarkisov link from Proposition
2.10
.

Links of type I
:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec{\Bbbk}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where
X
is a
G
-del Pezzo surface of rank one and
Y/{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is a

-Mori conic bundle.

\mathbf{(9,8)}
. We have
X\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{2},Y\cong{\mathbf{F}}_{1}
. Recall that

and
-K=2H+h
in

. Two SOD-s on

coming from

and from
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
are

(A.1)

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(-1),{\mathcal{O}}(-2H),{\mathcal{O}}(-H),{\mathcal{O}}\rangle,

(A.2)

\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h-E),{\mathcal{O}}(-E),{\mathcal{O}}(-h),{\mathcal{O}}\rangle

Applying mutations we get

\text{\eqref{eq_98a}}\overset{\eqref{lemma_OE}}{\leadsto}\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-2H)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-H)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-E)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\rangle\overset{-K}{\leadsto}\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-H)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-E)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-2H-K)\rangle\rangle=\\
=\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h-E)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-E)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(h)\rangle\rangle\overset{L_{4}}{\leadsto}\text{\eqref{eq_98b}}

\mathbf{(9,5)}
. One has

is the blow-up of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
at an orbit of order
4
. There are
4

(

).

\ldots\\

\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{E}}\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
\ldots\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{4})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{5})\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

by Lemma
3.15
, they are mutation-equivalent.

\mathbf{(8,6)}
. One has

is the blow-up of an orbit of order
2
on
{\mathbf{F}}_{0}
. Let

{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

\mathbf{(4,3)}
. Here

We apply mutations:

\text{\eqref{eq_43_1}}\overset{\eqref{lemma_OE}}{\leadsto}\langle{\mathbf{A}},\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-E)\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\rangle\overset{R_{1}}{\leadsto}\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-E)\rangle,{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime},\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\rangle\overset{-K}{\leadsto}\langle{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime},\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(h)\rangle\rangle\overset{L_{3}}{\leadsto}\text{\eqref{eq_43_2}}

\mathbf{(d,1,d)}

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(Z)=\langle\ker p_{1*},p_{1}^{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{1},\ldots,p_{1}^{*}{\mathbf{B}}_{m}\rangle,

(A.12)

. Let

.
Recall that

denotes the mutation of the

Iterating (

) one gets

\displaystyle(L_{2}L_{3}\ldots L_{m})(\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots{\mathbf{A}}_{m},\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\rangle\rangle)

\displaystyle=\langle S_{{\mathbf{A}}}({\mathbf{A}}_{m}),{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{m-1},\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\rangle\rangle,

\displaystyle(L_{2}L_{3}\ldots L_{m})^{2}(\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots{\mathbf{A}}_{m},\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\rangle\rangle)

\displaystyle=\langle S_{{\mathbf{A}}}({\mathbf{A}}_{m-1}),S_{{\mathbf{A}}}({\mathbf{A}}_{m}),{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{m-2},\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\rangle\rangle,

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle(L_{2}L_{3}\ldots L_{m})^{m}(\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots{\mathbf{A}}_{m},\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\rangle\rangle)

\displaystyle=\langle S_{{\mathbf{A}}}({\mathbf{A}}_{1}),\ldots,S_{{\mathbf{A}}}({\mathbf{A}}_{m}),\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\rangle\rangle,

\displaystyle(L_{2}L_{3}\ldots L_{m})^{km}(\langle{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots{\mathbf{A}}_{m},\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\rangle\rangle)

\displaystyle=\langle S_{{\mathbf{A}}}^{k}({\mathbf{A}}_{1}),\ldots,S_{{\mathbf{A}}}^{k}({\mathbf{A}}_{m}),\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\rangle\rangle.

We use Lemma

, saying that

and

.
Therefore,

for

takes (

) to

which is (
A.12
) since
p_{2}=p_{1}\sigma
.

\mathbf{(9,6,9)}
. Here

at orbits of order

. We note that by Lemma

(A.13)

\left\langle\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(-1)\\

pulled back from

is mutation-equivalent to

\left\langle\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})\rangle,\left\langle\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{1})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{2})\\
{\mathcal{O}}(-h_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\right\rangle,

which is the standard
3
-block SOD on a del Pezzo surface of degree
6

where the first block is split into two.
Then the result follows from symmetry after swapping the orthogonal line bundles
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{1})
and
{\mathcal{O}}(-H_{2})
.

\mathbf{(9,3,9)}
. Here
